It's a short, one-way garden stroll (~45–60 min); you view the pavilion from across the water, you don't go inside. In the northwest of the city, best paired with nearby Ryoan-ji rock garden.
    - 💴 ¥500 adults (~₹295); ¥300 children (~₹177). Buy at the gate, cash preferred. Note: Kyoto temple prices are rising in 2026 (its sister temple Ginkaku-ji doubled in April 2026) — carry extra cash in case.
    - 💡 Open 9:00–17:00. Go right at 9:00 AM for the quietest gold reflection and best morning light; it clogs badly by late morning, especially in autumn.
    - 🇮🇳 No advance online booking needed; the ¥500 ticket is doubled as a paper talisman souvenir. Cash-first — Indian cards often aren't accepted at temple windows.
- **Kiyomizu-dera — hillside wooden stage & the Otowa waterfall** — A UNESCO hillside temple in Southern Higashiyama famous for its huge wooden veranda ('stage') jutting over the maple valley, with sweeping views back over Kyoto. Below the main hall, drink from one of three streams at Otowa Waterfall (health/love/success — pick one). Approached via the atmospheric sloped lanes Ninenzaka and Sannenzaka, packed with sweet shops and machiya cafes leading toward Gion. (Jishu Shrine on-site is closed for renovation.)
    - 💴 ¥500 adults (~₹295); ¥200 students (~₹118). Buy at the gate — bring yen cash, cards unreliable at the window.
    - 💡 Opens early at 6:00 AM (6:30 PM close; to 6:30 PM Jul–Aug) — a rare chance to beat crowds; be inside before 8 AM. Special night illuminations in 2026: Mar 27–Apr 5 (spring), Aug 14–16, and Nov 21–30 (autumn), open till 9:30 PM (last entry 9:00 PM).
    - 🇮🇳 The Ninenzaka/Sannenzaka approach is the best spot to combine a kimono-rental photo walk with temple-hopping in one morning.
- **Arashiyama Bamboo Grove (+ Tenryu-ji) — free, but dawn or bust** — A ~400m path through towering green bamboo on Kyoto's western edge. It's a public road: free and technically open 24h, but there is zero lighting and no toilets inside, so it's a daytime sight. The adjacent Tenryu-ji Zen temple garden (separate ticket) and the Okochi-Sanso villa are worth combining, plus the Togetsukyo bridge and monkey park nearby.
    - 💴 Grove: free. Tenryu-ji garden: about ¥500 (~₹295) separate admission (extra for temple buildings). ~30 min from downtown by train.
    - 💡 Arrive before 8:00 AM — the corridor is packed and claustrophobic 11 AM–3 PM. May–June gives the greenest stalks; August is humid with mosquitoes.
    - 🇮🇳 It's 30+ min from the center with limited dining — not a place to base, but an easy half-day. Use toilets at the station or inside Tenryu-ji; the grove has none.
- **Ginkaku-ji (Silver Pavilion) & the Philosopher's Path** — A restrained Zen counterpoint to the flashy Golden Pavilion — famous for its raked-sand 'Sea of Silver Sand' cone, moss garden, and a hillside loop with city views (it was never actually silvered). In Northern Higashiyama, it anchors the north end of the Philosopher's Path, a canal-side cherry-tree walk (lovely and free) linking down toward Nanzen-ji.
    - 💴 Admission raised to ¥1,000 adults / ¥500 students from April 1, 2026 (was ¥500) — so at July 2026 expect ~₹590 adult. Cash at the gate.
    - 💡 Open 8:30–17:00 (Mar–Nov), 9:00–16:30 (Dec–Feb). Go at opening; it's a one-way garden circuit of ~45 min.
    - 🇮🇳 Pair the free Philosopher's Path stroll with the paid temple to stretch value; the path is one of Kyoto's best free experiences.
- **Gion — geisha district, with strict, enforced etiquette** — Kyoto's historic geisha quarter of wooden ochaya teahouses; you may glimpse a geiko or maiko hurrying to an appointment around dusk. Public streets you CAN photograph: Hanami-koji (main street), the willow-lined Shirakawa canal lane, and the Sannenzaka/Ninenzaka slopes toward Kiyomizu.
    - 💴 Free to walk public streets. But entering Gion's PRIVATE alleys or photographing geisha there carries a ¥10,000 fine (~₹5,900); multilingual warning signs are posted. Don't touch, chase, or block them.
    - 💡 Best time to spot geiko/maiko is early evening, roughly 17:00–20:00, as they head to engagements. Keep to public lanes.
    - 🇮🇳 Even on public roads, ask permission before photographing any geisha — a common tourist mistake that draws real penalties.
- **Nara day trip — free-roaming deer + Todai-ji's Great Buddha** — A ~45-min hop from Kyoto to a former capital. Nara Park is free and home to ~1,000 wild-but-tame sika deer that bow for crackers. The star temple is Todai-ji, whose Daibutsuden (one of the world's largest wooden halls) houses a 15m bronze Great Buddha. Add Kasuga Taisha (thousands of stone/bronze lanterns, 15-min walk), Kofuku-ji's five-storey pagoda (10 min), and Nigatsu-do hall for city views. Budget 3–4 hours.
    - 💴 Park & deer: free. Deer crackers (shika senbei) ¥200/bundle (~₹118). Todai-ji Daibutsuden: commonly ¥800 adult (~₹472) — one 2025 guide lists ¥600, so confirm on-site and carry cash. Train Kyoto→Nara: JR rapid ¥720 (~₹425, 45 min) or Kintetsu ¥760 (~₹448).
    - 💡 Bow to a deer and it often bows back before you feed it; then show empty palms so it moves on. Keep paper maps/tickets tucked away (deer eat them) and give antlered bucks space in autumn rut. Go early — crowds agitate the deer; quieter near the museum/Kofuku-ji.
    - 🇮🇳 The Kintetsu line drops you closer to the park than JR; JR is free if you (rarely) hold a JR Pass. Great low-cost, high-wow day for families.
- **Tea ceremony & kimono experiences — book ahead** — The classic cultural add-ons. Camellia (Higashiyama and Flower/Gion locations) is the best-known English-friendly tea ceremony: watch a matcha whisked and drink it in a tatami room. Kimono/yukata rental shops cluster around Higashiyama and Gion — you wear it all day for temple photos and return it by evening.
    - 💴 Camellia group tea ceremony ¥3,300/person (~₹1,950); 45-min private ¥6,600/person (~₹3,894); 1-hour private ¥8,800 (~₹5,192). Kimono rental roughly ¥5,000–6,000/day (~₹2,950–3,540).
    - 💡 Do the kimono rental in the morning so you can photo-walk Sannenzaka/Ninenzaka and Kiyomizu before returning it; slot the tea ceremony as a midday indoor break when temples are most crowded.
    - 🇮🇳 Both are easy English-guided experiences; book online weeks ahead — popular venues sell out months out in spring (sakura) and autumn.
- **Where to base yourself in Kyoto** — Best all-round for first-timers is Downtown Kyoto (Shijo–Kawaramachi, by Nishiki Market): central, on every bus/subway line, ~15 min from Kyoto Station, walkable to Gion and Southern Higashiyama; can be crowded (e.g. Mitsui Garden Hotel Kyoto Shijo, Good Nature Hotel Kyoto). For traditional atmosphere and ryokan, Southern Higashiyama/Gion (e.g. Hotel Celestine Kyoto Gion, century-old Seikoro Ryokan — book ~6 months ahead) but pricier and always busy. Kyoto Station suits budget + transport (Shinkansen, buses; e.g. The Thousand Kyoto, Miyako Hotel Kyoto Hachijo) but few sights at your doorstep. Arashiyama is NOT recommended as a base (30 min out, limited dining, mostly luxury ryokan ~$300–400/night).
    - 💴 Kyoto Station area = generally the most budget-friendly hotels; Higashiyama/Gion the priciest. Downtown is the safest flexible pick.
    - 💡 Whichever area, prioritize being within a 5–10 min walk of a subway/major bus stop; Kyoto's sights are spread out and taxis are costly.
    - 🇮🇳 Base in Downtown or near Kyoto Station so you can day-trip to Nara/Osaka easily and reach Kansai Airport (KIX) by direct express train.
- **Getting there & around — flights, KIX, and the JR Pass trap** — Fly India→Osaka Kansai (KIX), the gateway for Kyoto/Nara. No nonstop flights; expect ~11–12h flying time with one stop (carriers via the route include Air India, ANA, JAL, Cathay Pacific, Korean Air, SriLankan, Vietnam Airlines). From KIX, a JR limited express (Haruka) reaches Kyoto Station in ~75–90 min. Inside Kyoto, note the popular one-day city bus pass was abolished (end-2023) to fight overcrowding — it no longer exists.
    - 💴 Sample fares to Osaka seen at ~₹19,000–25,000 (budget, 1 stop) up to ~₹45,000+ (e.g. Air India Delhi–Osaka) — fares swing a lot, verify live. Nationwide 7-day JR Pass = ¥50,000 (~₹29,500) and rising ~5–6% from Oct 1, 2026. ICOCA IC card = ¥2,000 (~₹1,180) incl. ¥500 refundable deposit.
    - 💡 Get an ICOCA on arrival at KIX and use it for everything local. Buy the JR Pass only if your itinerary includes Tokyo or multiple bullet-train cities.
    - 🇮🇳 For a Kyoto/Nara/Osaka-only trip, SKIP the ¥50,000 JR Pass — it only pays off with long Shinkansen hops (e.g. Tokyo). Just load an ICOCA card (tap trains/buses/shops); it's far cheaper for regional travel.
- **Money & visa for Indian travelers (July 2026)** — Indians need a visa; a single-entry short-term tourist e-visa (up to 90 days) is applied for online via the JAPAN eVISA site (launched for India in 2024). You upload docs, pay, and receive a digital 'Visa issuance notice' shown on your phone at boarding — printouts/screenshots are NOT accepted. Processing is ~5 working days (longer in peak season). Note: several VFS centres (Chennai, Kochi, Hyderabad, Puducherry, Bengaluru) ended walk-ins from March 2, 2026 — book a slot online.
    - 💴 Exchange rate ~₹0.59 per ¥1 as of July 24, 2026 (¥100 ≈ ₹59); rates move, so check before you go. Visa fee for Indians from July 1, 2026: ₹500 (single/multiple) plus a VFS service fee.
    - 💡 Apply for the e-visa ~2–3 weeks before travel; book the VFS slot early in peak season. Keep ¥3,000–5,000 daily in cash for temple entries, deer crackers, and small shops.
    - 🇮🇳 Japan is heavily cash-based at temples, small eateries and shrines — carry yen. Show your e-visa notice on your phone at the airline gate; a printout won't be accepted.
- **Vegetarian / Jain-friendly & Indian food for Indian travelers** — Kyoto is unusually easy for vegetarians thanks to Buddhist shojin-ryori cuisine, tofu (yudofu) specialists, and Kyo-yasai (heirloom Kyoto vegetables). For familiar food, there are dedicated Indian spots: Shama Vegetarian Indian Restaurant (100% veg — no meat, fish, egg, onion, garlic or leek, i.e. Jain-friendly; dishes like eggplant keema curry, soy-meat tikka, samosas, naan), and Kerala, a well-rated Indian restaurant downtown.
    - 💴 Local veg options (tofu sets, temple cuisine) widely available; Indian restaurants price similar to other Kyoto sit-down meals.
    - 💡 Use HappyCow to find nearby veg/vegan spots on the fly; carry a printed 'no fish/meat/dashi' phrase card in Japanese for non-Indian restaurants.
    - 🇮🇳 Shama is the go-to for pure-veg and Jain travelers (no onion/garlic). Elsewhere, learn that 'dashi' fish stock hides in many 'vegetable' dishes — ask, or stick to shojin-ryori (temple vegan) and dedicated veg spots.

_Sources: https://onedayawaytravel.com/kiyomizudera-tickets/ · https://kinkakujitemple.com/entrance-fee/ · https://fushimi-inari.com/entrance-fee/ · https://www.japan-guide.com/e/e3915.html · https://arashiyamabamboogrove.org/hours-and-fees/ · https://www.japan-guide.com/e/e3907.html · https://www.afar.com/magazine/kyotos-gion-district-bans-photographing-geisha-on-private-streets · https://explore-japan.nicoxz.com/spots/nara-park-todaiji · https://www.japan-guide.com/e/e4107.html · https://www.charterbus-limousine.com/travelinfo/japan-unique-experience/02.html · https://www.japanhighlights.com/japan/kyoto/where-to-stay · https://www.klook.com/blog/japan-evisa-for-indian/_


### B6. Osaka & Western Japan (Kansai + Chugoku): must-dos, real costs, and itinerary slotting for an Indian traveler
_A tight 5-6 day loop built around Osaka as your base covers everything you asked for: Osaka Castle + Dotonbori/Namba/Kuromon in the city, a full day at Universal Studios Japan for Super Nintendo World, and westward day-trips to Kobe (beef), Himeji Castle, and Hiroshima (Peace Memorial) + Miyajima (floating torii). The single biggest money-saver for this exact route is the JR Kansai-Hiroshima Area Pass (5-day, ~JPY 17,000 / ~INR 10,000, foreign-tourist only) — one Shin-Osaka<->Hiroshima round trip on the Sanyo Shinkansen (~JPY 11,350 each way) nearly pays for it, and it also covers the JR Miyajima ferry, Kobe and Himeji trains. Conversion basis used throughout: 1 JPY ~= 0.59 INR (July 2026; 100 yen ~= INR 59). Two 2026 price changes matter: Osaka Castle keep rose to JPY 1,200 (Apr 2025) and Himeji Castle jumped to JPY 2,500 for non-resident adults (from March 2026, already in effect — but under-18s now enter free). Indians can skip VFS queues with the online single-entry tourism eVisa. Attraction entry fees are modest; your real budget lines are flights, the rail pass, USJ, and Kobe beef._

- **Osaka Castle (Osaka-jo) — the city's icon, morning slot** — Reconstructed main keep (tenshukaku) set in a huge free public park with moats and cherry trees; 8 floors of history exhibits and a top-floor observation deck over the city. Note the 2025 price change: adult admission to the main tower rose to JPY 1,200 in April 2025 (was JPY 600). Nishinomaru Garden (best lawn view of the keep, superb in sakura season) is a separate JPY 200. Open 09:00-18:00, last entry 17:30; interior closed Dec 28-Jan 1. The surrounding park and the photogenic exterior cost nothing.
    - 💴 Main keep JPY 1,200 (~INR 710) adult; Nishinomaru Garden JPY 200 (~INR 120); park free. Free if you already hold the Osaka Amazing Pass.
    - 💡 Go right at 09:00 to beat tour groups and heat; allow ~90 min. It's a 15-20 min walk from the nearest metro exits, so wear comfortable shoes.
    - 🇮🇳 Fully vegetarian-friendly to walk/photograph; no food restrictions apply to sightseeing. Pair with the Osaka Amazing Pass which bundles the keep entry.
- **Dotonbori & Namba — neon canal + Japan's best street-food crawl** — Ground zero for Osaka's 'kuidaore' (eat till you drop) culture: the giant running Glico Man sign, Ebisu Bridge, and canal-side neon. Must-eats: takoyaki (octopus balls) at Wanaka or Aizuya (considered the birthplace of takoyaki); Osaka-style okonomiyaki (all ingredients mixed into the batter, unlike Hiroshima's layered style) at Mizuno on Dotonbori or casual Fukutaro; 551 Horai butaman (pork buns); grilled ikayaki squid and gyoza. It's free to wander — you only pay for what you eat.
    - 💴 Street snacks roughly JPY 400-800 each (takoyaki ~JPY 500-600 for 6-8 pieces ~= INR 300-350); a sit-down okonomiyaki meal ~JPY 1,000-1,800 (~INR 600-1,060). Budget ~JPY 2,500-3,500 (~INR 1,500-2,065) for a full evening graze.
    - 💡 Come hungry after 18:00 for peak neon; the running-Glico-sign photo is best from Ebisu Bridge. Namba is your ideal hotel base — walkable to Dotonbori and Kuromon, and directly on the Nankai line to/from Kansai Airport.
    - 🇮🇳 Most iconic items (takoyaki, okonomiyaki with pork/dashi, ikayaki) are seafood/pork-based. Pure vegetarians/Jains should ask for veg okonomiyaki, seek out konamon veg options, or use nearby veg/Indian restaurants around Namba; halal takoyaki stalls exist but are rare — confirm before ordering. Carry cash; many stalls are cash-only.
- **Kuromon Ichiba Market — 'Osaka's Kitchen'** — A 580m covered arcade with ~150 stalls of fresh sashimi-on-a-stick, grilled scallops, crab legs, uni, wagyu skewers, tamago-yaki (rolled omelette) and seasonal fruit (premium strawberries, melon). Many stalls grill/prep to order and have small stand-and-eat counters. About a 10-15 min walk northeast of Dotonbori, near Nippombashi station.
    - 💴 Pay-per-item: grilled scallop ~JPY 500, wagyu skewer ~JPY 800-1,500, fruit cups ~JPY 500-1,000. Easy to eat well for JPY 2,000-3,000 (~INR 1,180-1,770).
    - 💡 Go before 11:00 for the freshest catch and calmest crowds; many stalls wind down by mid-afternoon. Combine with Dotonbori on the same Namba half-day.
    - 🇮🇳 Heavily seafood-focused — great for pescatarians, harder for strict vegetarians (stick to fruit, tamago, tempura-veg where available). Indian travelers used to bargaining: prices here are fixed. Cash preferred at many stalls.
- **Universal Studios Japan (USJ) + Super Nintendo World — a full day** — USJ is a full day on its own. Super Nintendo World (life-size Mario world with Mario Kart: Koopa's Challenge and the interactive Power-Up Band wristband game) is the headline; other big draws are the Wizarding World of Harry Potter, Jurassic Park, and Minion Park. Crucially, Super Nintendo World is INSIDE the park (no separate entry fee) but is so popular it usually requires a free Area Timed Entry ticket you grab in the official USJ app once you're inside on the day — OR guaranteed access via an Express Pass.
    - 💴 1-Day Studio Pass (park admission), date-tiered A-F: adult from JPY 8,900 (range ~JPY 8,600-10,900 ~= INR 5,075-6,430), child from JPY 5,700 (~INR 3,365), senior from JPY 8,000. Express Pass is a separate ADD-ON (does not include entry): Express Pass 4 from ~JPY 11,800 (~INR 6,960); packages run ~JPY 12,200-18,900. From Sep 1, 2026 USJ moves to demand-based dynamic pricing that can change after sale, so busy dates cost more.
    - 💡 Arrive before opening; the moment you tap in, open the app and reserve the earliest Super Nintendo World timed-entry slot — free slots vanish fast on busy days. If your date looks crowded, an Express Pass that INCLUDES Nintendo World guaranteed entry is the stress-free (pricey) fix. Reach via JR Yumesaki line to Universal City station (covered by the JR passes).
    - 🇮🇳 Book the Studio Pass online before you fly (dates sell out and prices climb on peak days); an Indian debit/credit card works on the official site or resellers like Klook. The USJ app (for the free timed-entry ticket) is essential — set it up and log in the night before.
- **Kobe beef in Kobe — teppanyaki lunch is the value play** — Kobe (Sannomiya district, ~20-30 min from Osaka) is the place to eat genuine certified Kobe beef teppanyaki, grilled in front of you. Two well-known picks: Steakland Kobe (budget-friendly, near Sannomiya station) and Kobe Beef Steak Ishida Honten (higher-grade A5). Lunch sets are dramatically cheaper than dinner for the same room and chef.
    - 💴 Steakland Kobe lunch sets from JPY 3,500 (~INR 2,065). Ishida Honten A5 Kobe beef lunch: 130g JPY 7,800 (~INR 4,600) or 150g JPY 8,800 (~INR 5,190), including appetizer, salad, vegetables, rice and dessert. Dinner runs far higher.
    - 💡 Do it as a LUNCH on your Himeji day (Kobe/Sannomiya is on the same JR line between Osaka and Himeji) to combine two western-Japan stops in one outing and get the cheaper lunch pricing.
    - 🇮🇳 This is a beef-centric experience — not for those who don't eat beef; Hindu/Jain travelers may prefer to skip or choose the seafood/veg teppan options some of these restaurants offer. Reserve ahead (online) as counter seats are limited; carry a card (these sit-down places take cards).
- **Himeji Castle (Himeji-jo) — Japan's finest original castle** — A UNESCO World Heritage 'White Heron' castle — the largest and best-preserved original wooden castle keep in Japan (not a concrete reconstruction like Osaka's). Steep interior climb to the top floor. About a straight 20-min walk (or short bus) from Himeji station down a grand boulevard with the castle in view the whole way. IMPORTANT 2026 change: since March 2026 the non-resident adult fee jumped to JPY 2,500 (from JPY 1,000) — but under-18s now enter FREE regardless of residency.
    - 💴 Non-resident adult JPY 2,500 (~INR 1,475) as of March 2026 (was JPY 1,000); under-18 free. Combo ticket with the adjacent Koko-en garden is slightly cheaper than buying separately.
    - 💡 Reach Himeji fast on the Sanyo Shinkansen from Shin-Osaka (~30 min) or a JR Special Rapid from Osaka station (~1 hr) — both covered by the Kansai-Hiroshima pass. Pair morning Himeji + Kobe-beef lunch on the way back. Add Koko-en garden next door if you have an hour.
    - 🇮🇳 Families win under the new rule — kids under 18 are free. Exterior and grounds photograph beautifully at no cost if you skip the interior climb.
- **Hiroshima Peace Memorial Park & Museum** — The moving core of any Hiroshima visit: the A-Bomb Dome (Genbaku Dome, UNESCO site) skeletal ruin, the Peace Memorial Park with the Children's Peace Monument and cenotaph, and the Peace Memorial Museum documenting Aug 6, 1945. The park and Dome are free and open-air; only the museum charges. Deeply affecting — allow 2-3 hours.
    - 💴 Museum: adult JPY 200 (~INR 118), high-school/65+ JPY 100, under-15 free; audio guide +JPY 400 (~INR 236). Hours vary by season (roughly 07:30-19:00 Mar-Jul, later in August). Book web tickets in advance — on-site queues can hit ~1 hour when busy.
    - 💡 Reserve the web ticket to skip lines. Do Hiroshima and Miyajima on back-to-back days (or a long single day) since both hang off Hiroshima; the Sanyo Shinkansen from Shin-Osaka is ~1.5 hr.
    - 🇮🇳 No dietary issues here; for lunch, Hiroshima-style okonomiyaki (layered with noodles, cabbage, egg) at Okonomimura is the local specialty — veg versions can be requested.
- **Miyajima & the Itsukushima 'floating' torii** — The vermilion torii gate of Itsukushima Shrine appears to float on the sea at high tide (and you can walk out to its base at low tide) — one of Japan's most iconic views. The shrine itself is built on stilts over the water. Free-roaming deer, Mount Misen (ropeway or hike), and Momijidani maple valley round out the island. Getting there: JR Sanyo line Hiroshima -> Miyajimaguchi (~25 min), then a 10-min ferry (~200 yen one way).
    - 💴 Itsukushima Shrine JPY 300 (~INR 177); combo with Treasure Hall JPY 500. Ferry JPY 200 one-way (JR ferry is FREE with the Kansai-Hiroshima / JR passes). Plus the Miyajima Visitor Tax JPY 100 per visit (~INR 59), added to the ferry fare since Oct 2023. Shrine open ~06:30-18:00 (seasonal).
    - 💡 CHECK THE TIDE TABLE before you go — you want high tide for the classic 'floating' photo (and low tide if you want to walk to the gate). Early morning or late afternoon gives the best light and fewest crowds; watch your food around the tame-but-cheeky deer.
    - 🇮🇳 The JR-operated ferry is covered by your rail pass — use JR (not the Matsudai line) to travel free. Vegetarian-friendly island snacks include grilled/steamed momiji-manju (maple-leaf cakes) with red-bean or custard filling.
- **THE key rail deal: JR Kansai-Hiroshima Area Pass (5-day)** — Tailor-made for this exact itinerary. Foreign-tourist-only pass giving 5 consecutive days of unlimited JR travel across Kansai and along the Sanyo coast to Hiroshima: non-reserved Sanyo Shinkansen (Shin-Osaka<->Hiroshima), Special Rapid trains to Kobe and Himeji, local/rapid JR lines, AND the JR Miyajima ferry. It does NOT cover the Osaka Metro subway or the Tokaido Shinkansen north of Shin-Osaka.
    - 💴 ~JPY 17,000 (~INR 10,030) adult / ~JPY 8,500 child for 5 days (a few resellers list ~JPY 18,000 — confirm at purchase). Value math: one Shin-Osaka<->Hiroshima Shinkansen leg is ~JPY 11,350, so a single round trip almost covers the pass; add Himeji, Kobe and the Miyajima ferry and it's a clear win.
    - 💡 Activate the pass on your first long-distance day (e.g. the Himeji/Kobe day) so all your Himeji + Hiroshima + Miyajima travel falls inside the 5-day window. For pure Osaka city days, use the metro/Amazing Pass instead — the rail pass doesn't cover the subway.
    - 🇮🇳 Buy before you fly (JR-West online or an overseas agent) — you must hold 'temporary visitor' status, so keep your eVisa/passport handy at pickup. The nationwide 7-day JR Pass (JPY 50,000 / ~INR 29,500) is overkill and NOT worth it for a Kansai+Hiroshima-only trip.
- **Osaka city transport & the Osaka Amazing Pass** — For your Osaka-based days (Castle, Dotonbori, Namba, Kuromon), the Osaka Amazing Pass gives unlimited Osaka Metro/bus rides plus free entry to ~40 attractions including Osaka Castle Museum and the Umeda Sky Building Floating Garden observatory. It's now a digital QR ticket only (physical card ended June 2024).
    - 💴 1-day JPY 3,500 (~INR 2,065); 2-day JPY 5,000 (~INR 2,950). Osaka Castle keep (JPY 1,200) and Umeda Sky (JPY 600) alone plus a few metro rides can justify a 1-day pass on a busy sightseeing day.
    - 💡 Only buy the Amazing Pass for a day you'll cram in 2-3 paid attractions + lots of metro hops; on a lighter day a single ICOCA is cheaper. Don't overlap it with a JR-heavy day (the rail pass already covers the JR loop line).
    - 🇮🇳 Buy digitally via Klook/official site with an Indian card; an IC card (ICOCA) is the flexible alternative for pay-as-you-go and works region-wide including convenience stores.
- **Getting there: flights India -> Osaka (KIX)** — Kansai International Airport (KIX) is the gateway. Direct options from India are limited; most itineraries connect via Singapore, Bangkok, Hong Kong, or a Japanese hub. From KIX, reach Osaka city in ~35-50 min via the Nankai Rapi:t (to Namba) or JR Haruka (to Shin-Osaka/Tennoji).
    - 💴 Round-trip fares seen ~INR 30,000-45,000 depending on season/airline; Air India Delhi-Osaka listed from ~INR 45,165, budget/connecting options (IndiGo, VietJet, Cathay, ANA-connections) often lower. Cheapest travel month cited: September (shoulder season).
    - 💡 Fly into and out of KIX (no need for Tokyo on this trip). Land, take Nankai to Namba, and base yourself there for the whole trip — every day-trip in this plan radiates out from Osaka.
    - 🇮🇳 Book 6-10 weeks ahead; connecting via SIN/BKK/HKG is usually cheaper than one-stop premium carriers. Airport train to the city is NOT covered by the Kansai-Hiroshima rail pass leg you'd want to save — but the Haruka to Shin-Osaka is a JR line if your pass is already active.
- **Visa & money for Indian travelers** — Indian passport holders need a visa. Since April 2024 India is eligible for Japan's single-entry short-term TOURISM eVisa: apply fully online (upload photo + documents, pay online), get a 'visa issuance notice' on your phone, no VFS appointment, no biometrics, no passport sticker. The traditional VFS route still exists if you prefer.
    - 💴 Consular fee ~INR 500 (single/multiple entry). Via VFS expect ~INR 2,200-2,900 total (consular ~INR 500 + VFS service ~INR 1,800 + biometric ~INR 500). eVisa online = just the ~INR 500 consular fee, no service charge. Processing ~5-7 working days.
    - 💡 Use the eVisa route to skip VFS queues entirely; show the digital issuance notice on your phone at the Indian departure gate/immigration. Get an ICOCA IC card or a travel eSIM on arrival for smooth transit and maps.
    - 🇮🇳 Prepare passport, day-by-day itinerary, hotel bookings, flight bookings and financial proof (bank statements/ITR). Apply 3-4 weeks before travel to be safe. Currency: 1 JPY ~= 0.59 INR (100 yen ~= INR 59, July 2026) — Japan is heavily cash-friendly at markets/stalls, so carry yen cash plus a card (many places, incl. small stalls, are cash-only).
- **How it all slots together — a 5-6 day plan** — Suggested flow from an Osaka (Namba) base. Day 0: Land KIX, Nankai to Namba, evening Dotonbori neon + street food. Day 1 (city): Osaka Castle at 09:00, then Kuromon Market late morning, afternoon shopping in Shinsaibashi, evening Namba. Day 2: Universal Studios Japan full day (Super Nintendo World). Day 3 (activate rail pass): morning Himeji Castle, Kobe-beef teppanyaki LUNCH in Sannomiya on the way back. Day 4: Sanyo Shinkansen to Hiroshima, Peace Memorial Park & Museum. Day 5: Miyajima (floating torii, tide-timed), return. Day 6: Buffer/shopping, fly out of KIX.
    - 💴 Rough per-person ground costs (excl. flights/hotels): Kansai-Hiroshima Pass ~JPY 17,000; USJ ~JPY 8,900+; entries (Osaka Castle 1,200 + Himeji 2,500 + Peace Museum 200 + Itsukushima 300) ~JPY 4,200; Kobe-beef lunch ~JPY 3,500-8,800; food/metro extras. Ballpark ~JPY 40,000-50,000 (~INR 23,600-29,500) in activities+transport for the 5-6 days.
    - 💡 Sequence Himeji+Kobe together (same line) and Hiroshima+Miyajima together (both off Hiroshima) so your 5-day pass window (Days 3-5 core, buy earliest peak use) captures every expensive Shinkansen leg. Keep Osaka-city days (metro-based) either before you activate the rail pass or use the Amazing Pass for them.
    - 🇮🇳 This is a compact, single-base loop — no hotel-hopping, which keeps costs and hassle low for a first Japan trip. All long legs are covered by one rail pass activated on Day 3.

_Sources: https://osaka-castle.org/hours-and-fees/ · https://osaka-info.jp/en/spot/osaka-castle-main-keep/ · https://www.japan-guide.com/e/e4000.html · https://eatinjapan.jp/articles/osaka-street-food-guide · https://www.magical-trip.com/media/kuromon-market-guide-the-ultimate-guide-to-osakas-kitchen/ · https://matcha-jp.com/en/8236 · https://www.kkday.com/en/blog/57997/usj-express-pass-guide · https://en.traicy.com/posts/2026063037290/ · https://osaka-info.jp/en/spot/universal-studios-japan/ · https://www.trvlogue.com/en/usj-ticket-express-pass-guide · https://onedayawaytravel.com/usj-express-pass-nintendo-world-guide/ · https://www.tripadvisor.com/Restaurant_Review-g298562-d1679701-Reviews-Steakland_Kobe-Kobe_Hyogo_Prefecture_Kinki.html_


### B7. Beyond the Golden Route: Hokkaido, Okinawa, the Japanese Alps, Fuji Five Lakes & hidden gems (for an Indian traveler)
_The classic Golden Route is Tokyo-Hakone/Fuji-Kyoto-Osaka. The best "beyond" add-ons split into three buckets. (1) The Japanese Alps / "New Golden Route" (Matsumoto-Takayama-Shirakawa-go-Kanazawa, plus the Tateyama-Kurobe Alpine Route and Kamikochi) sits BETWEEN Tokyo and Kyoto and is the easiest detour - Kanazawa is just 2h25 from Tokyo by Hokuriku Shinkansen, so it slots into a Golden Route trip with no flying. (2) Kawaguchiko / Fuji Five Lakes is barely off-route at all - ~2 hours from Shinjuku - and gives the postcard Mt Fuji shots (Chureito Pagoda, lake reflections) that Hakone often can't. (3) Hokkaido (Sapporo/Otaru/Niseko/Furano) and Okinawa are far-flung and realistically require a domestic FLIGHT (the Tokyo-Sapporo shinkansen still doesn't reach Sapporo; Okinawa is a 3-hour flight south) - treat them as separate 4-7 day legs, not day trips. Timing is everything: Hokkaido = winter (Dec-Feb, Sapporo Snow Festival 4-11 Feb 2026, Niseko powder) or summer lavender (mid-Jun to mid-Jul); the Alpine Route snow corridor opens 15 Apr 2026; Shirakawa-go's magical winter light-up is only ~4 reservation-only nights in Jan-Feb; Okinawa beaches peak late Mar-May and Oct-Dec; Fuji is clearest Nov-Apr. Practical India notes: e-Visa (base ₹500 + VFS ~₹800), a nationwide 7-day JR Pass is ¥50,000 (~₹29,500) and only worth it for heavy multi-region rail, and vegetarian/Jain and halal food is very findable in big cities._

- **Sapporo & the Snow Festival - Hokkaido's winter capital (FLY, don't train)** — Sapporo is Hokkaido's gateway city and the anchor of any winter trip. The headline event is the Sapporo Snow Festival (Yuki Matsuri), 4-11 February 2026, with giant carved snow/ice sculptures across three sites - Odori Park (the main strip), Susukino (ice sculptures in the nightlife district), and Tsudome (family/snow-play). Reachability is the key planning fact: there is NO direct shinkansen to Sapporo yet (the line still terminates at Shin-Hakodate-Hokuto; going by rail is ~7.5 hours total). Everyone flies: Tokyo (Haneda/Narita) to New Chitose Airport is ~1.5 hrs in the air, ~2-3 hrs door-to-door, and it's one of the busiest air routes in the world so fares are cheap and frequent.
    - 💴 Domestic Tokyo-Sapporo flights are frequent and often cheaper than rail. A JR Hokkaido rail pass (5-day) rose to ¥23,000 (~₹13,570) for getting around within Hokkaido.
    - 💡 Best window is December-February for snow; the festival's second week (early Feb) is peak crowd. Traveling after mid-February often means cheaper fares and calmer crowds while snow is still good.
    - 🇮🇳 Book Hokkaido as a self-contained 4-6 day leg reached by a domestic flight from Tokyo/Osaka - do not try to shinkansen there. Accommodation in Sapporo/Niseko/Otaru sells out months ahead for the Feb festival, so lock rooms early.
- **Otaru - the canal town of snow lanterns** — Otaru is a small, romantic port town ~30-40 min by JR from Sapporo, famous for its stone-warehouse canal, glassware and music-box shops. In winter it hosts the Otaru Snow Light Path Festival (Yuki Akari no Michi), when the historic canal and side streets are lined with snow lanterns and gas lamps at dusk - one of the most photogenic winter scenes in Japan. As a Sea-of-Japan coast town it gets heavy, reliable snowfall.
    - 💴 Easy cheap day trip from Sapporo by JR local train; festival entry is free (it's a public street illumination).
    - 💡 The Snow Light Path festival typically runs for about 10 days in early-mid February, overlapping the Sapporo Snow Festival, so you can do both on one trip. Go at dusk for the lantern glow.
    - 🇮🇳 Pairs perfectly with a Sapporo base - no extra flight or long transfer needed.
- **Niseko - Japan's world-famous powder skiing** — Niseko, inland from Otaru/Sapporo, is Japan's most famous ski destination, prized worldwide for its light, dry 'Japow' (Japan powder) snow and long season. It suits all levels and has the most international/English-friendly ski infrastructure in the country. It's the go-to if skiing/snowboarding is the point of the trip rather than city sightseeing.
    - 💴 Premium resort pricing and it books out far ahead; expect the highest lodging costs in Hokkaido during Jan-Feb peak.
    - 💡 January to early February is peak powder. Book accommodation months in advance - it's one of the first things in Hokkaido to sell out.
    - 🇮🇳 Reachable by bus/transfer from New Chitose Airport (~2.5-3 hrs), so again fly into Hokkaido first. Good English signage/lessons make it beginner-friendly for first-time-snow Indian families.
- **Furano & Biei - summer lavender fields (a totally different Hokkaido)** — Furano and neighbouring Biei, in central Hokkaido, are the summer face of the island: rolling lavender and flower fields with the Tokachi mountains behind. Farm Tomita is the iconic spot - purple lavender rows, flower fields, and lavender ice cream - and it draws around a million visitors a year. This is the reason to consider Hokkaido in SUMMER, not just winter: while mainland Japan is hot and humid, Hokkaido is cool and blooming.
    - 💴 Farm Tomita is FREE to enter. Reachable by JR to Furano/Biei plus local buses, or a rental car (easiest for the scattered fields).
    - 💡 Lavender peaks mid-June to mid-July (broader flower season late June-August). Come early July for peak purple; go early morning to beat tour-bus crowds.
    - 🇮🇳 A cool-climate summer escape - a strong pick for Indians wanting to avoid Japan's sticky Jun-Aug heat and humidity elsewhere.
- **Okinawa main island & the Kerama Islands - Japan's tropical beaches** — Okinawa is Japan's tropical south - white sand, coral reefs and turquoise water more like Southeast Asia than the rest of Japan. Base on the main island around Naha (Senaga Island near the airport, Umikaji Terrace shops) and take boats to the Kerama Islands (Aka-jima, Nagannu-jima) for genuinely pristine 'Kerama Blue' water, snorkelling and diving; Kouri Island's Kouri Beach is an easy main-island favourite. Public transport is weak, so most visitors rent a car at Naha Airport.
    - 💴 Naha is ~3 hrs by flight from Tokyo, ~2 hrs from Osaka. Rent a car online in advance (cars sell out on holidays).
    - 💡 Best beach weather is late March-early May and late September-December (Oct-Nov ideal). AVOID the rainy season (early May-June) and typhoon season (Jul-Sep). Jan-March is whale-watching season.
    - 🇮🇳 Realistically an add-on only if you have 5+ extra days - it needs a dedicated domestic flight, not a rail detour. Great warm-water beach leg for honeymooners flying via Tokyo/Osaka.
- **Miyako & Ishigaki - Okinawa's far southern islands (the best beaches)** — For the very best beaches, go to the outer Yaeyama/Miyako islands. Miyako-jima (~300 km southwest of Naha) has Yonaha Maehama Beach - a ~7 km ribbon of white sand often rated Japan's best. Ishigaki, gateway to the Yaeyama group, sits amid world-class coral reefs (Yonehara Beach for the clearest water; Sukuji for shallow family swimming) and is the springboard to Taketomi and Hateruma (Nishihama Beach, sea turtles).
    - 💴 There are direct flights from Tokyo (Narita/Haneda) to Ishigaki and Miyako; from Naha it's ~50 min to Miyako and ~1 hr to Ishigaki.
    - 💡 Same seasonality as the rest of Okinawa (spring and Oct-Nov best; dodge May-June rains and summer typhoons). Rent a car on each island.
    - 🇮🇳 Plan 7-10 days if you want to combine main island + Miyako/Ishigaki. This is a serious beach holiday, not a quick stop - budget the extra internal flights.
- **Takayama - the Alps' postcard old town (the New Golden Route hub)** — Takayama, in the mountainous Hida region, has a beautifully preserved Edo-era merchant old town (Sanmachi Suji), morning markets, sake breweries and Hida-beef street food. It's the natural base for the Alps loop and pairs with Shirakawa-go. Crucially it sits between Tokyo and Kyoto, so it fits a Golden Route trip without flying: Tokyo-Takayama is ~4h20 by train (change at Nagoya), and it's year-round friendly.
    - 💴 Reached on JR limited express 'Hida' via Nagoya. The Takayama-Hokuriku Area Tourist Pass and Alpine-Takayama-Matsumoto pass bundle this region for foreign tourists.
    - 💡 Good in every season. If you can, time it for the Takayama Festival (spring/autumn) - one of Japan's most beautiful float festivals - but it's very crowded then.
    - 🇮🇳 Vegetarian note: Takayama is Hida-beef country, but the old town has plenty of non-meat snacks (mitarashi dango, sarubobo sweets) and set-menu restaurants; still worth pre-checking HappyCow.
- **Shirakawa-go - the gassho-zukuri villages (winter light-up is the magic)** — Shirakawa-go (Ogimachi) is the UNESCO village of steep-thatched gassho-zukuri farmhouses, sitting between Takayama and Kanazawa - so you can do it as a day trip from either, or slot it in transit. It's stunning year-round, but the bucket-list version is the WINTER LIGHT-UP, when snow-blanketed farmhouses are illuminated at dusk. This is strictly limited and reservation-only.
    - 💴 Nohi bus from Takayama: ¥2,600 (~₹1,530) one-way, ~50-65 min; Takayama-through-to-Kanazawa via Shirakawa-go ¥4,000 (~₹2,360). From Kanazawa it's ~1h15. Some buses require seat reservations.
    - 💡 2026 winter light-up nights are only Jan 12, 18, 25 and Feb 1 (17:30-19:30). Otherwise, a fresh-snow morning in Jan-Feb still gives the classic snow-village look without the light-up crowds.
    - 🇮🇳 For the winter light-up you MUST pre-book (ryokan stay, bus tour, or parking permit) - you can't just turn up. Book weeks/months ahead.
- **Kanazawa - samurai & geisha districts with almost no crowds** — Kanazawa is a WWII-spared castle city often called Japan's best-kept secret - it has Kenroku-en (one of Japan's three great gardens), an intact samurai district (Nagamachi), preserved geisha teahouse quarters (Higashi Chaya, said to rival Kyoto's Gion but far quieter), Kanazawa Castle, the 21st Century Museum, and it produces ~99% of Japan's gold leaf. It's the western anchor of the New Golden Route and the easiest 'Alps-adjacent' city to reach.
    - 💴 Tokyo-Kanazawa is just ~2h25 on the Hokuriku Shinkansen (via Nagano/Toyama) - genuinely a low-effort detour.
    - 💡 Great in any season. Try gold-leaf-topped soft-serve ice cream as the local novelty; Omicho Market for food.
    - 🇮🇳 Because it's a fast direct shinkansen from Tokyo AND now connects toward Kyoto/Osaka (via Tsuruga), Kanazawa is the single best 'beyond the Golden Route' add for travellers who don't want to fly domestically.
- **Kamikochi - the Northern Alps' pristine valley (seasonal, no private cars)** — Kamikochi is a protected high-mountain river valley - crystal Azusa River, the iconic Kappa-bashi bridge, and the 3,000 m peaks of the Hotaka range - and Japan's premier easy-access alpine hiking/scenery spot. It's car-free (you arrive by bus) and, importantly, seasonal: it's only open roughly mid-April to mid-November each year.
    - 💴 Accessed via Matsumoto/Nagano side by train + shuttle bus (private cars banned; park-and-ride).
    - 💡 For hiking, late June-September. The most popular time is AUTUMN (mid-late October) for foliage. It is CLOSED in winter - don't plan a Dec-Mar visit.
    - 🇮🇳 No serious hiking required to enjoy it - the flat riverside walks give the big-mountain views, good for non-trekkers. Bring layers; it's cold at altitude even in summer.
- **Tateyama-Kurobe Alpine Route - the giant snow-wall corridor** — The Tateyama-Kurobe Alpine Route is a spectacular transport traverse across the Northern Alps by cable car, ropeway, trolley bus and highway bus (no hiking needed), linking Toyama and the Nagano side (Shinano-Omachi). Its signature is the Yuki-no-Otani snow corridor near Murodo - a road cut through snow walls roughly 15-20 m high (record 20 m). It also passes the Kurobe Dam.
    - 💴 Full traverse Nagano-Toyama is ¥16,600 (~₹9,800) adult / ¥8,340 child. The Alpine-Takayama-Matsumoto Area Tourist Pass (for foreign passports) is ¥24,500 (~₹14,500) and bundles this with the Takayama area.
    - 💡 2026 season: opens 15 April, closes 30 November. The snow-wall festival runs 15 April-25 June 2026 (walls best in late Apr-May). For autumn colours come late Sept-October. The whole route is CLOSED in deep winter.
    - 🇮🇳 You reach the Toyama end via Hokuriku Shinkansen (Tokyo-Toyama ~2h25), so it chains neatly with Kanazawa/Takayama. Great for families who want big snow without skiing.
- **Kawaguchiko / Fuji Five Lakes - the classic Mt Fuji shot (barely off-route)** — For the picture-perfect Mt Fuji (better than many Hakone angles), go to Lake Kawaguchi, the easiest of the Fuji Five Lakes. Highlights: the Chureito Pagoda (Arakurayama Sengen Park) - the famous pagoda-with-Fuji shot - the mirror-reflection of Fuji on a still lake, and the Kachi-Kachi (Mt Tenjo) ropeway viewpoint. It's essentially a light detour from Tokyo, not a far region.
    - 💴 From Shinjuku: highway bus ~2 hrs, ¥2,200 (~₹1,300), ~¥2,000 if booked online; direct limited-express train ~2 hrs, ¥4,200 (~₹2,480, seat reservation required); or JR Chuo to Otsuki + Fujikyu line (~2-3 hrs).
    - 💡 Fuji is shyest in summer haze - clearest views are November-April (crisp winter mornings are best). For flowers, the Fuji Shibazakura (pink moss) festival is mid-April-May; autumn colours late November. Always aim for early morning before cloud builds.
    - 🇮🇳 Cheapest/most reliable is the reserved highway bus from Shinjuku - book online in advance as Indian long-weekend/peak dates fill. An easy overnight from Tokyo before or after the Golden Route.
- **Hidden gems worth the detour - Toyama, Matsumoto, Kurobe Gorge, Noto** — Standouts near the Alps route: Matsumoto (Nagano) for the black 'Crow Castle' - one of Japan's 12 original castles - and a gateway to Kamikochi; Toyama, named in the New York Times '52 Places to Go in 2025', a stylish port-and-glass city and the hub for the Alpine Route, Kurobe Gorge (dramatic torokko sightseeing train through a ravine) and the Noto Peninsula coast. For an off-season foliage alternative to crowded Nikko, Niigata has superb autumn colours. Gokayama, near Shirakawa-go, has similar gassho farmhouses with a fraction of the visitors.
    - 💴 All lie on or just off the Hokuriku Shinkansen / Alpine corridor, so they add little transport cost if you're already doing Kanazawa/Takayama.
    - 💡 Matsumoto and Kanazawa are all-season; Kurobe Gorge's torokko train and Noto/Alps scenery are best late spring-autumn (gorge line is a warm-season operation).
    - 🇮🇳 These give the 'authentic, uncrowded Japan' many Indian repeat-visitors want on a second trip, without needing a domestic flight.
- **India practicalities - visa, flights, JR Pass, currency, food** — Visa: Indians now get a Japan e-Visa (rolled out for India in April 2024) - base fee ~₹500 (single or multiple entry) plus a VFS service charge of ~₹800, so budget ~₹1,300 total; you still submit documents through VFS and it arrives as an e-Visa rather than a passport sticker. Apply up to ~90 days before travel. Flights: direct Delhi-Tokyo options exist (Japan Airlines, Air India; aggregators also list IndiGo direct), ~7h40 in the air; aggregator fares around ₹70,000+ appear for the route (verify one-way vs return and dates yourself - fares swing a lot). Rail: a nationwide 7-day JR Pass is ¥50,000 (~₹29,500) ordinary / ¥70,000 (~₹41,300) Green (14-day ¥80,000, 21-day ¥100,000); it does NOT fully cover the fastest Nozomi/Mizuho (a supplement applies - use Hikari instead). It's only worth buying for heavy multi-region rail; overseas-agent prices rise ~5-6% from 1 Oct 2026. Food: vegetarian, Jain and halal food is very findable in Tokyo/Kyoto/Osaka (e.g. Vege Herb Saga in Tokyo offers Jain options; Nishi-Kasai in Tokyo is a 'Little India' with halal Indian restaurants); use HappyCow and pre-plan, as small towns and the Alps/Hokkaido countryside are harder.
    - 💴 7-day JR Pass ¥50,000 (~₹29,500); Green ¥70,000 (~₹41,300). Currency reference: 1 JPY ~ 0.59 INR as of 24 Jul 2026 (so ¥10,000 ~ ₹5,900) - rates move, so re-check.
    - 💡 Cheapest month to fly Delhi-Tokyo tends to be October. Reserve seats on limited-express and direct Fuji/Alps trains in advance during peak Indian holiday periods.
    - 🇮🇳 Do the maths before buying a JR Pass: if your trip is mostly Golden Route + one Alps detour, individual tickets + a regional pass often beat the ¥50,000 nationwide pass. Carry some cash (Japan is still cash-friendly at small shops/rural inns), and note IC cards (Suica/Pasmo) for city transit. Book domestic flights for Hokkaido/Okinawa separately - the JR Pass doesn't help there.

_Sources: https://www.japan-guide.com/e/e2400_hokkaido_winter.html · https://matcha-jp.com/en/3742 · https://www.sapporo.travel/en/event/event-list/sapporo_snow_festival/ · https://www.japan-bullettrain.com/articles/blog/hokkaido-shinkansen · https://tokyocheapo.com/travel/transport/tokyo-to-sapporo/ · https://www.gltjp.com/en/article/item/20292/ · https://www.japan-guide.com/e/e6826.html · https://blog.japanwondertravel.com/complete-guide-to-okinawa-19091 · https://www.visit-okinawa.com/en/plan-your-trip/ · https://www.klook.com/blog/japan-tateyama-alpine-route-guide/ · https://www.travelclassroom.net/eng/snow-wall.html · https://www.alpen-route.com/en/fare/_


### B8. Must-try Japanese foods and the best streets, markets and named restaurants to eat them (for an Indian traveler)
_Japan rewards eating your way through it city by city: Tokyo for sushi, tempura, tonkatsu and yakitori alleys; Osaka for takoyaki/okonomiyaki/kushikatsu "kuidaore" street food; Kyoto for kaiseki, soba, matcha and wagashi. The single best value moves for a first-timer are (1) morning sushi at Tsukiji Outer Market, (2) ¥110-a-plate conveyor-belt sushi chains (Sushiro/Kura/Hama), (3) grazing the covered markets (Nishiki in Kyoto, Kuromon in Osaka, Ameyoko in Tokyo), and (4) a splurge Kobe-beef teppanyaki lunch (from ~¥3,500 vs ¥15,000+ at dinner). Rough currency anchor for this blog: in July 2025 1 JPY ≈ ₹0.586, so ¥1,000 ≈ ₹586 (rates move — quote live). Indian travelers should plan around few beef/pork-free needs: Japan now has ~800 halal/pork-free certified restaurants, strong shojin-ryori (Buddhist vegetarian) and Indian-restaurant clusters in Shinjuku/Ikebukuro, plus apps HappyCow and Halal Gourmet Japan. India needs a Japan eVISA (single-entry, up to 90 days; ~INR 500 fee via accredited agency); a 7-day JR Pass is ¥50,000 (~₹29,300) after the 2023 hike, so cost it against your actual route._

- **Sushi at Tsukiji Outer Market (Tokyo) — the classic morning ritual** — Tsukiji Outer Market (the shops district that stayed after the wholesale auctions moved to Toyosu) is the beginner-friendly place to eat serious sushi and seafood bowls (kaisendon). Named affordable spots: Sushi Katsura (weekday lunch 'ichininmae' set = 9 nigiri + 1 maki), Motodane (a backstreet local favourite, chirashi bowl ~¥1,000), and Tsukiji Koromo Sushi (a 9-seater on the market's rim, morning nigiri sets and kaisendon). Go hungry and early — most stalls wind down by early afternoon.
    - 💴 Sushi Katsura lunch set ~¥1,300 (~₹760); Motodane plates under ¥2,500 (~₹1,465), chirashi ~¥1,000 (~₹586); Koromo Sushi kaisendon ¥1,650–¥4,400 (~₹970–₹2,580), 8:30am–noon nigiri sets from ¥1,540 (~₹900)
    - 💡 Arrive by 8–9am to beat crowds and get the freshest cuts; eat standing near the stall (walking-and-eating is frowned upon). Tsukiji is a short walk from Tsukiji or Tsukijishijo stations.
    - 🇮🇳 Sushi is mostly fish/shellfish; strict vegetarians can still enjoy tamago (sweet egg), kappa-maki (cucumber), inari (sweet tofu pouch) and market fruit/tofu-skin stalls nearby. Most Tsukiji stalls are cash-only — carry ¥10,000 in small notes (~₹5,860).
- **Conveyor-belt sushi (kaiten-zushi) — the ₹65-a-plate gateway** — The cheapest, most fun, most kid- and first-timer-friendly sushi is the big conveyor-belt chains: Sushiro (Japan's #1, 500+ branches), Kura Sushi (touch-screen ordering, capsule-toy rewards) and Hama Sushi. You order on a tablet, plates arrive on a belt/express lane, and you stack plates to tally the bill. Great for trying salmon, tuna, ebi, tamago, and cooked/veg options without commitment.
    - 💴 Standard plates ¥110–¥150 (~₹65–₹88); premium ¥220–¥330 (~₹130–₹193); a full meal of 8–12 plates + drink ≈ ¥1,500–¥2,500 (~₹880–₹1,465). Kura branches are tiered ¥115/¥120/¥130/¥150 — check the door sign.
    - 💡 Peak lunch/dinner means waits — pull a ticket from the machine at the door. Green tea (agari) is free: matcha powder at the seat + a hot-water tap.
    - 🇮🇳 Tablet menus usually have English; plenty of non-raw picks (corn, inari, cucumber roll, fried chicken/karaage, egg) so vegetarians and the raw-fish-hesitant eat well and cheaply.
- **Ramen — know the regional styles before you slurp** — Ramen is regional. Hakata/Fukuoka = rich milky tonkotsu (pork-bone) broth with thin straight noodles (order kaedama = noodle refill). Sapporo/Hokkaido = hearty miso ramen built for snow, often with butter and corn. Tokyo = classic soy (shoyu) broth. Kyoto leans chicken/poultry and lighter soy. Ramen-hunting hubs: Sapporo's Ramen Yokocho (Ramen Alley) in Susukino (~17 shops since 1951), Fukuoka's Ramen Stadium (5th floor of Canal City Hakata, many styles in one spot), and Kyoto Ramen Koji inside Kyoto Station. Kyoto institutions: Shinpuku Saikan (since 1938) and Menya Gokkei (dense tori-paitan).
    - 💴 A bowl typically runs ¥800–¥1,200 (~₹470–₹700); toppings/refills add ¥150–¥300 (~₹90–₹175)
    - 💡 Many shops use a vending-machine ticket system: buy your ticket at the door, hand it to the counter. Slurping is normal and encouraged.
    - 🇮🇳 Most broths are pork- or chicken-based — not vegetarian and rarely halal. Look for dedicated vegan ramen shops (e.g. T's TanTan in Tokyo Station) or halal ramen spots; check HappyCow / Halal Gourmet Japan first.
- **Osaka takoyaki & okonomiyaki — Dotonbori and Kuromon Market** — Osaka is Japan's street-food capital ('kuidaore' = eat till you drop). Takoyaki (molten octopus dough balls): Wanaka (near the Glico running-man sign), Aizuya (credited as takoyaki's birthplace), and Acchichi Honpo (fluffy, fresh octopus). Okonomiyaki (savory cabbage pancake, griddle-cooked): Mizuno and casual Fukutaro on Dotonbori. In Kuromon Market, Takoyaki Doraku Wanaka's Kuromon branch sells a box for ¥500 and okonomiyaki for ¥800; Mitsuki does okonomiyaki ¥1,000–¥2,000.
    - 💴 Takoyaki ~¥500–¥800/box (~₹293–₹470); okonomiyaki ~¥800–¥2,000 (~₹470–₹1,170), pricier ¥1,500–¥2,000 sit-down versions in Dotonbori
    - 💡 Kuromon Market is best 9:00–11:00 before the lunch crush (most shops close ~16:30); carry ¥10,000 cash. Dotonbori is a night spectacle under the neon signs.
    - 🇮🇳 Standard batter/toppings use bonito flakes, and okonomiyaki often has pork/seafood — ask for veg okonomiyaki and no katsuobushi. Takoyaki centers on octopus (not veg).
- **Osaka kushikatsu in Shinsekai — deep-fried skewers, 100-yen a stick** — Shinsekai is the retro district for kushikatsu: battered, deep-fried skewers of meat, seafood and veg dunked in a shared sauce. The famous chain is Daruma; Yaekatsu is the more old-school pick. Also try doteyaki (simmered beef tendon in miso) and grilled offal here.
    - 💴 ~¥100–¥200 per skewer (~₹59–₹117); a filling round is easily under ¥1,500 (~₹880)
    - 💡 The one iron rule, posted everywhere: NO double-dipping in the communal sauce — one dip per skewer, then use the cabbage as a spoon.
    - 🇮🇳 Veg skewers exist (lotus root, mushroom, asparagus, potato) but everything shares the same fryer and communal sauce — not suitable for strict vegetarians/vegans or those avoiding cross-contamination with beef/pork.
- **Tempura & tendon in Tokyo — light, crisp, affordable** — For approachable, high-quality tempura: Tsunahachi (founded 1923, main branch in Shinjuku plus department-store outlets; celebrated its 100th year in 2025), Daikokuya in Asakusa (old-school tendon since the Meiji era), and the budget chain Tenya for a quick tendon (tempura over rice). High-end reference points include Tenichi in Minato for a splurge omakase counter.
    - 💴 Tsunahachi from ¥1,540 (~₹900); Tenya tendon ¥1,000–¥2,000 (~₹586–₹1,170); Daikokuya ¥1,200–¥2,500 (~₹700–₹1,465); high-end counters run much higher
    - 💡 Sit at the counter if you can and eat each piece as it's fried — tempura is at its best in the first minute.
    - 🇮🇳 Vegetable tempura (yasai tendon) is a great vegetarian option, but confirm the dipping sauce/dashi and frying oil — most tempura is fried in vegetable oil, though shared fryers may cook seafood too.
- **Tonkatsu — Japan's crispy pork cutlet done right** — Tonkatsu is a panko-crusted, deep-fried pork cutlet with shredded cabbage, rice and miso soup. The most accessible famous name is Tonkatsu Maisen (founded 1965) — its Aoyama Honten near Omotesando/Shibuya is a converted bathhouse and a pilgrimage; the prized cut is 'kurobuta' (black Berkshire pork). Meguro's Tonki is another beloved old-guard counter.
    - 💴 Maisen set meals from ~¥990 (~₹580); premium kurobuta sets up to ~¥4,000 (~₹2,344)
    - 💡 Grind the sesame seeds tableside and mix in the thick tonkatsu sauce; cabbage refills are usually free.
    - 🇮🇳 This is a pork dish — not for Muslim or vegetarian travelers. If you want the crispy-cutlet experience without pork, look for chicken katsu or halal katsu spots.
- **Udon & soba — Kyoto's noodle soul** — Soba (buckwheat) and udon (thick wheat) noodles, hot in dashi broth or cold with dipping sauce (zaru). Kyoto's signature is nishin soba (hot soba topped with sweet-simmered herring). Landmark Kyoto shops: Honke Owariya (a centuries-old soba house) and Kanei (a soba-otaku pilgrimage spot).
    - 💴 Casual bowls from ~¥1,000 (~₹586); mid-range specialty ¥1,500–¥2,000 (~₹880–₹1,170); refined/kaiseki-style nishin soba ¥2,000–¥2,500 (~₹1,170–₹1,465)
    - 💡 Cold zaru soba is the summer move; slurping cools the noodles and is polite. Soba-yu (hot noodle water) is often served to drink with the leftover dipping sauce.
    - 🇮🇳 Ask for kake/zaru with a vegetarian/kombu dashi — standard broths use bonito (fish) and sometimes contain fish/meat toppings. Plain zaru soba with tempura veg is a safe, tasty combo.
- **Yakitori in Omoide Yokocho (Shinjuku) — smoky charcoal alley** — Omoide Yokocho ('Memory Lane') is a warren of ~70 tiny post-war eateries just outside Shinjuku Station's west exit, thick with charcoal smoke and grilled-chicken skewers (yakitori) — every part of the bird, salt (shio) or sweet soy (tare). Pair with beer or a highball elbow-to-elbow at the counter. For cheap chain yakitori elsewhere, Torikizoku prices most skewers at a flat ¥298.
    - 💴 Skewers roughly ¥150–¥400 each (~₹88–₹234); Torikizoku flat ¥298 (~₹175); budget ¥2,000–¥3,500 (~₹1,170–₹2,050) for a full sit-down with drinks
    - 💡 Most shops open from 5pm and cap you at ~90 minutes when busy; photography is banned inside many stalls — ask first. Bring cash.
    - 🇮🇳 Chicken-heavy and rarely halal; strict vegetarians will struggle here beyond grilled veg skewers and edamame. Many alley bars also levy an otoshi (seating-charge appetizer) of ¥300–¥700.
- **Wagyu / Kobe beef teppanyaki — the big splurge, cheaper at lunch** — Kobe beef (from Tajima cattle in Hyogo) is the world-famous, intensely marbled wagyu, best experienced teppanyaki-style with the chef grilling it on an iron plate in front of you. Eat it in Kobe's Sannomiya district: Steak Land is the go-to value institution; Teppan Dining Houki does budget A5 sets; Steak House Ikuta is a higher-end option.
    - 💴 Steak Land business lunch Kobe beef from ~¥3,500 (~₹2,050); Steak Land sets ¥3,200 (regular) to ¥5,300 (Kobe, 180g) (~₹1,875–₹3,105); full teppanyaki dinner typically ¥8,000–¥30,000 (~₹4,690–₹17,580); reckon a few thousand yen per 100g of Kobe beef
    - 💡 Book the LUNCH slot for the same beef at roughly half the dinner price; reserve ahead for Steak Land as queues are long.
    - 🇮🇳 This is beef — off the table for many Hindu and all vegetarian travelers. If you want the wagyu experience without beef, some yakiniku spots in Shinjuku are halal-certified (wagyu), but classic 'Kobe beef' venues are not.
- **Kaiseki — Kyoto's seasonal multi-course haute cuisine** — Kaiseki is the pinnacle of Japanese dining: a choreographed, seasonal multi-course meal emphasizing presentation and local produce, at its best in Kyoto's ryotei. Prices span from accessible lunches to eye-watering dinners; byFood and Tabelog list reservable options from great-value to luxury.
    - 💴 Kyoto kaiseki dinner averages ~¥15,000/person (~₹8,790); value dinners under ¥10,000 (~₹5,860); lunch far cheaper at ~¥3,000–¥4,000 (~₹1,758–₹2,344); top ryotei can exceed ¥50,000 (~₹29,300)
    - 💡 Do kaiseki at LUNCH for a fraction of the dinner price. Many require advance reservation (byFood/Tablecheck take English bookings).
    - 🇮🇳 Book a kaiseki that offers shojin-ryori (Buddhist temple vegetarian) or a dedicated veg course — a standard kaiseki is fish/meat-forward and dashi-based. Flag dietary needs at reservation, not on arrival.
- **Matcha & wagashi in Kyoto/Uji — tea capital sweets** — Uji, just south of Kyoto, is Japan's premier matcha region. Historic tea houses: Tsuen Teahouse (said to be Japan's oldest, does matcha soba and kakigori shaved ice), Marukyu Koyamaen (top-tier tea since 1704, with the Motoan tea room), and Mitsuboshien Kanbayashi Sannyu (founded 1522, ceremonies with rare grinding). Wagashi to try: nama-yatsuhashi (soft mochi-skin sweets filled with red bean) — Otabe is the famous maker — plus strawberry daifuku from Nishiki Kofukudo in Nishiki Market.
    - 💴 Matcha + wagashi set ~¥700–¥2,500 (~₹410–₹1,465); tea-ceremony experiences ¥3,000–¥15,000 (~₹1,758–₹8,790)
    - 💡 A matcha-and-sweet set at a garden tea house is a cheaper, calmer alternative to a full ceremony. Uji is a short JR/Keihan ride from Kyoto.
    - 🇮🇳 Wagashi and matcha are almost all vegetarian (red-bean/mochi/tea) — one of the safest, most delightful categories for Indian veg travelers. Watch only for occasional gelatin in some jellies.
- **Izakaya — Japan's pub-grub culture, how to do it right** — An izakaya is a casual drinking-and-small-plates pub: order rounds of karaage (fried chicken), sashimi, grilled skewers, edamame and agedashi tofu with beer, highballs or sake. Value picks: Torikizoku (flat ~¥298 yakitori), Tsukada Nojo (Kyushu chicken), and Gonpachi (the Edo-themed 'Kill Bill' izakaya, surprisingly affordable). Sasagin has held a Bib Gourmand for quality sake pairings.
    - 💴 Small plates ¥300–¥800 each (~₹175–₹470); expect an otoshi seating-charge appetizer of ¥300–¥700 (~₹175–₹410); a night out ¥2,500–¥5,000/person (~₹1,465–₹2,930)
    - 💡 Look for chains with English/picture menus (One Day Away guide flags Shinjuku spots for English speakers). 'Toriaezu nama' (a draft beer to start) is the customary opener.
    - 🇮🇳 The otoshi cover charge is automatic and non-optional — not a scam. Vegetarian-friendly orders: edamame, agedashi tofu, veg tempura, potato salad, grilled shiitake, hiyayakko (cold tofu). Say 'osake nomimasen' if you don't drink — soft drinks/oolong tea are fine.
- **Nishiki Market (Kyoto) — 'Kyoto's Kitchen', 400 years old** — A 400m covered arcade of 100+ stalls, the best single spot to graze Kyoto specialties. Must-tries: tako-tamago (candied baby octopus with a quail egg inside its head), dashimaki tamago (dashi rolled omelette), hamo (pike-eel) and shrimp tempura skewers, fresh yuba (tofu skin, a Kyoto delicacy), obanzai home-cooking, and Kyoto pickles — senmaizuke, suguki, shibazuke. Sweets everywhere: matcha soft-serve, warabi-mochi, and strawberry daifuku at Nishiki Kofukudo.
    - 💴 Skewers/snacks mostly ¥200–¥700 (~₹117–₹410); roll-chicken skewer ~¥700 (~₹410); graze the whole street for ¥1,500–¥2,500 (~₹880–₹1,465)
    - 💡 Etiquette: don't walk-and-eat — stand aside near the stall or use its seating. Come mid-morning; some stalls sell out by late afternoon.
    - 🇮🇳 Excellent for vegetarians: yuba, tofu, pickles, dashimaki (egg), matcha sweets and fresh fruit. Tako-tamago and tempura contain seafood, so pick selectively.
- **Kuromon Market (Osaka) — 'Osaka's Kitchen' for seafood** — A 190-year-old covered market and Osaka's top grazing ground, heavy on grilled and raw seafood. Named stalls: Kuromon Sanpei (praised salmon sashimi), Maguroya Kurogin (three-colour tuna bowl ~¥1,500), Maguro no Entoki (otoro fatty-tuna sushi ¥2,000–¥2,900), Uofuko (charcoal-grilled fish to order), Takoyaki Doraku Wanaka (¥500 box), and MARU (photogenic melon soft-serve). Also wagyu skewers, grilled scallops, crab legs and tamago-yaki.
    - 💴 Street-food only under ¥2,000 (~₹1,170); sit-down lunch ~¥3,000 (~₹1,758), dinner ~¥4,000 (~₹2,344); tuna bowl ~¥1,500 (~₹880)
    - 💡 Arrive by 11:00 for lunch (peak 12:00–13:30); most shops close by 16:30. Open roughly 9:00–16:00.
    - 🇮🇳 Seafood-dominant; vegetarians get fruit, melon soft-serve, tamago and grilled veg but limited mains. Many vendors are cash-only — carry ¥10,000 (~₹5,860).
- **Ameyoko (Tokyo) & Omoide Yokocho — Tokyo's raucous food lanes** — Ameyoko is a ~500m open-air market strip along the Yamanote line between Ueno and Okachimachi — a chaotic run of fresh seafood, dried goods, fruit, spices, cheap eats and standing bars, great for kaisendon, grilled seafood and snacks. Pair a Tokyo food-alley day with Omoide Yokocho (Shinjuku) for yakitori after dark and, for the tiniest bars, Golden Gai nearby.
    - 💴 Street snacks ¥300–¥1,000 (~₹175–₹586); standing-bar seafood plates ¥500–¥1,500 (~₹293–₹880)
    - 💡 Ameyoko is liveliest late afternoon and into evening; it's a short walk from Ueno Station (easy on the Yamanote loop). Great for bargain shopping too.
    - 🇮🇳 Ameyoko has some of Tokyo's best-value fresh fruit and dried snacks (good for veg travelers), plus multi-ethnic stalls. Most stalls are cash-only.
- **Indian-traveler logistics: money, veg/halal, visa & rail** — Currency anchor for budgeting: in July 2025 1 JPY ≈ ₹0.586 (₹586 per ¥1,000) — the 2025 average was ~0.583 and rates swing, so quote a live rate at publish time. Food-wise, Japan now has ~800 halal/pork-free certified restaurants nationwide, strong shojin-ryori (Buddhist vegetarian) at temples, and Indian-restaurant clusters in Shinjuku, Ikebukuro and near Osaka; use HappyCow (veg) and Halal Gourmet Japan / Halal Japan apps to locate spots and mosques. Getting there: India requires a Japan eVISA (single-entry, up to 90 days) applied via an accredited agency; a JR Pass 7-day is ¥50,000 (~₹29,300), 14-day ¥80,000, 21-day ¥100,000 after the 2023 ~65–70% hike.
    - 💴 eVISA fee ~INR 500; JR Pass 7-day ¥50,000 (~₹29,300); Delhi–Tokyo airfare seen from ~US$249 one-way up to ~US$1,671 depending on dates (Air India flies Delhi–Tokyo; Mumbai–Tokyo direct launching summer 2026)
    - 💡 Only buy the JR Pass if your route (e.g. multiple Tokyo–Kyoto–Osaka–Hiroshima shinkansen legs) beats individual fares — for a Tokyo-Kyoto-Osaka triangle it often doesn't. Apply for the eVISA at least 1–2 weeks before travel.
    - 🇮🇳 Carry cash: Japan is still cash-heavy at markets/street stalls (¥10,000 buffer in small notes). IC cards (Suica/ICOCA) cover trains and convenience stores. Convenience stores (7-Eleven, Lawson, FamilyMart) are lifesavers for cheap veg onigiri (check fillings), egg sandwiches and fruit.

_Sources: https://tokyocheapo.com/food-and-drink/sushi/best-affordable-tokyo-sushi-restaurants/ · https://www.tokyoweekender.com/food-and-drink/restaurants-and-bars/affordable-omakase-sushi-restaurants-in-tokyo/ · https://www.nippon.com/en/japan-topics/c13806/ · https://www.tatlerasia.com/dining/food/regional-ramen-styles-japan · https://blog.gaijinpot.com/japans-top-3-regional-ramen-sapporo-tokyo-and-fukuoka/ · https://matcha-jp.com/en/8236 · https://eatinjapan.jp/articles/osaka-street-food-guide · https://www.fun-japan.jp/en/articles/11627 · https://www.byfood.com/blog/kyoto/nishiki-market-street-food-guide · https://www.byfood.com/blog/shinjuku-omoide-yokocho-p-832 · https://www.japan-guide.com/e/e3564.html · https://savorjapan.com/contents/discover-oishii-japan/5-famous-restaurants-for-enjoying-kobe-beef-on-yakiniku-day-sannomiya_


### B9. Shopping in Japan (districts, stores, what to buy, tax-free rules) — for an Indian traveler
_Japan is a shopper's playground where the 10% consumption tax comes off for tourists. RIGHT NOW (mid-2026) you show your passport at the register and pay the tax-free price on the spot once you cross 5,000 yen (about ₹2,950) at one store in one day. Big caveat for anyone travelling after 1 November 2026: the whole system flips to "pay full price in-store, claim the refund at the airport" — so plan for a customs terminal / Visit Japan Web step on departure. Tokyo covers every taste (Ginza for luxury + Itoya stationery, Shibuya/Harajuku for youth fashion, Akihabara for electronics + anime); Osaka's Shinsaibashi arcade and Den Den Town mirror it; Asakusa's Nakamise is the traditional-souvenir strip. Indians reliably load up on electronics/cameras (Bic Camera + Yodobashi, where you can stack a scan-coupon on top of tax-free for ~17% off), drugstore skincare (Hada Labo, Shiseido — 30-50% cheaper than in India), Kit Kats, matcha, kitchen knives, whisky, and haul basics at Uniqlo/Muji/Loft/Daiso. Two hard limits for the flight home: India lets you bring back only 2 litres of alcohol duty-free, and goods worth up to ₹75,000 duty-free. Rate used: 1 JPY ≈ ₹0.59 (24 Jul 2026)._

- **Tax-free = 10% off, but grab it NOW — the rules change 1 Nov 2026** — Japan's consumption tax is 10%. Today, at any 'Tax-Free' shop you present your original passport (photocopies are refused) OR a Visit Japan Web QR code, and the 10% is deducted right at the till — instant, in-store. From 1 November 2026 this ends: tourists will pay the full tax-inclusive price in the shop and claim the refund at the airport before departure, clearing a customs terminal / Visit Japan Web check (a 'green response') before check-in. The Nov-2026 reform also scraps the consumables-vs-general-goods split and sets one window — all purchases within 90 days of departure.
    - 💴 Saves the 10% tax. On a ¥50,000 (approx ₹29,500) camera that is ¥5,000 (approx ₹2,950) back.
    - 💡 If you fly on/after 1 Nov 2026, budget extra airport time and keep purchases accessible for customs. Set up Visit Japan Web before you land to skip passport re-scanning at every store.
    - 🇮🇳 You qualify on a normal tourist (short-term visitor) status — the exact status tourist Indians travel on. Carry the physical passport for shopping even if you also have Visit Japan Web.
- **The minimum spend & the consumables-vs-goods split (until Nov 2026)** — You must spend at least 5,000 yen (before tax) at the SAME store on the SAME day. Two buckets, counted separately: 'General goods' (electronics, clothes, watches, knives) and 'Consumables' (food, cosmetics, medicine, matcha, snacks). Consumables must total 5,000–500,000 yen and get sealed in a tamper-evident bag you must NOT open until you leave Japan. In department stores/malls with a central tax-free counter, purchases from multiple in-mall shops can be combined to clear the 5,000-yen floor.
    - 💴 Threshold: 5,000 yen approx ₹2,950 (before tax); consumables cap 500,000 yen approx ₹2,95,000.
    - 💡 Don't rip open that sealed cosmetics/snack bag in your hotel — opening it in Japan voids the exemption and can be flagged at departure. Save all tax-free receipts (they're stapled/attached to your passport in some stores).
    - 🇮🇳 Since 1 April 2025, tax-free goods shipped/couriered to India separately no longer qualify — you must physically carry them out of Japan.
- **Tokyo districts: match the neighbourhood to what you want** — GINZA = luxury + flagships: the 12-floor Uniqlo Ginza global flagship, Ginza Six, the renovated Loft Ginza, and Itoya (a whole building of stationery). SHIBUYA = youth fashion + one-stop souvenir runs: Shibuya 109, Shibuya Parco (with Nintendo Tokyo + Pokemon Center), and a Mega Don Quijote. HARAJUKU = Takeshita-dori for loud teen street-fashion and crepes, parallel Omotesando for designer boutiques, and Cat Street for vintage/indie labels. AKIHABARA = 'Electric Town' for electronics, cameras, gaming, anime and manga.
    - 💴 Free to wander; most big stores here are tax-free registered.
    - 💡 Do Harajuku (Takeshita) early morning to beat crushing crowds; Ginza's main street goes pedestrian-only on weekend afternoons — nicest time to stroll.
    - 🇮🇳 Ginza and Shibuya have the most English-signed, card-accepting tax-free counters — easiest first stop if it's your first Japan trip.
- **Osaka: Shinsaibashi arcade, Den Den Town, and Dotonbori** — SHINSAIBASHI-SUJI is a ~600m covered arcade — one of Osaka's oldest — packed with brand shops, chains, boutiques and drugstores (rain-proof, great for a knife/cosmetics/fashion sweep). DEN DEN TOWN (Nipponbashi) is Osaka's Akihabara: electronics, gaming and otaku goods, with 'Ota Road' for anime figures and manga. DOTONBORI, ~500m along the canal under the iconic Glico running-man sign, is the street-food + neon strip (takoyaki, okonomiyaki) that flows straight off the south end of Shinsaibashi.
    - 💴 Shinsaibashi and Dotonbori are adjacent — one afternoon covers both.
    - 💡 Nearby Kuromon Ichiba market is the 'kitchen of Osaka' for fresh food and cheap eats. Chain drugstores here (Matsumoto Kiyoshi, Kokumin) often run tourist coupons on top of tax-free.
    - 🇮🇳 Osaka's Kansai (KIX) airport has direct flights from major Indian metros, so many Indians start their shopping here before Kyoto/Tokyo.
- **Asakusa's Nakamise: the traditional-souvenir strip** — Nakamise is a 250m lane of ~89 stalls running from the Kaminarimon 'Thunder Gate' to Sensoji temple. It's THE place for traditional gifts: folding fans (sensu), mini Kaminarimon paper lanterns, chopsticks, hand towels (tenugui), kokeshi wooden dolls, yukata, and Japanese T-shirts — plus fresh snacks like ningyo-yaki (red-bean doll cakes), senbei rice crackers, taiyaki and melonpan.
    - 💴 Small souvenirs from a few hundred yen; a decent folding fan roughly 1,000–3,000 yen (approx ₹590–1,770).
    - 💡 Eat the fresh snacks on the spot — vendors discourage walking-and-eating, so step aside. Prices here run a bit touristy; Daiso/Don Quijote are cheaper for bulk trinkets.
    - 🇮🇳 Great for lightweight, flat gifts (fans, tenugui, chopsticks) that survive a checked bag and spread across many relatives back home.
- **Bic Camera & Yodobashi: stack a coupon ON TOP of tax-free** — For cameras, electronics, beauty appliances and gadgets, the two giants are Bic Camera and Yodobashi (Yodobashi's Akiba store is a landmark). The trick at Bic Camera: show a live scan-coupon (from the store's app/site — screenshots don't work, you need internet) FIRST for 3–7% off, THEN take the 10% tax-free — roughly 17% total on eligible items. Typical coupon tiers: 7% on cameras/beauty/watches, 5% on cosmetics/medicine/snacks, 3% on liquor.
    - 💴 Worked example: a ¥40,000 (approx ₹23,600) beauty appliance drops to ~¥37,200 (approx ₹21,950) after 7% coupon + tax-free — about ¥6,800 (approx ₹4,000) saved.
    - 💡 Apple products, imported watches and game consoles/software usually get ZERO coupon (tax-free only). Bic wins for tourists over Yodobashi because Yodobashi's big rewards are store-credit 'Gold Points' — useless once you fly home. The free site TAXFREESHOPS.JP aggregates digital coupons.
    - 🇮🇳 Check the plug/voltage: Japan is 100V, India 230V. Cameras/laptops are dual-voltage but hair dryers/appliances often are NOT — buy the international model or you'll fry it in India. Also confirm the warranty is international.
- **Don Quijote (Donki): the chaotic one-stop souvenir warehouse** — Open late (many 24h), Don Quijote crams snacks, cosmetics, electronics, character goods, whisky and quirky souvenirs into cramped, maze-like floors. It's the fastest single-stop to knock out a whole gift list, and it runs its own tourist tax-free counters plus stackable digital coupons.
    - 💴 Digital coupons can save up to ~17% on top of tax-free on eligible items.
    - 💡 Prices are keen but not always the absolute lowest on electronics — Donki wins on breadth and late hours, not always price. Great for last-night, pre-flight bulk snack/Kit Kat hauls.
    - 🇮🇳 The go-to for Indian travellers doing a big 'gifts for the whole family' sweep in one late-night visit near the hotel.
- **Uniqlo, Muji & Loft: quality basics and design goods** — UNIQLO in Japan has the fullest range and often Japan-only items (HeatTech, AIRism, Kaws/UT collabs) at home-country prices; the Ginza flagship is 12 floors. MUJI (Mujirushi Ryohin) is minimalist stationery, skincare, muji-labelled snacks, travel goods and homeware — many lines are Japan-only. LOFT is a lifestyle/variety store for well-designed stationery, beauty, kitchen and travel gadgets — Loft Ginza is a strong made-in-Japan souvenir stop with tax-free.
    - 💴 All three are tax-free registered once you cross 5,000 yen (approx ₹2,950).
    - 💡 Buy Uniqlo/Muji apparel in Japan for the widest sizes/colours and seasonal ranges you won't see in Indian stores. Muji's aroma diffusers, pens and skincare are cult gifts.
    - 🇮🇳 Both Uniqlo and Muji already have stores in India, so compare — buy in Japan mainly for Japan-exclusive lines, collabs, and the tax-free saving.
- **Daiso & the 100-yen shops (Seria, Can Do)** — Daiso is the king of 100-yen shops — most items are 100 yen + tax (i.e. 110 yen), with some 300/500-yen lines. Over 4,600 stores nationwide. Unbeatable for cheap, cute, giftable bulk: stationery, chopsticks, ceramics, phone accessories, snacks, cosmetics tools, cleaning gadgets. Seria and Can Do are the stylish/design-led rivals.
    - 💴 110 yen incl. tax per item ≈ ₹65. A whole gift stash for a few thousand yen.
    - 💡 Many 100-yen shops do NOT do small-value tax-free (you rarely hit 5,000 yen in useful items anyway) — treat the 110-yen price as final. Perfect for 'small gift for every colleague' runs.
    - 🇮🇳 The single best value-for-money souvenir source for Indian travellers wanting many small gifts on a tight budget.
- **Skincare & cosmetics: the drugstore haul Indians love** — Japanese drugstores (Matsumoto Kiyoshi, Welcia, Kokumin, Sundrug) are a beauty goldmine. Cult buys: Hada Labo Gokujyun hyaluronic lotion, Shiseido Senka Perfect Whip face wash, Biore UV Aqua Rich sunscreen, Melano CC vitamin-C serum, Kao steam eye masks, Cure exfoliating gel, and DHC/Shiseido items. Buy at the drugstore, not import shops.
    - 💴 Drugstore skincare runs roughly 30–50% cheaper bought in Japan than via importers/specialty stores abroad; most drugstores stack a 5% cosmetics coupon on top of tax-free.
    - 💡 Cosmetics count as 'consumables' — they'll be sealed in a bag you can't open till you leave Japan. Combine to clear the 5,000-yen consumables threshold.
    - 🇮🇳 Biore/Anessa sunscreens and Melano CC are dramatically cheaper here than Indian import prices — a top ask-list item for family and friends. All are vegetarian-relevant (topical, not food), no food restriction issue.
- **Japanese kitchen knives: the premium craft souvenir** — A Japanese chef's knife (gyuto, santoku, nakiri) is the classic 'buy-it-for-life' souvenir — famed for sharpness and craftsmanship. Best hunting grounds: Kappabashi 'Kitchen Town' in Tokyo (near Asakusa), Sakai knife shops near Osaka, and Nishiki Market area in Kyoto. Brands to know: Shun, Tojiro (great value), Global, and artisan Sakai/Seki forges.
    - 💴 Solid entry santoku from roughly 8,000–15,000 yen (approx ₹4,700–8,850); artisan blades run far higher. Tax-free applies (general goods, 5,000-yen+ min).
    - 💡 Many shops offer free engraving of a name in Japanese — a memorable gift. Ask them to box it well.
    - 🇮🇳 MUST go in checked baggage — a knife in cabin bags will be confiscated at security. Cushion it; declare only if you exceed the ₹75,000 goods allowance.
- **Matcha & green tea: buy in Kyoto/Uji** — The most prized matcha comes from Uji, near Kyoto — look for houses like Ippodo, Marukyu Koyamaen, and Nakamura Tokichi. Buy ceremonial-grade tins for drinking and culinary-grade for baking, plus matcha KitKats, matcha sweets and a bamboo whisk (chasen) set as gifts.
    - 💴 A quality 20–40g tin of ceremonial matcha commonly runs ~1,000–3,000 yen (approx ₹590–1,770); prices vary widely by grade.
    - 💡 Matcha is a consumable — keep it sealed if bought tax-free. It's light and flat — easy to distribute as gifts.
    - 🇮🇳 Fully vegetarian and a huge hit as a gift in India where matcha is trendy but pricey. Keep it away from heat/humidity on the journey.
- **Japanese whisky: what's realistic to bring back** — Accessible bottles you'll actually find: Suntory Toki, Suntory Kakubin, Nikka Days / Nikka From the Barrel, and Suntory Chita. The famous aged single malts (Yamazaki 12, Hakushu 12, Hibiki) are scarce, allocated and expensive — often lottery-only or hugely marked up. Premium tier has surged: Yamazaki 25 / Hakushu 25 / Hibiki 30 recently jumped ~125% to about 360,000 yen (approx ₹2,12,000).
    - 💴 Entry blends are the sane buy (widely available at supermarkets, Don Quijote, Bic Camera; exact shelf prices vary — verify in store). Bic gives 3% coupon on liquor + tax-free.
    - 💡 Don't chase Yamazaki 12 at airport shops expecting bargains — it's rarely there and heavily marked up. Buy your bottle in the city, carry it out.
    - 🇮🇳 HARD LIMIT: India allows only 2 LITRES of alcohol duty-free per adult (18+), combined across all types. That's about two 750ml bottles + a small one, or two 1L bottles. Excess is taxed ~150% or confiscated. Bottle must be sealed, under 70% ABV. Airport duty-free liquor is the safest carry, but a city-bought sealed bottle goes in checked baggage.
- **Kit Kats & snacks — and the vegetarian check** — Japan has hundreds of regional/seasonal Kit Kat flavours — Uji matcha, Shiro (white strawberry), sake, wasabi, strawberry cheesecake. Buy singles at any konbini/Don Quijote, or premium gift boxes at the Kit Kat Chocolatory. Other icons: Tokyo Banana sponge cakes, Royce' nama chocolate, Shiroi Koibito (Hokkaido), Pocky, senbei, and Calbee snacks.
    - 💴 Konbini single Kit Kat bags roughly 300–700 yen (approx ₹180–410); premium Chocolatory boxes cost more.
    - 💡 Buy perishables (Royce' nama chocolate, Tokyo Banana) LAST — they're chilled/short-dated and won't survive a long unrefrigerated flight well.
