    - 🇮🇳 Veg note: Japanese Kit Kats contain no gelatin (broadly vegetarian-suitable) but aren't halal-certified and don't disclose emulsifier sources. Tokyo Banana normally contains gelatin + liqueur — but a Muslim-friendly gelatin-free/alcohol-free version exists (green bow). Royce' nama chocolate usually contains brandy; pick the explicitly alcohol-free 'Mild Cacao' type. On labels, watch for ゼラチン (gelatin) and ゼラチン/洋酒 (liqueur).
- **Practical souvenir shopping list (copy this)** — CHEAP & MANY (Daiso/Donki): assorted Kit Kats, Pocky, senbei, cute stationery, chopsticks, folding fans, character phone charms, face masks. MID (drugstores/Loft/Muji): Hada Labo lotion, Biore/Anessa sunscreen, Melano CC serum, Kao steam eye masks, Muji pens & aroma diffuser, Uji matcha tin + bamboo whisk. NICE GIFTS (Nakamise/Kappabashi/Ginza): tenugui towels, kokeshi doll, Edo Kiriko glass, a Tojiro/Shun kitchen knife (engraved), Itoya notebooks/fountain pen. INDULGENT (self/close family): a whisky bottle, a camera/lens or beauty appliance from Bic Camera, a premium Kit Kat Chocolatory box, ceremonial matcha.
    - 💴 Mix keeps you under India's ₹75,000 duty-free goods cap while covering everyone.
    - 💡 Pack a foldable extra duffel — most travellers underestimate volume. Keep sealed tax-free consumables together for the airport check.
    - 🇮🇳 Prioritise items that are far cheaper or unavailable in India: drugstore skincare, Japan-exclusive Uniqlo/Muji lines, artisan knives, regional Kit Kats. Skip things you can buy at home for similar money.
- **The flight home: India's customs limits & money tips** — India duty-free baggage allowance was raised (effective Feb 2025) to goods worth up to ₹75,000 for Indian-origin travellers (₹25,000 for foreign nationals); above that, duty applies (broadly a flat ~10% under 2025-26 baggage rules on the excess). Alcohol: max 2 litres duty-free per adult. Jewellery (if abroad >1 year): 40g for women, 20g for men.
    - 💴 Exchange reference: 1 JPY ≈ ₹0.59 (24 Jul 2026); so 5,000 yen ≈ ₹2,950, 100,000 yen ≈ ₹59,000. Rates move — verify before you travel.
    - 💡 Japan is still cash-friendly in small shops/markets — carry some yen, but Bic/Yodobashi/Donki/department stores take cards and often UPI/wallet apps. Notified goods above allowance should be declared on the red channel to avoid penalties.
    - 🇮🇳 For best value, use a zero-forex-markup travel card or withdraw yen from 7-Eleven/Japan Post ATMs (reliably accept Indian Visa/Mastercard) rather than airport exchange counters. Keep big-ticket receipts (camera, knife) in case customs asks at an Indian airport.

_Sources: https://www.japan-guide.com/news/tax-free-shopping.html · https://livejapan.com/en/article-a0000238/ · https://travel.rakuten.com/contents/usa/en-us/guide/tax-free-shopping-japan/ · https://www.tokyotourism.org/tokyo-shopping-guide/ · https://www.magical-trip.com/media/tokyo-shopping-districts-guide-2024-best-areas-for-fashion-electronics-souvenirs/ · https://www.japan-guide.com/e/e3053.html · https://www.japan-guide.com/e/e4013.html · https://www.magical-trip.com/media/osaka-nipponbashi-guide-the-town-famous-for-den-den-town/ · https://www.machupicchu.org/nakamise-shopping-street-guide-what-to-buy-near-sensoji.htm · https://www.gotokyo.org/en/spot/73/index.html · https://watabi.org/en/articles/bic-camera-coupon-guide · https://japancoupons.com/yodobashi-camera-vs-bic-camera/_


### B10. Free things to do & freebies in Japan (for an Indian traveler)
_Japan is one of the most rewarding countries in the world for a zero-budget day: its greatest icons — Senso-ji, Meiji Jingu, Fushimi Inari, Nara's deer park, the Imperial Palace East Gardens — charge no admission, and even the panoramic city view usually sold as a paid "SkyTree/Shibuya Sky" experience is available FREE from government and corporate observation decks (the Tokyo Metropolitan Government Building at 202m is the flagship, with Mt Fuji views on clear days). Beyond sights, the "free layer" of Japan is deep: free corporate museums/showrooms (Ad Museum Tokyo, Nissan Crossing, Sumo Museum), free-to-watch matsuri festivals and hanami cherry-blossom picnics, generous free food samples in depachika (department-store food basements) and covered markets like Nishiki and Ameyoko, and genuinely free tourist services — volunteer local guides (Tokyo Free Guide, Kyoto Free Walking Tour), JNTO tourist-information centres, and nationwide free public Wi-Fi. All money figures below use the July 2025 rate of ~100 JPY = 58.6 INR (1 JPY ≈ 0.586 INR); note this rate moves, and most items here cost literally 0 JPY. For Indians, Japan is now easier than ever: an online e-visa (~Rs 500 fee) launched for India in 2024, and Indian arrivals jumped ~40% in 2024._

- **Tokyo Metropolitan Government Building — the free 202m observation deck (Shinjuku)** — The flagship free view in Tokyo. Two observatories (North & South towers) on the 45th floor at 202m above ground, a 10-minute walk from Shinjuku Station. On a clear day you see Tokyo Skytree, Meiji Shrine and even Mt Fuji on the horizon. It is Tokyo's government HQ, not a tourist trap, which is exactly why it's free while the private Shibuya Sky and Tokyo Skytree charge ~2,000-3,000 JPY.
    - 💴 Completely FREE (0 JPY). Compare: Shibuya Sky ~2,500 JPY / ~1,465 INR; Tokyo Skytree ~2,100 JPY / ~1,230 INR.
    - 💡 Generally open ~9:30 to 22:00 (last entry ~21:30); a security bag check is done at entry. One tower is closed on alternating Mondays/Tuesdays for maintenance — the other stays open. Go at dusk to catch both daylight Fuji and the night skyline in one visit.
    - 🇮🇳 No booking, no queue-jump ticket needed — just walk in with your passport-carrying self; ideal first-evening orientation to Tokyo for a family arriving jet-lagged from India.
- **More free observation decks across Tokyo (skip every paid tower)** — Tokyo has a whole circuit of free high-floor lookouts beyond the Metropolitan Building: Caretta Shiodome Sky View (46F, ~210m, Rainbow Bridge & Odaiba views, 1 min from Shiodome Stn); Yebisu Garden Place Tower Sky Lounge (38F, ~160m, sees Tokyo Tower + Skytree + Rainbow Bridge together); LUFTBAUM at NEWoMan Takanawa (28F, 150m, Tokyo Bay) which newly opened September 2025; Sky Carrot / Carrot Tower (26F, 124m, Sangenjaya); Hokutopia (17F, Oji — Shinkansen trains pass directly below); and Nerima City Hall (20F, 80m, Diamond Fuji around 21 Jan & 21 Nov).
    - 💴 All FREE (0 JPY).
    - 💡 Note Bunkyo Civic Center's free deck is closed for renovation until December 2026, so don't route around it. Caretta Shiodome (open to 23:00) is the best late-night free view.
    - 🇮🇳 These are great rainy-day / no-spend afternoons if you're pacing a long multi-city trip and want to keep JPY for shopping or the JR Pass.
- **Senso-ji, Meiji Jingu, Asakusa Shrine & Fushimi Inari — Japan's icon shrines, all free** — Tokyo's most photographed temple, Senso-ji in Asakusa (with the giant red Kaminarimon lantern and Nakamise shopping street), keeps its grounds open and free. The forested Meiji Jingu shrine beside Harajuku is free to enter (only its Inner Garden costs 500 JPY). Asakusa Shrine, tucked quietly behind Senso-ji, is free and far less crowded. In Kyoto, Fushimi Inari Taisha — the endless tunnel of ~10,000 vermilion torii gates up Mt Inari — is free and open 24 hours.
    - 💴 FREE (0 JPY). Only add-ons like Meiji Inner Garden (500 JPY / ~293 INR) or drawing an omikuji fortune (~100-300 JPY) cost anything.
    - 💡 Fushimi Inari at 6-7am is empty and magical; the full loop up the mountain is ~2-3 hours and costs nothing. At Senso-ji, the Nakamise street samples and window-shopping are free even if you buy nothing.
    - 🇮🇳 Shrines have no dress code and welcome all faiths/beliefs — comfortable for Indian visitors of any background; just rinse hands at the water pavilion and be quiet, no ticket needed.
- **Ueno Park, Yoyogi Park & Shiba Park — free green giants of Tokyo** — Ueno Park is a huge free park with ponds, a shrine, cherry trees and the plaza outside its (paid) museums; a classic free stroll. Yoyogi Park, next to Meiji Jingu and Harajuku, is free, pet- and wheelchair-friendly, and on weekends fills with buskers, dancers and free street performance. Shiba Park wraps around Zojo-ji temple with a postcard view of Tokyo Tower and a giant ginkgo, free to wander.
    - 💴 FREE (0 JPY) to enter and picnic.
    - 💡 Weekend afternoons at Yoyogi Park are the best free 'show' in Tokyo — rockabilly dancers, cosplay near Harajuku's Takeshita Street, and impromptu music. Bring a convenience-store bento (~500 JPY / ~293 INR) and picnic.
    - 🇮🇳 Public parks are a safe, free way for families with kids to burn energy after long flights; tap water in Japan is potable so you can refill bottles free rather than buying.
- **Imperial Palace East Gardens (Tokyo) — free but time your visit** — The former grounds of Edo Castle, now landscaped gardens with stone ramparts and seasonal blooms in the heart of Tokyo, are free — you simply walk in, no ticket. This is the only publicly accessible part of the Imperial Palace complex.
    - 💴 FREE (0 JPY).
    - 💡 Crucial: CLOSED on Mondays and Fridays (and Dec 28-Jan 3). Seasonal hours run roughly 9:00 to 16:00-18:00 depending on month, last entry 30 min before closing. If a Mon/Fri is a national holiday it closes the next day instead — check before you walk over.
    - 🇮🇳 Central and easy to combine with a free Tokyo Free Guide volunteer walk (below); pair it with nearby Chidorigafuchi moat for free cherry-blossom viewing in spring.
- **Nara Park — free deer, free ancient shrines (day trip from Kyoto/Osaka)** — Nara Park is free, open 365 days, and roamed by ~1,000 wild-but-tame sika deer that bow for food. Wandering the park, its lawns and the outer grounds of Kasuga Taisha and Todai-ji is free (only the temple interiors/inner halls charge).
    - 💴 Park entry FREE (0 JPY). Deer crackers (shika senbei) from licensed vendors are ~200 JPY / ~117 INR per bundle — the only cost if you want to feed them.
    - 💡 Buy crackers only from official vendors; hide them until you're ready or the deer mob you. Easy free-to-enter day trip — the train fare from Kyoto (~40 min) is the only real spend.
    - 🇮🇳 A huge hit with Indian families and kids; the deer-feeding photo is iconic. Deer are wild animals — supervise small children.
- **Free things beyond Tokyo — Kyoto's Philosopher's Path & Osaka Castle Park / Dotonbori** — Kyoto's Philosopher's Path is a free canal-side walk lined with cherry trees, running to Nanzen-ji temple, named after philosopher Nishida Kitaro who strolled it to think. Osaka Castle Park is free to roam (only the castle keep interior charges admission) with great blossom and moat views. Dotonbori in Osaka — the neon canal district with the Glico running-man sign — costs nothing to soak in; you only pay if you eat.
    - 💴 FREE (0 JPY) for the walks, park and street atmosphere.
    - 💡 Do the Philosopher's Path in cherry-blossom season (late March-early April) or autumn for the free spectacle. Dotonbori is best after dark when the billboards light up.
    - 🇮🇳 These free anchors let you see three cities cheaply if you're using a rail pass; window-shopping Dotonbori is free even if the takoyaki isn't veg-friendly.
- **Free museums & corporate showrooms in Tokyo** — The Ad Museum Tokyo (Shiodome) is one of the city's best free museums, covering Japanese advertising and Edo-era art. The Sumo Museum inside Ryogoku Kokugikan is free outside tournament periods. Sumida Edo Kiriko Museum shows traditional cut-glass with free general admission. Nissan Crossing in ritzy Ginza is a free car showroom/gallery. The Japanese Sake & Shochu Information Center (Minato) has a free library, with tastings for just 100 JPY. Yebisu Beer Museum is free to enter (tastings are the only paid part).
    - 💴 Entry FREE (0 JPY). Sake tasting 100 JPY (~59 INR); beer tastings at Yebisu are optional paid add-ons.
    - 💡 Mega Web / Toyota's big car theme park is permanently CLOSED (since end-2021) — don't rely on old blog posts listing it. Verify hours on official sites, as small free museums keep limited days.
    - 🇮🇳 The 100-JPY sake tasting is a low-risk way to try Japanese rice wine; skip if you don't drink — the museum library and exhibits are still free.
- **Toyosu Market — free tuna-auction viewing (the successor to Tsukiji inner market)** — The world's largest fish market moved to Toyosu; visitors can watch the famous pre-dawn tuna auction from free public observation decks/windows above the trading floor, plus walk the market complex.
    - 💴 Viewing FREE (0 JPY). (The nearby old Tsukiji Outer Market is also free to wander and browse.)
    - 💡 Auction action is very early morning (from ~5:00am); the upper observation windows are open to walk-ins for free, while a limited floor-level viewing spot needs advance reservation.
    - 🇮🇳 Purely a spectacle if you don't eat seafood — the auction theatre and market energy are the free draw; vegetarian Indians can watch without eating anything.
- **Free festivals (matsuri), hanami & Harajuku street culture** — The vast majority of Japanese matsuri are free to watch — you pay nothing to see the processions, portable-shrine (mikoshi) parades and shrine grounds; only food stalls and games cost money. Tokyo's wild Sanja Matsuri in Asakusa (mid-May; 2026 dates 15-17 May) sends 100+ mikoshi through the streets. Spring hanami (late March-mid April) means free cherry-blossom picnics in every park, plus free night illuminations like Chidorigafuchi's yozakura by the Imperial moat. The Takayama Spring Festival (14-15 April, Gifu) — one of Japan's three most beautiful festivals — is free to watch. Harajuku's Takeshita Street is a free daily parade of youth fashion.
    - 💴 FREE (0 JPY) to attend/watch. Only stall food, games and souvenirs cost extra (optional).
    - 💡 Check exact 2026 dates before you fix travel — matsuri dates shift yearly. Arrive early for a good curb spot at big processions; hanami spots in popular parks fill by mid-morning on weekends.
    - 🇮🇳 Festival street food often isn't vegetarian/halal — enjoy the spectacle and buy safe items (grilled corn, sweet potato, taiyaki, dango) if unsure; the atmosphere itself is the free experience.
- **Free food samples — depachika food halls & covered markets** — Depachika (department-store basement food halls, e.g. under Daimaru, Isetan, Takashimaya) hand out generous free tasting samples of sweets, pickles, and prepared foods — you can graze while browsing without buying. Kyoto's 400-year-old Nishiki Market ('Kyoto's Kitchen', 100+ family stalls) and Tokyo's Ameyoko market near Ueno are famous for free tasters of things like yuba (tofu skin), tea, mochi and dried snacks.
    - 💴 Samples are FREE (0 JPY) — offered so you'll consider buying; there's no obligation to purchase.
    - 💡 Sampling is expected and polite when done in moderation — take one, and buy if you love it. Late afternoon in depachika sometimes brings marked-down bento (a cheap, not-free, tip for dinner).
    - 🇮🇳 Ask before tasting if you're vegetarian/Jain — many Japanese samples contain dashi (fish stock), pork or bonito even when they look plant-based; learn 'bejitarian desu' and check for katsuo/niku. Nishiki has tofu/yuba options that are often vegetarian.
- **Free volunteer guides & free walking tours (Tokyo & Kyoto)** — Tokyo Free Guide (tokyofreeguide.org) pairs overseas visitors with local volunteer guides for a private, no-fee tour of Tokyo (and even Kamakura); routes avoid paid-entry sites. Tokyo Free Walking Tour (TFWT / Tokyo Localized) runs volunteer-led group walks with no mandatory fee. Kyoto Free Walking Tour is 100% free, run by Kyoto locals. Nationwide, government-recognised SGG (Systematized Goodwill Guide) groups — often retirees and students — guide visitors for free all over Japan.
    - 💴 Guide service is FREE (0 JPY). You're expected to cover your guide's incidental costs (their train fare/entry tickets if a site charges), and tips are optional/appreciated.
    - 💡 Book Tokyo Free Guide well ahead online (volunteers are limited). Confirm the meeting point (usually a station or tourist-info centre) and that the route is entry-fee-free if you want a truly zero-cost day.
    - 🇮🇳 Most volunteers speak English, which suits Indian travelers well; a local guide also helps navigate vegetarian food options and etiquette for free.
- **Free tourist-information centres (JNTO) — maps, advice, freebies** — Japan's tourist information centres (marked with a red '?' or the JNTO logo) at airports, major stations and city centres give out free multilingual maps, transit guides, event calendars and pamphlets, and offer free help with directions and bookings. Many hand out free brochures and coupons and have free Wi-Fi and luggage/rest facilities.
    - 💴 FREE (0 JPY) — maps, advice, brochures and often free Wi-Fi.
    - 💡 Grab the free area maps and current festival/event flyers on arrival — they surface free events happening during your dates that aren't in guidebooks. Airport counters can also help set up your free-Wi-Fi app.
    - 🇮🇳 A first stop straight off your flight from India — staff can explain the free-Wi-Fi apps, point you to vegetarian restaurants, and confirm your e-visa 'issuance notice' process if asked.
- **Free public Wi-Fi across Japan** — Free Wi-Fi is available at international airports, major railway stations, some trains/buses, convenience stores, chain cafes, and tourist-information offices (look for the 'public wireless LAN' mark). The JNTO-backed Japan.Free Wi-Fi service and the 'Japan Wi-Fi auto-connect' app unify many hotspots into one registration. NTT's platform offers free Wi-Fi at ~150,000 locations (app + email registration); SoftBank offers ~400,000 locations (get the password via a free-dial number, no app).
    - 💴 FREE (0 JPY). Note: free public Wi-Fi is less blanket than tourists expect and can be spotty.
    - 💡 Install the 'Japan Wi-Fi auto-connect' app before you travel and register once, so you connect automatically. Because coverage is patchy, many travelers still pair it with a cheap eSIM/SIM (not free) for maps and translation.
    - 🇮🇳 You need internet to show your e-visa 'visa issuance notice' on your phone at immigration, so don't rely solely on free airport Wi-Fi — have an eSIM or roaming ready the moment you land from India.
- **India-specific: cheap e-visa now makes a free-heavy Japan trip realistic** — Japan launched its online e-visa for India on 1 April 2024. It's a single-entry short-term tourist visa (up to 90 days), applied via VFS Global / Japan Visa Application Centres; you receive an electronic 'visa issuance notice' to show on your phone at the airport rather than a passport sticker. Indian arrivals to Japan hit ~233,000 in 2024, up ~40% year-on-year.
    - 💴 Reported fees are low: the visa fee is around Rs 450-500, plus a VFS service charge (~Rs 800 incl. taxes) per some sources — figures vary by source and can change, so confirm on VFS/the Japan Embassy site before applying.
    - 💡 Apply for the e-visa well before travel and keep the digital issuance notice accessible offline + online. Once you're in, the sightseeing itself (shrines, parks, decks, festivals) can be almost entirely free.
    - 🇮🇳 This is the single biggest 'freebie-enabler' for Indians — the low visa cost plus Japan's deep free-attraction layer means the main spend becomes flights, rail and food, not entry tickets. Verify current fees on official VFS Global India / Embassy of Japan pages.

_Sources: https://www.gotokyo.org/en/see-and-do/attractions/free/index.html · https://tokyo.how/guides/free-observation-decks-tokyo · https://www.gltjp.com/en/directory/item/10148/ · https://www.qantas.com/travelinsider/en/explore/asia/japan/tokyo/the-best-free-things-to-do-in-tokyo-for-budget-travel.html · https://www.tokyofreeguide.org/en/tour-guide-service/ · https://www.tokyolocalized.com/ · https://mai-ko.com/travel/japan/kyoto/kyoto-travel-guide/kyoto-has-the-best-free-tour-guides-in-japan/ · https://www.getyourguide.com/kyoto-l96826/kyoto-nishiki-market-and-depachika-food-tour-with-a-local-t463956/ · https://narapark.org/ · https://www.japan-guide.com/e/e3018.html · https://www.gltjp.com/en/directory/item/18846/ · https://festinjapan.jp/articles/free-festivals-japan_


### B11. Best deals & transport passes for Japan (budget guide for an Indian traveler, mid-2026 prices)
_Since the Oct-2023 hike the nationwide JR Pass (7-day ¥50,000 / ~₹29,500) is no longer a default buy: on the classic Tokyo-Kyoto-Osaka "golden route" you LOSE ~¥22,000 versus point-to-point Shinkansen, and it only pays off if you add far cities (Hiroshima, Tohoku, Hokkaido, Kyushu). For most first-timers the winning combo is an IC card (Welcome Suica / ICOCA) for daily hops + city tourist passes (Tokyo Subway Ticket ¥800-1,500, Kyoto Subway & Bus ¥1,100, Osaka Amazing Pass ¥3,500) + regional JR passes only where they fit + Klook/official-site discounts for the odd Shinkansen leg. Big India-specific update: since Sep 2025 Indians get a fully online Japan e-Visa (govt fee just ₹500 from Apr 2026). All yen prices below are current for 2026; INR is approximate at ¥100 ≈ ₹59. Buy the JR Pass on the OFFICIAL JR website to dodge the Oct-1-2026 travel-agency price rise. Note: several passes are tourist-only (show passport), and gyudon/sushi cheap-eats are beef/fish based, not vegetarian._

- **Nationwide JR Pass — current price after the Oct-2023 hike (7/14/21-day)** — The whole-country Japan Rail Pass, valid on almost all JR trains incl. most Shinkansen (except the fastest Nozomi/Mizuho, which need a top-up). Ordinary class: 7-day ¥50,000, 14-day ¥80,000, 21-day ¥100,000. Green (first) class: 7-day ¥70,000, 14-day ¥110,000, 21-day ¥140,000. Kids 6-11 are half price. In Oct 2023 the 7-day jumped from ¥29,650 to ¥50,000 (a ~69% hike) — that single change is why the pass is now a calculation, not a reflex buy.
    - 💴 7-day ¥50,000 (~₹29,500); 14-day ¥80,000 (~₹47,200); 21-day ¥100,000 (~₹59,000). Green: ¥70,000/₹41,300, ¥110,000/₹64,900, ¥140,000/₹82,600
    - 💡 Validity is consecutive calendar days from activation, not 24-hr blocks — start it the morning of your first long-haul day, not on an arrival evening you'll spend in the city.
    - 🇮🇳 You no longer must buy an exchange voucher before flying from India — you can buy the pass online and also at JR stations in Japan. Carry your passport (tourist status) to buy/exchange.
- **Oct-1-2026 price change — buy on the OFFICIAL JR site to avoid it** — From 1 October 2026 the pass gets more expensive ONLY when bought through overseas travel agencies (e.g. 7-day rises to ¥53,000, 14-day ¥84,000, 21-day ¥105,000). If you buy on the official Japan Rail Pass website the current prices (¥50,000/80,000/100,000) stay unchanged. This is a rare case where the manufacturer's channel is cheaper than resellers.
    - 💴 Agency 7-day ¥53,000 (~₹31,300) from Oct 2026 vs official-site ¥50,000 (~₹29,500) — save ~₹1,800 just by buying direct
    - 💡 For a single Shinkansen leg you don't need the pass at all — Klook or the official smartEX app is fine (see Klook item).
- **Is the nationwide JR Pass still worth it? The break-even math** — A Tokyo–Kyoto round trip on a reserved Hikari Shinkansen is only about ¥26,600–27,700. So the classic Tokyo–Kyoto–Osaka 'golden route' generates nowhere near ¥50,000 of JR travel — buyers typically LOSE ~¥22,000 on a 7-day pass. It flips to worth-it when you pile on long JR legs: e.g. Tokyo + Kyoto + Hiroshima in 7 days is roughly ¥61,950 of rides for ¥50,000, and it gets better if you add Kanazawa, Tohoku, Hokkaido or Kyushu.
    - 💴 Tokyo–Kyoto round trip ~¥27,700 (~₹16,300) vs 7-day pass ¥50,000; break-even needs ~¥50,000+ of JR long-distance in 7 days
    - 💡 Rule of thumb: if your itinerary isn't at least Tokyo↔Kyoto/Osaka PLUS one far city (Hiroshima/Kanazawa/Sendai) within the window, skip the nationwide pass and go point-to-point + IC card + regional/city passes.
- **Regional JR passes — JR East (Tohoku/Nagano/Niigata) and Hokkaido** — JR East Pass (from March 2026 a single pass covering Tohoku, Greater Tokyo, Nagano and Niigata): 5-day ¥35,000, 10-day ¥50,000 — great for Tokyo + Nikko/Sendai/Aomori/snow-country loops (the older separate Tohoku ¥30,000 and Nagano-Niigata ¥27,000 5-day passes are being phased out). JR Hokkaido Rail Pass: 5-day ¥22,000, 7-day ¥28,000, 10-day ¥37,000 (the old 3-day is discontinued). These beat the nationwide pass when you stay within one region.
    - 💴 JR East 5-day ¥35,000 (~₹20,650) / 10-day ¥50,000 (~₹29,500); Hokkaido 5-day ¥22,000 (~₹12,980), 7-day ¥28,000 (~₹16,500), 10-day ¥37,000 (~₹21,800)
    - 💡 JR East passes are flexible-day only within a set overall window on some products — check the activation window when you buy; all are tourist-priced (bring passport).
- **Regional JR passes — Kansai (best value for Kyoto/Osaka/Nara/Himeji)** — JR Kansai Wide Area Pass: 5-day ¥12,000 — covers Kyoto, Osaka, Nara, Kobe, Himeji, and even the Shinkansen hop to Okayama, plus the Haruka express from Kansai Airport (KIX) into Kyoto. If you fly into KIX and base yourself in the Kansai region this is far better value than the nationwide pass.
    - 💴 JR Kansai Wide Area Pass 5-day ¥12,000 (~₹7,080); one-way Haruka KIX→Kyoto alone is ~¥3,600, so the pass pays back fast
    - 💡 Don't double-buy: a Kansai JR pass plus the Kansai Thru Pass overlap a lot — pick one based on whether you'll ride mostly JR or mostly private lines/subway.
- **IC cards — Suica/Pasmo/ICOCA and the 2023-25 shortage (now resolved for tourists)** — IC cards are tap-to-ride cards for trains, subways, buses and konbini nationwide. Regular Suica/Pasmo card sales were suspended in 2023 due to a global chip shortage; regular unregistered cards remain hard to get, but tourist versions are back. ICOCA (Kansai's card, sold at Osaka/Kyoto) costs ¥2,000 including a ¥500 refundable deposit (¥1,500 usable). An IC card is the single most useful buy — no fumbling for single tickets.
    - 💴 ICOCA ¥2,000 (~₹1,180) incl. ¥500 refundable deposit; top up as you go
    - 💡 You can refund an ICOCA deposit at JR West machines/counters when leaving. Balance itself is refundable too (minus a small fee) — so avoid over-loading on your last day.
- **Welcome Suica & Pasmo Passport — the tourist IC cards (sales resumed March 2025)** — Welcome Suica is the tourist card designed for short visits: ¥1,500 loaded, no issuing fee, valid 28 days, but NO refund of leftover balance — so spend it down before you fly home. Sales resumed March 2025 after the chip shortage. Buy at JR East Travel Service Centers and vending machines at Haneda and Narita airports, plus Tokyo, Ueno, Ikebukuro, Shibuya, Shinagawa stations. Pasmo Passport is the equivalent (¥1,500, 28 days, no refund). In 2025 JR East also launched a Welcome Suica Mobile app in English that accepts foreign cards via Apple Pay.
    - 💴 Welcome Suica ¥1,500 loaded (~₹885), no card fee, no deposit; Pasmo Passport ¥1,500 (~₹885)
    - 💡 iPhone users can skip the plastic entirely — add a digital Suica in Apple Wallet if it's linked to a Mastercard or Amex. Android support for foreign cards is still limited, so Android users are safer with a physical Welcome Suica.
    - 🇮🇳 Indian-issued Visa cards often can't load a digital Suica on iPhone — carry a Mastercard/Amex if you want the phone-only option, otherwise just grab a physical Welcome Suica at the airport on arrival.
- **Tokyo — Tokyo Subway Ticket (24/48/72-hour, tourists only)** — Unlimited rides on ALL Tokyo Metro + Toei Subway lines for 24, 48 or 72 continuous hours (time-based, not calendar-day). Also gives discounts at 350+ attractions (Ueno Zoo, Sumida Aquarium, etc.). Sold only to overseas tourists and domestic visitors from outside the Kanto area — bring your passport.
    - 💴 24h ¥800 (~₹470), 48h ¥1,200 (~₹710), 72h ¥1,500 (~₹885) — adult; kids 6-11 half price
    - 💡 Buy at airports/tourist centers. It does NOT cover JR lines (incl. the Yamanote loop), so pair it with your IC card for those. Two-plus subway-heavy sightseeing days easily beat pay-per-ride.
- **Kyoto — Subway & Bus 1-Day Pass ¥1,100 (bus-only pass was scrapped)** — Kyoto's tourist workhorse is the Subway & Bus 1-Day Pass at ¥1,100, covering city subway, City Bus, Kyoto Bus and some Keihan/JR buses — good because Kyoto's big sights are bus-reachable. Note the old cheap ¥700 Bus-Only One-Day Pass was discontinued in fall 2023, so don't rely on old blog advice quoting it. The pass also unlocks small museum/shop discounts on the day.
    - 💴 Subway & Bus 1-Day Pass ¥1,100 (~₹650); single city bus rides are ~¥230, so ~5 rides breaks even
    - 💡 If you'll mostly walk between nearby temples, an IC card may be cheaper than the day pass — buy the pass only on heavy-bus days (e.g. Arashiyama + Kinkaku-ji + Gion in one go).
- **Osaka — Osaka Amazing Pass (transport + 40+ free attractions)** — The standout city deal: unlimited Osaka Metro/bus/New Tram AND free entry to 40+ attractions. 1-day ¥3,500, 2-day ¥5,000. Free entries include Osaka Castle Museum (worth ¥1,200), Umeda Sky Building observatory (¥2,000) and the Tombori River Cruise (¥2,000) — hit those three and you've already cleared ¥5,200 of value on a ¥3,500 pass. Now digital-only (QR on your phone; physical cards ended June 2024); activation clock starts 3:00 a.m.
    - 💴 1-day ¥3,500 (~₹2,065), 2-day ¥5,000 (~₹2,950); 3 top attractions ≈ ¥5,200 value already
    - 💡 Only worth it if you're actually doing paid attractions — pure shopping/foodie days in Dotonbori don't justify it. There are airport (Itami) and monorail variants at slightly higher prices if you need those legs.
- **Kansai Thru Pass (Kansai Railway Pass Lite) — private lines across Kansai** — Covers unlimited rides on private railways and subways across Kansai (Osaka, Kyoto, Nara, Kobe, Koyasan) — but NOT JR trains and NOT the Kyoto city subway. Non-consecutive days, so you can use it on any 2 or 3 days within your trip. Ideal if you're hopping between cities on Hankyu/Hanshin/Keihan/Kintetsu/Nankai rather than JR.
    - 💴 2-day ¥5,200 (~₹3,070), 3-day ¥6,500 (~₹3,835), adult
    - 💡 Great for a Koyasan or Nara day trip on Nankai/Kintetsu. Don't stack it with a JR Kansai pass — the coverage overlaps and you'll waste money.
- **Klook / booking-site discounts (and an India-specific card offer)** — Klook (and KKday) routinely discount Japan travel: Shinkansen tickets up to ~15-25% off in promos, airport transfers up to 15-20% off, and eSIM/data plans up to ~20% off. City passes (Tokyo Subway Ticket, Osaka Amazing Pass, Kyoto pass) are all bookable there too, often with a small saving and airport pickup. Promo codes rotate monthly, so check the live promo page before booking.
    - 💴 Up to ~15-25% off Shinkansen; ~20% off eSIM; plus a 10% discount when paying with an India-issued Visa credit card
    - 💡 For a single bullet-train leg, compare Klook vs the official JR smartEX app — sometimes direct booking with a seat reservation is simplest. Book eSIM before departure so you land connected.
    - 🇮🇳 Pay with an Indian Visa credit card on Klook for an extra ~10% off; buy your Japan eSIM/data on Klook before you fly since Indian roaming to Japan is pricey.
- **Discount shops & tax-free shopping (10% back for tourists)** — Don Quijote ('Donki') is the go-to discount megastore — 700+ branches, many open 24h, everything from snacks to electronics to souvenirs, with in-store tax-free counters. 100-yen shops (Daiso — 3,300+ stores, plus Seria and Can Do) sell genuinely useful items at ¥110 incl. tax. Uniqlo and Matsumoto Kiyoshi drugstores are cheap for clothes and cosmetics. Tax-free: spend ¥5,000+ (before tax) at one store the same day and the 10% consumption tax is waived/refunded — show your passport at purchase.
    - 💴 100-yen shop items ¥110 incl. tax (~₹65); tax-free saves the 10% consumption tax on ¥5,000+ (~₹2,950+) single-store spends
    - 💡 Consolidate buys into one store to clear the ¥5,000 tax-free threshold in a single transaction. Keep the tax-free paperwork stapled in your passport — customs can check it on exit.
- **Cheap eats — gyudon, kaiten sushi, konbini** — Japan is a budget-food paradise. Gyudon (beef bowl) chains Yoshinoya, Sukiya and Matsuya sell a regular beef bowl for ~¥450 (mini ¥390, large ¥650) — a full hot meal for under ₹300. Conveyor-belt (kaiten) sushi runs ¥110-220 per 2-piece plate; ~8-10 plates (~¥1,500) fills you up. Konbini (7-Eleven, Lawson, FamilyMart) bento are ¥400-700 and onigiri rice balls ~¥150-200. Budget eaters manage on ¥1,000-1,500/day.
    - 💴 Gyudon regular ~¥450 (~₹265); kaiten sushi ¥110-220/plate (~₹65-130); konbini bento ¥400-700 (~₹235-410)
    - 💡 Supermarkets slash-price bento/sushi ~30-50% in the last 1-2 hours before closing — look for yellow 'discount' stickers for a cheap dinner.
    - 🇮🇳 Gyudon is beef and kaiten sushi is fish — not for vegetarians or those avoiding beef. Veg Indians should lean on konbini onigiri (pick vegetable/umeboshi fillings), tempura/veg sets, Indian restaurants, and Daiso/supermarket snacks; halal diners should seek out certified halal ramen/curry spots and check konbini labels.
- **Capsule & business hotels — cheapest private-ish beds** — Capsule hotels cost roughly ¥2,000-5,000/night (budget pods ¥2,500-4,500; nicer 'poshtels' ¥7,000-10,000) — a clean pod, shared bath, often with sauna. Business hotels (private room, private bath) like Toyoko Inn and APA run ~¥6,000-10,000/night for a single, with the wider market ¥7,000-15,000 depending on city and season. Weekends/Fridays add ~30-60% at capsules.
    - 💴 Capsule ~¥2,000-5,000/night (~₹1,180-2,950); business-hotel single ~¥6,000-10,000 (~₹3,540-5,900)
    - 💡 Book business hotels midweek and early — APA/Toyoko rates swing hard with demand. Toyoko Inn holds steadier prices across the week and often includes a free simple breakfast; capsules save most for solo travelers who are out sightseeing all day.
- **General money-saving strategies (incl. overnight buses & free sights)** — Biggest levers: (1) overnight highway buses beat trains AND save a night's hotel — Willer Express Tokyo↔Kyoto/Osaka from ~¥3,500 one-way; (2) skip the nationwide JR Pass unless the math (above) says yes — use IC card + city/regional passes; (3) load up on free attractions — Meiji Jingu, Senso-ji temple, Fushimi Inari shrine, and most shrine/temple grounds are free or a few hundred yen; (4) always do tax-free at ¥5,000+; (5) eat konbini/gyudon and supermarket end-of-day deals; (6) buy your Shinkansen legs with Klook/smartEX discounts.
    - 💴 Willer overnight bus Tokyo–Kyoto from ¥3,500 (~₹2,065) vs Shinkansen ~¥14,000 one-way — saves the fare AND a hotel night
    - 💡 Base yourself near a JR/subway hub to lean on your day passes, and cluster sightseeing by area so a single city day-pass covers the whole day.
    - 🇮🇳 Withdraw yen at 7-Eleven/Japan Post ATMs (accept foreign cards 24/7) rather than carrying lots of cash; tell your Indian bank you're travelling to avoid card blocks. At ¥100 ≈ ₹59 (late-July-2026 rate, ~0.59 INR/JPY), a ¥1,000 meal is ~₹590 — handy for quick mental math.
- **India-specific: Japan e-Visa since Sep 2025 (fee just ₹500 from Apr 2026)** — Major update for Indian passport holders: India was added to the Japan eVISA system from 1 September 2025. You can now apply for a single-entry tourist visa fully online through a registered agency — upload documents, pay, and receive a digital 'Visa Issuance Notice' shown on your phone at airline check-in and at Japanese immigration (no passport sticker for eligible tourist cases). Processing is generally around 5 working days but can stretch in peak season. From 2 March 2026, VFS walk-ins ended in Chennai, Kochi, Hyderabad, Puducherry and Bengaluru — you must book an online slot.
    - 💴 From 1 April 2026 the government visa fee for Indians is ₹500 for single AND multiple-entry tourist visas (plus any agency service fee)
    - 💡 Apply ~3-4 weeks before travel to buffer peak-season delays. Keep the digital Visa Issuance Notice accessible offline on your phone for check-in and immigration.
    - 🇮🇳 This is India-only news — the online e-Visa removes the old passport-sticker + mandatory VFS-visit hassle for most tourist applications; confirm eligibility on the official JAPAN eVISA website before booking flights.

_Sources: https://www.japan-guide.com/e/e2361.html · https://www.japan-guide.com/e/e2361_02.html · https://selfguidejapan.com/blog/japan-rail-pass-price-2026 · https://selfguidejapan.com/blog/is-jr-pass-worth-it-2026 · https://www.klook.com/blog/is-jr-pass-worth-it-after-price-increase/ · https://www.jrhokkaido.co.jp/global/english/ticket/railpass/index.html · https://watabi.org/en/articles/hokkaido-jr-pass-guide · https://www.jreast.co.jp/en/multi/pass/eastpass.html · https://japantravel.navitime.com/en/area/jp/guide/NTJjtg2602016-en/ · https://trulytokyo.com/how-to-get-a-suica-pasmo-ic-card/ · https://travel.rakuten.com/contents/usa/en-us/guide/suica-card/ · https://loyaltylobby.com/2025/02/20/update-japan-railways-suica-card-sales-will-resume-on-march-1-2025/_


### B12. Japan's stamp culture — a practical mini-guide (eki stamps, goshuin, stamp rallies, stamp books)
_Japan has three overlapping stamp traditions that make a trip feel like a treasure hunt, and they're one of the most rewarding (and cheapest) souvenirs an Indian traveler can bring home. (1) Eki stamps — free, self-serve ink stamps at almost every train station, each with a unique local design (a sumo wrestler at Ryogoku, Hiroshima's floating torii at Miyajimaguchi, a geisha at Gion-Shijo). Press them into any notebook; there's also a free digital version, the JR East EKITAG app, live at 1,235 stations. (2) Goshuin — hand-brushed calligraphy-plus-vermilion-seal stamps at Shinto shrines and Buddhist temples, done live by a priest for a ~300-500 yen (~175-295 INR) donation. These are SACRED, not a game: you buy a special accordion-fold book called a goshuincho (~1,000-3,000 yen / ~590-1,770 INR), pray first, carry exact change/cash only, and never mix them with tourist stamps. (3) Stamp rallies — organized collect-em-all events with prizes, the biggest being the JR East x Pokemon Mega Stamp Rally (runs mid-July to Aug 31 each year, incl. 2026) and the no-deadline 100 Famous Castles Stamp Rally. Crucially, the fun secular stamp rally and the sacred goshuin are two different worlds and must never be mixed. Exchange rate used throughout: ~1 JPY = 0.59 INR (100 yen ~ 59 INR), as of 24 July 2026 — rates move, so treat INR figures as approximate._

- **Eki stamps: the free souvenir hiding in every train station** — 'Eki' means station. Most Japanese train stations — especially JR (Japan Rail) ones — keep a rubber commemorative stamp (eki stamp) that you press yourself into a book. Each design is unique to that station and celebrates a local feature, so a stamp collection becomes a hand-pressed map of your entire journey. They're typically placed on a small table or counter, usually OUTSIDE the ticket gates or near the Midori-no-Madoguchi (green ticket office) or the station master's window. In huge Tokyo hubs (Shinjuku, Shibuya, Ikebukuro, Tokyo Station) they can be tucked away, so it's worth looking up the exact spot in advance or just asking staff.
    - 💴 100% free — no purchase or fee to stamp. You only pay if a stamp sits inside the paid gates and you need a platform/train ticket to reach it.
    - 💡 If you can't find it, ask a staff member 'Eki stamp wa doko desu ka?' Many stations also leave a stack of loose scrap paper by the pad if you have nothing to stamp into.
    - 🇮🇳 This is the single best zero-cost souvenir for a budget-conscious traveler from India — a full book of dozens of stamps costs nothing but the notebook. Great for kids and a lightweight, flat, checked-bag-safe keepsake.
- **How to actually collect an eki stamp (the little ritual)** — 1) Find the stamp and its ink pad. 2) Do a quick test press on a loose sheet first — many stations still use vintage wooden-handled stamps with worn, uneven pads that print faint, so a test press saves you a ruined page. 3) Press firmly and evenly into your book. 4) Note the station name and the date next to it — that turns a random ink blob into a dated travel record. Some newer stations have crisp self-inking stamps; older rural ones are charmingly hit-or-miss.
    - 💴 Free activity; only cost is your notebook.
    - 💡 Use a book with thick pages so ink doesn't bleed through to the next page. Carry it in your day bag every day — you never know which station will have one, and you can't go back easily.
- **Eki stamp designs worth going out of your way for** — The designs are the whole joy. Documented examples: Ryogoku Station (Tokyo) shows a sumo wrestler (it's Tokyo's sumo district); Shinjuku shows skyscrapers plus the local heirloom naito chili pepper; Gion-Shijo (Kyoto) shows a geisha; Miyajimaguchi (Hiroshima) shows the famous floating red torii gate; Kuki (Saitama) is lantern-shaped; and Nobeyama (Nagano) proudly marks itself as Japan's highest-altitude JR station. JR East has also run special commemorative designs, e.g. for the Tokaido Shinkansen's 60th anniversary.
    - 💡 If you're following a typical India-to-Japan itinerary (Tokyo–Kyoto–Osaka–Hiroshima), you can bag the sumo, geisha and floating-torii stamps without any detour — they're on stations you'll pass anyway.
- **EKITAG: the free digital eki-stamp app (no notebook needed)** — JR East launched EKITAG in 2023 — a free official app that collects eki stamps digitally. At a participating station you find the EKITAG logo and scan its RFID/NFC touchpoint (or QR) with your phone; the stamp drops into a digital 'book' in the app, and you can overlay stamps onto your travel photos. It now covers 1,235 stations nationwide, including tiny unmanned ones, and during special event periods you can unlock secret stamps and mementos.
    - 💴 The app is completely free; no charge per stamp.
    - 💡 Physical + digital aren't either/or — collect both. The app also nudges you toward small lines and stations you'd never otherwise visit.
    - 🇮🇳 App interface is in English and Japanese only. It's a great backup when a physical stamp is missing or the pad is dried out — and it means zero extra weight in your luggage. Download before you fly (available on Apple App Store and Google Play).
- **Goshuin: sacred calligraphy-and-seal stamps at shrines & temples** — A goshuin ('honorable red seal') is completely different from an eki stamp: it's hand-created for you on the spot at a Shinto shrine or Buddhist temple. A priest or attendant brushes the shrine/temple name in black calligraphy, adds the date and 'houhai' (to worship), and presses one or more vermilion (red) seals over it. It's considered proof of your visit and a token of connection with the enshrined deity or Buddha — a small work of art, and a spiritual record, not a collectible. You receive it at the shrine/temple office (juyosho/shuinjo), usually near where amulets (omamori) are sold.
    - 💡 Famous, beginner-friendly places to get one include Sensoji in Asakusa and Meiji Jingu in Tokyo, and Kiyomizu-dera, Yasaka-jinja and Chion-in in Kyoto. Imado-jinja (Tokyo) has cute cat motifs.
- **The goshuincho: the special accordion book you MUST buy first** — You cannot collect goshuin in a random notebook — you need a goshuincho, a dedicated book with thick, accordion-folded (concertina) washi pages that open out in one long strip and absorb the wet ink and calligraphy without bleeding. Staff will politely turn you away (and it reads as not understanding the point) if you hand over anything else. Buy the book BEFORE you ask for your first stamp.
    - 💴 Goshuincho typically cost 1,000-2,000 yen (~590-1,180 INR) at shrines/temples; stationery-shop and designer versions run ~1,000-3,000 yen (~590-1,770 INR), and elaborate carved-wood-cover ones ~3,500-6,000 yen (~2,065-3,540 INR).
    - 💡 Buy your first goshuincho AT a major shrine or temple — many sell their own beautiful cover design, so the book itself becomes a souvenir of where you started.
    - 🇮🇳 At ~590-1,180 INR the book is a one-time buy that lasts a whole trip (and beyond); pack it flat between clothes on the way home so pages don't crease.
- **Goshuin cost, and the exact-change / cash-only rule** — A standard goshuin asks for a donation of about 300-500 yen (~175-295 INR) each; special or limited-edition seasonal ones (New Year, cherry blossom, autumn) can be 500-1,000 yen (~295-590 INR) or more. Payment is CASH ONLY — no cards, no Apple Pay — and you're culturally expected to have near-exact change. Shrines carry little change, so don't try to pay a 300-yen goshuin with a 5,000 or 10,000 yen note.
    - 💴 ~300-500 yen / ~175-295 INR standard; ~500-1,000 yen / ~295-590 INR for limited editions.
    - 💡 Some places give you the goshuin on a loose pre-written sheet (kakioki) that you glue into your book later — handy when a priest isn't available.
    - 🇮🇳 Carry a coin purse of 100-yen coins and 500-yen coins. As an Indian traveler used to UPI/cards everywhere, this is the one place you truly need physical cash and small change — withdraw yen from a 7-Eleven or Japan Post ATM (they reliably accept Indian Visa/Mastercard/RuPay-International) and deliberately keep the coins.
- **Goshuin etiquette: it is NOT a stamp rally (the one rule that matters most)** — This is the etiquette that trips up tourists. Goshuin are sacred — the strict separation of the sacred and the mundane is a cultural absolute in Japan. So: (1) Pray/pay respects at the main hall FIRST, then go to the goshuin window — the stamp is proof of worship, not an entry souvenir. (2) Never mix sacred and secular in the same book: putting eki stamps, tourist stamps, or a cartoon train mascot next to hand-brushed sacred calligraphy is considered deeply offensive. (3) Some stricter Buddhist temples won't stamp a book that already has Shinto shrine seals, and vice-versa — if you'll visit a lot of both, carry two goshuincho (one for shrines, one for temples). (4) Behave quietly and don't photograph the priest writing without asking. In short: don't collect goshuin at all if you're not going to respect what they are.
    - 💡 Keep your eki-stamp/stamp-rally notebook and your goshuincho as two totally separate books — physically and mentally. That single habit keeps you on the right side of every rule above.
- **Where to buy a beautiful goshuincho (real shops)** — Best options: (1) At the shrine/temple itself — often the most meaningful, with a location-exclusive cover. (2) Kyukyodo — a revered traditional paper/incense/calligraphy house founded in 1663, with Tokyo branches at Ginza, Marunouchi, Shibuya, Shinjuku and Ikebukuro; a go-to for elegant washi-covered books. (3) In Kyoto, the shops at the eastern end inside Nishiki Market have a great selection, and Kyoto craftsmen make handmade goshuincho with hand-dyed Yuzen washi covers and double-washi pages. (4) Big lifestyle/stationery stores — Tokyu Hands and Loft — carry many designs (just confirm it's an accordion-fold goshuincho, not a flat notebook). Online, Etsy and specialty Japanese stationery stores ship handmade washi ones.
    - 💴 Temple/shrine books ~1,000-2,000 yen (~590-1,180 INR); stationery-store/designer ~up to 3,000 yen (~1,770 INR); handmade Yuzen-washi and carved-wood-cover ~3,500-6,000 yen (~2,065-3,540 INR).
    - 💡 Tell it apart from a plain notebook by the pages: a real goshuincho unfolds like an accordion. A hand-dyed Yuzen-washi cover makes a gorgeous gift to bring back.
- **Stamp rallies: the organized 'collect-em-all' events (with prizes)** — A stamp rally (sutanpu rari) is an organized hunt where you collect a set of themed stamps and redeem completed sheets for prizes. They pop up at train stations, airports (Haneda has multiple per terminal), castles, museums, zoos, aquariums, and landmarks like Tokyo Tower and Tokyo Skytree. Recurring favorites include the JR Pokemon summer rally and seasonal park rallies (e.g. a Tokyo Parks autumn rally). A handy dedicated rally book is the Sanrio Characters Stamp Book (~1,650 yen / ~975 INR), an A6 80-page book with Hello Kitty/My Melody/Cinnamoroll and built-in blotting paper — sold on Amazon Japan, Rakuten and Kinokuniya.
    - 💴 Most rally stamps are free to press; you may pay transport or an attraction's admission to reach some. Sanrio stamp book ~1,650 yen (~975 INR).
    - 🇮🇳 A brilliant, low-cost way to keep kids engaged across a long Japan itinerary — turns train changes and park visits into a game with tangible rewards.
- **JR East x Pokemon Mega Stamp Rally (the big summer event — incl. 2026)** — The most famous rally for families. It runs roughly mid-July to 31 August each year across Tokyo. In 2026 it runs 16 July–31 August; the 2025 edition ran 18 July–31 August with stamps at 36 Tokyo-area stations (35 JR East + 1 Tokyo Monorail) plus 7 Shinkansen stations (Shin-Aomori, Morioka, Sendai, Akita, Yamagata, Niigata, Nagano). You grab a FREE stamp book at major JR stations, pick a course — a 6-station course (any 6 of the 36), a 9-station set course, the full 36-station course, or a Shinkansen course — and collect. Completing tiers wins Pokemon prizes: past years gave a Jumbo Pikachu ex card, special picks/pins, clear cases, neck straps, keychains and commemorative medals. Most stamps sit outside the ticket gates, so you pay normal train fares to reach them.
    - 💴 Basic stamp book is FREE. An optional Special Edition Stamp Book (~2,420 yen / ~1,430 INR) with a Pokemon pass case is sold in limited stock at select NewDays station convenience stores.
    - 💡 Prizes and free stamp books run out — go early in the period and early in the day. Note: a LINE-app digital coupon may be required to claim some prizes (an exemption applies for young children). Prize redemption usually closes ~1 Sept.
    - 🇮🇳 Perfectly timed for Indian families traveling in the summer school break (May-July). Pokemon merch that's a free reward here would cost a fortune as imported merchandise back home.
- **100 Famous Castles Stamp Rally (for history buffs, no deadline)** — Run by the Japan Castle Foundation since 2007, this rally has you collect stamps at 100 castles it selected as Japan's most significant (Himeji, Matsumoto, Nagoya, Osaka, etc.). The stamps are prized for bold, historical designs. To join you use the official stamp book that comes bundled with the official guidebook (the Funakiya travel-stamp resource also tracks the 100-castle list). There's no deadline, so you collect at your own pace over many trips; some castles charge admission to reach the stamp, and stamp spots can move for weather/events.
    - 💴 Stamp pressing is free; budget for each castle's entry fee (commonly a few hundred yen to ~1,000 yen, i.e. up to ~590 INR).
    - 💡 If you're only in Japan once, don't chase all 100 — just grab the castle stamps where you already are (e.g. Himeji on a Kyoto/Osaka trip, Matsumoto on a Nagano trip).
- **Passport-style stamp books & where to buy one cheaply** — For eki stamps and rallies (NOT goshuin), the classic choice is a passport-sized book (~the size of a passport, ~50 thick pages, or 4x6 inch). Cheapest and most authentic: Kinokuniya bookstores stock proper eki-stamp notebooks in the travel section for about 825 yen (~490 INR), with covers per train company (plus a highway service-area version). Other great picks: Traveler's Company (Traveler's Factory shops) sells a dedicated stamp notebook; and slim passport-size notebooks from Muji or Midori fit in a pocket. Station shops sometimes sell their own, and many stations offer free loose paper or pretty stamp cards. English-language passport-size eki-stamp books are also sold on Amazon if you want to buy before you fly.
    - 💴 Kinokuniya eki-stamp notebook ~825 yen (~490 INR); Muji/Midori passport notebooks similar range; loose station paper free.
    - 💡 Pick thick pages (ink bleed-through is the #1 regret). Keep this book strictly for eki/tourist/rally stamps and your goshuincho strictly for shrines and temples — never one book for both.
    - 🇮🇳 Buy the notebook on arrival in Japan (Kinokuniya, Tokyu Hands, Loft, or airport shops) rather than importing — it's cheaper there and you get authentic train-company designs.
- **India-specific practical wrap-up: cash, currency, timing** — Key numbers: at ~1 JPY = 0.59 INR (100 yen ~ 59 INR, as of 24 July 2026 — rates fluctuate, so verify before you travel), a full eki-stamp trip costs essentially nothing but a ~490 INR notebook, while a goshuin habit costs ~590-1,180 INR for the book plus ~175-295 INR per stamp. Bring/withdraw yen CASH and hoard 100- and 500-yen coins specifically for goshuin (cash-only, exact change). Download the free EKITAG app before departure. The golden rule to remember: eki stamps and stamp rallies are playful and secular; goshuin are sacred — pray first, cash only, and never let the two share a book.
    - 💴 Rough trip budget for stamps: notebook ~490 INR + goshuincho ~590-1,180 INR + goshuin ~175-295 INR each; eki/rally stamps free. Pokemon rally basic book free.
    - 💡 Carry two books, a coin purse, and a fine-tip pen (to date each stamp). Pack both books flat on the way home to protect the calligraphy and ink.
    - 🇮🇳 7-Eleven and Japan Post ATMs reliably accept Indian Visa/Mastercard for yen withdrawals; get cash on arrival at the airport for immediate coins. Verify the JPY-INR rate near your travel date — it moves often.

_Sources: https://www.nationalgeographic.com/travel/article/practical-guide-to-collecting-japans-eki-train-station-stamps · https://www.jrailpass.com/blog/eki-train-stamps · https://www.japan.travel/en/blog/collecting-goshuin-shrine-and-temple-stamps/ · https://matcha-jp.com/en/10460 · https://www.jeepe.jp/en/articles/goshuin-how-to-get-1502 · https://www.machiya-inn-japan.com/blog/goshuincho-guide/ · https://www.fun-japan.jp/en/articles/14112 · https://matcha-jp.com/en/4445 · https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/100_Fine_Castles_of_Japan · https://stamp.funakiya.com/en/japan-100castles.html · https://soranews24.com/2026/07/07/tokyos-giant-pokemon-stamp-rally-is-back-for-2026-with-exclusive-prizes-up-for-grabs/ · https://livejapan.com/en/article-a0006053/_


### B13. Anime & character attractions in Japan (Ghibli, Pokémon, Sanrio, Nintendo, Harry Potter) — and why Indian families must book months ahead
_This is the missing "character layer" of a Japan trip that pulls Indian families and anime/manga/Pokémon fans just as hard as teamLab or DisneySea. The catch that makes it blog-worthy: almost every one of these sells only date-and-time-stamped tickets on a strict advance calendar, and the marquee ones (Studio Ghibli Museum in Mitaka, Ghibli Park near Nagoya, the Nintendo Museum in Kyoto, the Pokémon Café) vanish within minutes or run a monthly lottery. You cannot walk up. For an Indian traveller this means the booking has to happen from India, weeks-to-months before the flight, with alarms set in IST (Japan is 3.5 hrs ahead) and an international card ready — the single biggest planning trap after the JR Pass. Prices below are official 2026 figures; INR is approximate at ~₹0.59 per ¥1 (100 yen ≈ ₹59, July 2026). Order of urgency: Ghibli Museum and Ghibli Park weekends, Nintendo Museum lottery, and the Pokémon Café are the ones that "sell out in minutes." Sanrio Puroland and Warner Bros Harry Potter are easier but still cheaper and safer booked online in advance._

- **Studio Ghibli Museum (Mitaka, Tokyo) — the hardest ticket in Japan, sold on the 10th at 10am JST** — The intimate Miyazaki museum in the Mitaka/Inokashira Park area of west Tokyo. Admission is by advance, date-and-time-stamped ticket ONLY (entry slots 10:00, 12:00, 14:00, 16:00) — nothing is ever sold at the door. Overseas visitors buy through the official Lawson Ticket English site (l-tike.com/st1/ghibli-en). It is first-come-first-served (not a lottery), and popular dates and weekends sell out within minutes — guides warn that being even 60 seconds late can mean missing out. This is separate from Ghibli PARK; fans often do both.
    - 💴 Official 2026 prices: adult 19+ ¥1,000 (~₹590); age 13–18 ¥700 (~₹415); age 7–12 ¥400 (~₹235); age 4–6 ¥100 (~₹60); under 3 free. No 'skip-the-line' resale allowed.
    - 💡 Have 2–3 backup date/time combos ready, log in before 10:00 JST, and don't refresh wildly — the site freezes at peak. If the 10th sells out, cancellations occasionally reappear. Combine with Inokashira Park and the nearby Mitaka/Kichijoji cafes.
    - 🇮🇳 Tickets go on sale at 10:00 Japan time on the 10th of the month, for the FOLLOWING month — that is 06:30 IST on the 10th, so an Indian traveller must set an early-morning alarm and book from home months before flying. The ticket is name-locked: carry the exact passport whose name is on the booking, as you exchange the voucher for the real ticket at the museum. The Lawson interface is largely Japanese, so have dates, times and an international (Visa/Mastercard) card pre-loaded before the clock strikes.
- **Ghibli Park (Nagakute, near Nagoya) — book 2 months ahead via Klook; weekends gone in minutes** — The full open-air Ghibli theme park in Aichi (reach it via Nagoya on the Shinkansen), with five areas: Grand Warehouse, Hill of Youth, Dondoko Forest, Mononoke Village and Valley of Witches (Howl's moving castle, the witch's house). Every ticket is date-specific and must be bought in advance — none sold at the gate. Overseas visitors book through Klook or Lawson Ticket. A new 'Satoyama Pass' bundling Dondoko Forest/Mononoke Village/Witch Valley launches July 2026.
    - 💴 Official 2026: O-Sanpo Pass PREMIUM (all 5 areas + big buildings) ¥7,300 weekday / ¥7,800 weekend-holiday adult (~₹4,300–4,600), child 4–12 ¥3,650/¥3,900. O-Sanpo Pass STANDARD (park + gardens, cheaper) ¥3,300 weekday / ¥3,800 weekend adult (~₹1,950–2,240), child ¥1,650/¥1,900. Under 3 free. Individual buildings: Howl's Castle ¥1,000/500.
    - 💡 If a Premium weekend is gone, grab a weekday, or take the cheaper Standard pass and add individual-building tickets. Budget a full day and the Nagoya Shinkansen leg into the itinerary.
    - 🇮🇳 Tickets go on sale 2 MONTHS in advance, on the 10th at 14:00 Japan time (= 10:30 IST) — e.g. October dates open August 10. Weekends and Japanese holidays sell out in minutes, so an Indian family syncing the trip around school holidays should book the moment the window opens, from India. Klook (which accepts Indian cards and has an India app) is the easiest overseas channel vs the Japanese Lawson Loppi machines.
- **Nintendo Museum (Uji, Kyoto) — free monthly LOTTERY, apply 3 months out with a Nintendo Account** — The Nintendo Museum that opened in Uji, Kyoto is a fans' pilgrimage (retro consoles, giant hands-on exhibits) and is entirely lottery-gated — you cannot just buy a ticket. You need a free Nintendo Account, then enter the official lottery at museum-tickets.nintendo.com choosing up to three date/time slots. This is distinct from Super Nintendo World at Universal Studios Japan (Osaka), which is inside the USJ park many itineraries already cover.
    - 💴 Official: adult 18+ ¥3,300 (~₹1,950); youth 12–17 ¥2,200 (~₹1,300); child 6–11 ¥1,100 (~₹650); under 5 free. Entering the lottery itself is free.
    - 💡 Pick 3 different slots to raise your odds. Uji is a short train ride from Kyoto/Osaka, so pair it with Kyoto sightseeing. Losers can still get their Nintendo fix at Super Nintendo World in USJ, Osaka.
    - 🇮🇳 Applications open ~3 months before the visit month and the draw is on the 1st of the preceding month; results land in your Nintendo Account. For an Indian trip this means creating the account and entering the lottery well before you book non-refundable flights — ideally have flexible museum dates. If you don't win, last-minute cancellation tickets (up to ~2 months ahead) sometimes appear for instant purchase, so keep checking.
- **Pokémon Centers (4 in Tokyo) + Pokémon Café — the Café books 31 days ahead at 6pm JST and vanishes in minutes** — Pokémon Centers are free-entry official megastores. Central Tokyo has four: Shibuya (Shibuya PARCO 6F), Tokyo DX (Nihonbashi Takashimaya, attached to the Café), Skytree Town (Solamachi 4F), and Mega Tokyo in Ikebukuro's Sunshine City — note Mega Tokyo has been temporarily CLOSED since March 26, 2026 (check before going). The Pokémon Café is a themed sit-down restaurant, reservation-only, in Nihonbashi (Tokyo) and DAIMARU Shinsaibashi (Osaka).
    - 💴 Pokémon Centers: free to enter, pay for merch. Pokémon Café: no cover charge but every guest aged 6+ must order at least 1 item (dishes/drinks roughly ¥1,500–2,300, ~₹885–1,360); merch capped at 3 per special item.
    - 💡 If the Café is fully booked, watch for weekday cancellations the day before. For pure shopping, skip the Café entirely — the Centers need no reservation. Skytree Town Center pairs neatly with the Skytree observation deck.
    - 🇮🇳 Café reservations open exactly 31 days in advance at 18:00 Japan time (= 14:30 IST) on the official site only (no phone/email) — for up to 6 people, and slots typically go in the first 1–2 minutes. An Indian traveller should count 31 days back from each Tokyo/Osaka day and set an afternoon-IST alarm. Booking is via the official English site; have an international card ready. Vegetarian Indian families should note the Café menu is character-themed and limited for pure-veg diners — the free-entry Pokémon Centers are the safer 'fan hit' for kids.
- **Sanrio Puroland (Tama, Tokyo) — Hello Kitty indoor park; cheaper and calmer if booked online** — The all-indoor Hello Kitty / My Melody / Cinnamoroll theme park in Tama, west Tokyo — a big draw for Indian families with younger kids and Sanrio fans, and rain-proof. Uses a variable 'passport' price that changes by date/season (higher on weekends, Golden Week, Obon). Tickets are sold day-of at the counter, but online advance passports are cheaper and guarantee entry on busy dates.
    - 💴 Official direct 2026 ~¥5,300 weekday / ¥5,600 weekend adult (18+) (~₹3,130–3,300); child 3–17 ¥3,400/¥3,800 (~₹2,000–2,240). Discount platforms (Klook/KKday/Rakuten) sell 1-Day Passports well below gate price — around ¥3,600 (~₹2,125) and pair/afternoon deals up to ~47% off onsite price; a ¥200 park coupon is floating around (valid to 31 Mar 2027).
    - 💡 Go on a weekday for lower price and shorter show queues; time your visit around the parade/show schedule. Afternoon-only passports cut the cost further if you arrive after 14:00.
    - 🇮🇳 English-language booking via Klook/KKday/Trip.com/Rakuten works well with Indian cards, so pre-book from India to lock the cheaper price and skip counter queues. Being fully indoor, it's a reliable monsoon/heat backup day for families. Check the official price calendar for your exact date before buying.
- **Warner Bros. Studio Tour Tokyo — The Making of Harry Potter (Nerima) — date/time-stamped, book ahead** — The world's largest Harry Potter indoor attraction, on the former Toshimaen site in Nerima, north-west Tokyo (~half a day). Every ticket is for a specific date AND entry time, and popular slots/weekends sell out, so advance online booking is strongly recommended. A ticket-price update applies to visits from 1 July 2026, and prices vary by date.
    - 💴 Official 2026 standard: adult ¥6,600~ (~₹3,900); junior ¥5,500~ (~₹3,250); child ¥4,000~ (~₹2,360). Packages (with photo/guidebook) from ¥10,080 adult; Afternoon Tea from ¥14,000; Evening High Tea from ¥16,500 (books up to 72 hrs before); a student discount ticket at ¥5,000 runs 17 Jul–30 Sep 2026 (excl. 8–16 Aug).
    - 💡 Choose an early or late slot to dodge crowds, allow 3.5–4 hours minimum, and pre-book the butterbeer/Great Hall experience. The Wand Workshop and dining packages must be reserved with the ticket, not on arrival.
    - 🇮🇳 Book the specific date/time slot online from India before you fly — walk-up is not the model here. Reachable by train to Toshimaen; budget a half-day. It's a strong all-weather pick for Potterhead teens travelling from India, and prices are fixed per slot so you know the cost upfront.
- **The one thing to tell every Indian reader: build a 'booking calendar' before you fly** — The through-line for this whole anime/character layer is that the hard part isn't being in Japan — it's securing tickets from India in advance. Japan Standard Time is 3.5 hours ahead of IST. Convert every on-sale moment to IST and put it in your phone as a recurring reminder: Ghibli Museum = 10th at 06:30 IST (next month); Ghibli Park = 10th at 10:30 IST (2 months out); Pokémon Café = 31 days before at 14:30 IST; Nintendo Museum = lottery, apply ~3 months out (draw on the 1st). Sanrio Puroland and Warner Bros Harry Potter aren't time-critical but are cheaper and safer pre-booked.
    - 💴 No extra cost to plan — but missing the window means either paying resellers or missing the attraction entirely. Klook/KKday often beat gate prices for Sanrio and offer overseas-friendly Ghibli Park access.
    - 💡 Sequence your booking alarms in the order the windows open, keep 2–3 backup dates per attraction, and treat the Ghibli Museum/Park and Nintendo Museum lottery as the non-negotiable 'book first' items. Everything else can flex around them.
    - 🇮🇳 Use an international-enabled Visa/Mastercard (many Japanese ticket portals reject Indian domestic-only RuPay/cards, and some Lawson pages are Japanese-only). Klook has an India presence and an app, making it the friendliest channel for Indian travellers on Ghibli Park and Sanrio. Do all of this BEFORE booking non-refundable India–Japan flights around fixed dates, and keep passports handy since Ghibli Museum name-checks the ticket.

_Sources: https://www.ghibli-museum.jp/en/tickets/ · https://en.japantravel.com/tokyo/ghibli-museum-how-to-get-tickets-2026-guide/71834 · https://trulytokyo.com/how-to-get-ghibli-museum-tickets/ · https://ghibli-park.jp/en/ticket/ · https://www.wbstudiotour.jp/en/tickets/ · https://www.wbstudiotour.jp/en/news_20251120/ · https://en.puroland.jp/language/ · https://thirdeyetraveller.com/sanrio-puroland-hello-kitty-theme-park/ · https://museum.nintendo.com/en/index.html · https://www.klook.com/en-US/blog/nintendo-museum-kyoto-how-to-visit/ · https://japancheapo.com/entertainment/nintendo-museum-kyoto/ · https://www.pokemon-cafe.jp/en/cafe/faq/_


### B14. Uniquely-Japanese experiences for first-timers: sumo, karaoke, arcades/purikura, character & animal cafes, street go-karting, high-tech toilets/vending machines
_Japan's "only-in-Japan" experiential layer is the biggest delight-per-yen win for first-time Indian visitors and is largely bookable in advance. The anchor is grand sumo: six 15-day honbasho tournaments run in 2026 (Tokyo Jan 11-25, Osaka Mar 8-22, Tokyo May 10-24, Nagoya Jul 12-26 at the brand-new IG Arena, Tokyo Sep 13-27, Fukuoka Nov 8-22), with tickets from ~¥3,500 (arena chair, ≈₹2,065) up to ¥20,000 ringside (≈₹11,800) and box seats ¥34,000-60,000 for four; buy on the official sumo.pia.jp ~1 month ahead. Beyond the ring: all-night "free-time" karaoke (Manekineko/Big Echo/JOYSOUND, ¥900-2,100 pp, ≈₹530-1,240) with big English catalogues; Akihabara arcades and purikura sticker-photo booths (¥100-200 per play, purikura ¥400-500 per session but effectively girls-or-mixed-groups-only); reservation-only character cafes (Pokemon Cafe Nihonbashi/Osaka, Kirby Cafe at Tokyo Skytree — books out in seconds) and walk-in animal cafes (Cat Cafe MoCHA ¥1,188/30 min, Akiba Fukurou owl cafe ~¥2,000, hedgehog and capybara cafes); street go-karting in costume with Street Kart (¥7,500-25,000) past Rainbow Bridge and Tokyo Tower; and the everyday novelties of TOTO washlet toilets and ubiquitous vending machines. Exchange reference used throughout: 1 JPY ≈ ₹0.59 (July 2026). A crucial India-specific unlock: India is a 1949 Geneva Convention signatory, so an Indian IDP from your RTO legally lets you drive the go-karts._

- **Grand sumo (honbasho): the exact 2026 schedule across four cities** — Six 15-day grand tournaments run in 2026. Confirmed dates: January (Hatsu) Tokyo, Ryogoku Kokugikan, Jan 11-25; March (Haru) Osaka, EDION Arena Osaka, Mar 8-22; May (Natsu) Tokyo, Ryogoku Kokugikan, May 10-24; July (Nagoya) at the brand-new IG Arena Nagoya, Jul 12-26 (its inaugural sumo); September (Aki) Tokyo, Ryogoku Kokugikan, Sep 13-27; November (Kyushu) Fukuoka Kokusai Center, Nov 8-22. Wrestlers fight one bout each per day; the top-division (makuuchi) bouts and the ring-entering ceremony run in the late afternoon (~15:30 onward), building to the last bout around 18:00. Tokyo's Ryogoku Kokugikan is the most iconic venue and sits beside the Sumo Museum.
    - 💴 Attendance is a full-day event; entry priced separately (see ticket item). No charge to walk the Ryogoku sumo district.
    - 💡 Tokyo has three of the six tournaments (Jan/May/Sep), so a Tokyo-based itinerary in those months can catch sumo without a detour. If your trip misses all six, a stable morning-practice tour or the Sumo Museum is the fallback.
    - 🇮🇳 The January tournament overlaps the popular India winter-holiday travel window; a Tokyo trip in early-mid Jan can slot sumo in with zero extra internal travel.
- **Sumo tickets: seat types, real prices and how to buy from India** — Three seating tiers at Ryogoku Kokugikan. Arena/balcony chair seats (2nd floor, Western-style, graded SS/A/B/C) are the tourist-friendly budget option at ¥3,500-¥11,000 depending on grade and weekday/weekend. Masu box seats (1st-floor Japanese-style cushion boxes seating up to 4, graded A/B/C) are sold per whole box: ¥34,000-¥56,000 weekday and ¥38,000-¥60,000 weekend (you pay for the box regardless of how many sit in it). Tamari (ringside floor cushions, age 16+) are the priciest and hardest to get at ~¥20,000 per person, with a real risk of a 150kg wrestler landing in your lap. Tickets go on sale about one month before each tournament and often sell out, especially weekends and the final days.
    - 💴 Arena chair from ¥3,500 (≈₹2,065); ringside ¥20,000 (≈₹11,800); box of four ¥34,000-60,000 (≈₹20,000-35,400 total, i.e. ≈₹5,000-8,850 per person for four). Official sumo.pia.jp is cheapest; Viator/Klook/GetYourGuide bundle guide+meal at a premium.
    - 💡 Book on the official English portal sumo.pia.jp the day tickets open (~1 month prior) with an international credit card; collect at the venue's self-service machine or any Seven-Eleven. A box seat is best value only if you're a group of 3-4 who'll share it.
    - 🇮🇳 Indian Visa/Mastercard credit cards work on sumo.pia.jp; buy before you fly since popular days sell out. A group of four Indian friends splitting one masu box lands sumo at ~₹5,000-8,850 each, comparable to a premium cricket ticket back home.
- **Watching morning stable (heya) practice instead of a tournament** — There are ~40 sumo stables, mostly around Tokyo's Ryogoku district, where wrestlers do keiko (morning practice) that outsiders can occasionally watch. Stables are private and demand strict silence, seiza-style floor sitting for 2-3 hours, and a Japanese-fluent host who knows the etiquette — so independent visits are effectively off-limits to tourists. The practical route is an organized small-group tour run by Klook, Rakuten or BuySumoTickets.
    - 💴 Guided stable-visit tours run around ¥10,000 (≈₹5,900) for the first person and ~¥4,000 (≈₹2,360) per additional group member.
    - 💡 This is the year-round substitute when no tournament is on. Expect early starts (often 07:00-09:00), no talking, no flash, and no getting up mid-session; dress modestly.
    - 🇮🇳 For an Indian family travelling outside the six tournament windows, a stable tour is the only reliable way to still see live sumo — book the English-language slot in advance.
- **All-night karaoke: the private-room 'free-time' ritual** — Japanese karaoke is done in your own private box (not a public bar), billed per person by time band, with a tablet to queue songs and a phone to order food/drinks. Big chains: Manekineko (630+ branches, lets you bring outside food), Big Echo (~500 branches, uses the DAM system with 340,000+ songs and JP/EN/CN/KR menus), and JOYSOUND (368,000+ songs, largest catalogue, strong foreign-language selection). Rates are cheapest in the daytime band and 10-30% higher on weekends. 'Free time' = unlimited hours within a band; overnight 'gofuri'/all-night plans (e.g. a fixed price for 17:00-05:00) let you sing till the trains restart.
    - 💴 Manekineko daytime free-time ¥900-1,100 pp (≈₹530-650), evening ¥2,100 pp (≈₹1,240); Big Echo daytime free-time ¥1,200-1,800 pp; JOYSOUND member daytime ¥550-880 pp; all chains require a min. 1-drink order per person (¥420+, ≈₹250). Nomihodai (all-you-can-drink) add-ons available.
    - 💡 Search the tablet in English/romaji; Bollywood and English pop are widely stocked on DAM/JOYSOUND. Go in the daytime band for the lowest rate, or take an overnight plan as a cheap 'hotel-alternative' if you miss the last train.
    - 🇮🇳 A private room is ideal for Indian families/groups who want a fun night without a bar/alcohol scene — you can order just soft drinks (the ¥420 drink minimum is satisfied by a Coke), and Manekineko even lets you bring your own snacks.
- **Akihabara arcades and UFO-catcher crane games** — Tokyo's Akihabara is the arcade heartland. Major chains: Round1 (120+ giant venues, 6-10 floors mixing arcade, bowling, karaoke, billiards), GiGO (formerly SEGA, strong on rhythm and fighting games; late-2025 its main Akihabara buildings are #2/#3/#5), Taito Station (170 branches, often inside train stations), and Namco (anime/figure crane prizes). Games run ¥100-200 per play; UFO-catcher (claw) machines ¥100-200 per attempt with anime figures and plush as prizes. Beginner-friendly picks: Taiko no Tatsujin (drum rhythm game) and photo-worthy claw machines.
    - 💴 ¥100-200 per play/attempt; budget ¥3,000-5,000 (≈₹1,770-2,950) for a fun 2-hour mixed visit. IC cards (Suica/PASMO) or chain prepaid cards sometimes give bonus credits.
    - 💡 Set a per-machine cap (¥500-1,000) for claw games — they're deliberately hard. Weekday mornings have the shortest waits for popular machines.
    - 🇮🇳 Arcades are heavily coin/IC-card driven, so load a Suica/PASMO (or carry ¥100 coins) rather than relying on the cards Indians usually use; UPI and foreign cards don't work in the machines.
- **Purikura: Japan's beautifying photo-sticker booths (and the girls-only rule)** — Purikura are photo booths that shoot you against backdrops, auto-apply beautifying filters (enlarged eyes, smoothed/whitened skin, longer legs), let you doodle with digital pens and stamps, and print the result as glossy stickers plus a QR download. Taito Station Akihabara has a bank of them in the basement (open 10:00-23:30); many booths also lend free costumes/props. Critical access rule: solo men and all-male groups are generally barred from purikura areas (a policy to prevent voyeurism); mixed-gender groups with at least one woman are almost always allowed.
    - 💴 ¥400-500 per session (≈₹235-295) for the whole group regardless of headcount — so it's cheapest split among friends.
    - 💡 Go with at least one woman in the group or you may be turned away. The filters skew very 'kawaii'; it's part of the fun, not a malfunction.
    - 🇮🇳 For groups of Indian male friends travelling together, plan to do purikura only when a female friend/family member is with you — otherwise you'll be refused entry to the booth zone.
- **Character cafes: Pokemon Cafe and Kirby Cafe (reservation-only)** — Two flagship IP cafes. Pokemon Cafe: reservation-only, at Nihonbashi Takashimaya (5F) in Tokyo (10:30-21:00) and DAIMARU Shinsaibashi (9F) in Osaka (10:00-20:00); themed Pikachu/Eevee/Snorlax dishes, a live character show, and exclusive merch. Kirby Cafe: at Tokyo Skytree/Solamachi, Osaka, and Canal City Hakata (Fukuoka); heavily themed interiors (Tokyo has a giant Whispy Woods installation) and its own gift shop. Both require advance online booking that sells out fast.
    - 💴 Pokemon Cafe: food from ¥2,530, sweets from ¥2,090, drinks from ¥1,375 (≈₹810); expect ~¥4,000-6,000 pp (≈₹2,360-3,540) with merch. Kirby Cafe: dishes ¥1,580-2,180; a sample meal of three small dishes + coffee came to ¥6,589 + tax (≈₹3,900).
    - 💡 Pokemon Cafe reservations open roughly 5-6 weeks ahead; Kirby Cafe reservations drop on the 10th of each month at 18:00 JST for the following month and sell out within seconds — be logged in and ready. Bring the passport of the person who booked, as ID is checked on arrival.
    - 🇮🇳 Set a phone alarm for the exact JST reservation window before you leave India (India is 3.5 hrs behind Japan, so 18:00 JST = 14:30 IST). Food is portioned small and themed; strict vegetarians should scan the online menu first, as many dishes are meat/seafood-based, though drinks and desserts are safe.
- **Animal cafes: cats, owls, hedgehogs, capybaras** — Tokyo pioneered animal cafes. Cat Cafe MoCHA Harajuku (Jingumae 1-14-25, Shibuya): walk-in, unlimited self-serve soft drinks, best around feeding times (10:30 and 19:30). Akiba Fukurou (owl cafe, Akihabara): reservation-only sessions where you hold owls among 30+ rescue birds; no food served. Hedgehog Cafe HARRY Harajuku (Jingumae 1-13-21, on Takeshita Street): 30-min sessions holding hedgehogs with provided gloves. cafe capyba Asakusa: timed sessions with real capybaras. These are novelty encounters, not full meals.
    - 💴 Cat Cafe MoCHA ¥1,188 / 30 min weekday (≈₹700), incl. unlimited soft drinks; Akiba Fukurou ~¥2,000 per session (≈₹1,180). Hedgehog/capybara cafes charge timed-session fees (varies).
    - 💡 Book owl cafes well ahead (reservation-only); cat cafes like MoCHA are walk-in. Visit weekday mornings for calmer animals and shorter queues. Be aware some travellers raise animal-welfare concerns about owl/hedgehog cafes — reputable ones (e.g. Akiba Fukurou uses rescue birds) are the better choice.
    - 🇮🇳 These are gentle, low-commitment, family-friendly stops good for kids; no dietary issues since you're mostly holding animals and sipping soft drinks.
- **Street go-karting in costume (Street Kart, formerly MariCar)** — Drive a low-slung go-kart on real Tokyo public roads in a superhero/character costume, in a guided convoy. Street Kart operates in Tokyo (Shinagawa, Akihabara, Shibuya, Tokyo Bay, Asakusa), Osaka and Okinawa. The Tokyo Bay route runs past the Rainbow Bridge and toward Tokyo Tower; tours last ~1.5-2 hours, with costumes included and a 4K action-camera rental option. Crucially, because you're on public streets you MUST show a valid driving credential — an International Driving Permit under the 1949 Geneva Convention (plus your passport and home licence). Turn up without the original documents and you're refused with no refund.
    - 💴 ¥7,500-¥25,000 per person (≈₹4,400-14,750) depending on time slot, course and season (August is cheapest); early 'review price' bookings are discounted. Optional full-insurance upgrade over the standard ¥50,000-deductible cover.
    - 💡 Book a slot in advance, arrive 30 min early, and carry the physical IDP booklet — a digital copy is not accepted. Cancellations within 6 days incur fees.
    - 🇮🇳 Big win for Indians: India is a party to the 1949 Geneva Convention, so an International Driving Permit issued by your local RTO (apply at your RTO with licence, passport, visa, photos; fee ~₹1,000, valid 1 year) is legally accepted in Japan and lets you join. Get it before you fly — you cannot obtain it in Japan, and without it you'll be turned away at the shop.
