- **High-tech washlet toilets: the everyday 'only in Japan' delight** — TOTO 'Washlet' seats (term coined by TOTO in 1980; other makers LIXIL, Panasonic) are standard in Japanese hotels, department stores, malls, major stations, highway rest stops and many convenience stores — roughly 82% of multi-person households own one. Features: heated seat, warm-water rear wash (button おしり/oshiri), front bidet for women (ビデ/bidet), adjustable pressure, warm-air dryer, deodorizer, auto lid, and an 'otohime' (音, 'sound princess') button that plays masking water sounds for privacy. Flush controls are 大 (large) / 小 (small); 止 is STOP.
    - 💴 Free to use in public/hotel restrooms — a zero-cost novelty. TOTO showrooms let you geek out; a seat as a souvenir purchase runs into the tens of thousands of yen.
    - 💡 Not every toilet is high-tech — older buildings and small rural stations may still have traditional squat toilets, so learn both. The buttons are usually pictogram-labelled; おしり = rear wash, 止 = stop.
    - 🇮🇳 For Indian travellers used to a health-faucet/jet spray, the washlet is an intuitive, welcome upgrade (warm-water wash + dryer) — arguably the feature first-timers rave about most; try it at your hotel on day one.
- **Vending-machine culture as a mini-experience** — Japan has one of the world's highest vending-machine densities — they're on virtually every street corner and station platform, offering hot AND cold canned coffee, tea, soft drinks, water, and quirky items. They're a reliable, cash-or-IC-card way to stay hydrated and a fun novelty (a warm can of coffee from a machine in winter is a classic first-timer moment). They pair naturally with the washlet story as the two 'everyday tech' surprises of Japan.
    - 💴 Typical drink ¥100-¥180 (≈₹60-105). Payable by coins, ¥1,000 notes, or tapping a Suica/PASMO IC card.
    - 💡 Look for the red 'あたたかい' (hot) vs blue 'つめたい' (cold) tags on drink machines. Keep a few ¥100 coins or a loaded IC card handy.
    - 🇮🇳 Load a Suica/PASMO IC card (buy at any station or via Apple Wallet) — you can then tap-pay vending machines, convenience stores, trains and many arcades without hunting for coins; Indian debit/UPI won't work directly in the machines.

_Sources: https://www.japan-guide.com/e/e2080.html · https://ryogokukokugikan.com/ryogoku-kokugikan-tickets · https://ryogokukokugikan.com/ryogoku-kokugikan-schedule-2026 · https://kart.st/ · https://kanko-map.com/en/karaoke-price-compare/ · https://kanko-map.com/en/japan-arcade-claw-machine-guide/ · https://www.pokemon-cafe.jp/en/cafe/ · https://www.planmyjapan.com/kirby-cafe-japan-review/ · https://tourli.com/guides/japan/tokyo/best-themed-cafes · https://entdecken.tokyo/why-japanese-toilets-are-high-tech/ · https://en.akihabara-bc.jp/akihabara-purikura/ · https://www.buysumotickets.com/_


### B15. Onsen & ryokan bathing etiquette for Indian travelers — nudity, wash-first rules, tattoo ban, and private-bath workarounds
_The onsen is Japan's signature ritual, but for Indian travelers the blockers are cultural, not logistical: you bathe fully nude (swimsuits are banned outright), baths are strictly gender-separated, and — critically — a majority of facilities still refuse or restrict visible tattoos, a real issue for the many Indians with tattoos (and a smaller consideration for fresh henna/mehndi). The good news is that every one of these anxieties has a concrete workaround. The single best solution for modesty, multi-generation families, and tattoos alike is a kashikiri-buro (private rental bath) or a ryokan room with its own in-room rotenburo — you bathe alone or as a family, clothed status and ink are nobody's business. For public baths there is a clear playbook: a strict wash-before-you-soak sequence, a small modesty towel you carry (never in the water), tattoo cover-seals for small ink (up to ~postcard size), and entire tattoo-welcoming towns like Kinosaki (all 7 public baths admit tattoos) and Beppu, plus a 1,000+ facility tattoo-friendly database. Figures below are in JPY with approximate INR at the mid-market rate of ~₹0.59 per ¥1 (Wise, 25 Jul 2026 — rates move, reconfirm before travel)._

- **The nudity shock, explained: fully naked, no swimsuit, ever — and how the modesty towel actually works** — This is the #1 anxiety and there is no way around it in a public onsen: 'Hot springs are enjoyed naked, and swimming suits are not allowed' (japan-guide). You strip completely in the changing room and enter the bath with only a small hand towel (about the size of a face towel) for modesty. That towel is your only cover: while walking around the washing/bathing area you hold it in front of you, and once in the water most people 'fold it up and place it on their head' or set it on the bath's edge. It must NEVER touch the bath water (hygiene). Swimsuits are barred 'for hygiene reasons', not prudishness — so wearing one is not an allowed compromise. If communal nudity is a hard no for you (very common for Indian women and mixed families), do NOT try a public bath — book a private bath instead (see the kashikiri item).
    - 💴 The small modesty towel is usually sold or rented at reception for ~¥100-200 (~₹60-120); many ryokan and hotels provide it free in the room.
    - 💡 Practice the towel drill: carry it in front while moving, park it on your head or the rim while soaking. Keep long hair tied up and out of the water.
    - 🇮🇳 For Indian travelers used to always-clothed public settings this is the single biggest cultural leap. Nobody stares — averting eyes is the norm — but if you (or elder family members) are not comfortable being nude among strangers, treat public onsen as optional and default to a private/family bath, which delivers the identical hot-spring experience with zero exposure.
- **The wash-first ritual, step by step (the etiquette that matters most)** — The onsen is for soaking in already-clean water, not for washing — so the pre-soak scrub is 'the rule that matters most; entering the bath soapy or unwashed is the one true offense' (yurutabi). Sequence: (1) shoes off, into slippers; (2) enter the correct gender-separated changing room and undress fully, everything in a locker/basket except the small towel; (3) go to the washing area, sit on a low stool at a shower station, and scrub your entire body with soap, then rinse thoroughly — twice; alternatively pour water over yourself (kakeyu) with the provided bowl; (4) enter the bath slowly, feet first, no splashing/swimming/jumping; (5) soak quietly (~10 min), no loud talking, no submerging your head, towel never in the water; (6) before returning to the changing room, wipe yourself down with the small towel so you don't drip on the floor. Soap and shampoo are almost always provided at the shower stations.
    - 💴 Body wash/shampoo at stations is included in entry; day-use public onsen run 'a few hundred to ~¥2,000' (~₹300-1,180), a Tokyo sento is the regulated ~¥550 (~₹324).
    - 💡 Don't rinse off the mineral water when you finish soaking if you want the skin benefits — but DO towel-dry before the changing room.
    - 🇮🇳 The wash-first logic will feel intuitive to Indian bathers (you clean off, THEN soak) — lean on it; getting the ritual right is what earns respectful nods and defuses any 'foreigner' scrutiny.
- **Gender separation and reading the noren curtains (so you don't walk into the wrong bath)** — Baths are 'almost always gender-segregated'. You identify your entrance by the noren (fabric curtain) color and kanji: RED curtain = women (女, onna), BLUE curtain = men (男, otoko). Truly mixed-gender baths (konyoku) 'are now rare and mostly rural'. Changing rooms and bathing halls are entirely separate, so families and couples split up at the entrance and regroup after — one more reason multi-gen or shy families often prefer a private bath where everyone stays together.
    - 💴 No extra cost, but note colors can occasionally swap day-to-day at some facilities, so check the kanji, not just the color.
    - 💡 Memorize the two kanji: 女 = women, 男 = men. When unsure, ask staff 'Onna wa dochira desu ka?' (Which way for women?).
    - 🇮🇳 For a family traveling together (parents + kids + grandparents), gender-separation means you cannot all bathe as one group in a public onsen — a private family bath is the only way to share the experience together.
- **The real fix for modesty, families AND tattoos: kashikiri-buro / kazoku-buro / in-room rotenburo** — A kashikiri-buro is 'a private bath you reserve for yourself, a couple, or a family for a set time' at a ryokan or bathhouse (also called kazoku-buro, family bath). You bathe with no strangers present — so nudity is private, families stay together, and tattoos are a non-issue because no one is checking. The premium version is a ryokan guest room with its own in-room rotenburo (private outdoor hot-spring bath) fed by the same spring water — described across guides as 'the ultimate solution'. Sessions typically last 45-60 minutes; at ryokan the private bath is sometimes included in the room rate. This is the option to default to if communal nudity, a mixed-gen family, or visible tattoos are your concern — it removes all three blockers at once.
    - 💴 Reservable kashikiri/family baths commonly run ¥2,000-¥5,000 per session (~₹1,180-₹2,940), on top of any bathing fee; rooms with a private in-room onsen cost meaningfully more than a standard ryokan room but include unlimited private soaking.
    - 💡 When booking a ryokan, filter/ask for 'kashikiri-buro' or 'room with private onsen / rotenburo'. Confirm whether it's included or a paid add-on, and reserve your time slot at check-in.
    - 🇮🇳 This is THE recommendation for most first-time Indian visitors: it sidesteps communal-nudity discomfort, lets a whole family bathe together (impossible in gender-split public baths), and quietly solves the tattoo/henna problem. Book it when reserving the ryokan — availability is limited and often sells out.
- **The tattoo ban: why it exists and how widespread it still is (2023-2026 figures)** — Visible tattoos bar entry at many baths because 'for generations, elaborate body tattoos in Japan were the mark of the yakuza' (organized crime), so facilities banned ink to keep them out. This is the modesty-adjacent blocker most Indians don't see coming. How common is it today? Sources converge: a Japan Tourism Agency-cited figure that 'about 60% of facilities still have tattoo policies', and a 2023 survey that 'roughly half of facilities now show some flexibility' — allowing small pieces, offering cover patches, or providing private baths. One first-timer guide's honest breakdown: ~30% of hot springs allow entry if you cover the ink with a seal, and ~20% welcome tattoos regardless. Bottom line: assume a random public onsen may refuse visible tattoos, and plan one of the three workarounds (cover, tattoo-friendly venue, or private bath) rather than risk being turned away at reception.
    - 💴 Being refused costs you nothing but embarrassment and a wasted trip out — 'a uniquely humiliating expat experience' — so the workarounds below are worth the small spend.
    - 💡 Ask reception before undressing: 'Tattoo wa daijoubu desu ka?' (Are tattoos OK?). A 'no' at the desk is far better than being asked to leave the water.
    - 🇮🇳 Tattoos are common among younger Indian travelers, and even a small wrist/ankle/nape tattoo can trigger a refusal. Don't assume a discreet tattoo is fine — check or cover it. (Henna/mehndi is temporary, not permanent ink, so it isn't the yakuza-association concern; but fresh, very dark henna can look like a tattoo to staff and may run in hot water — if you have elaborate fresh mehndi, a private bath avoids any awkward questions. This henna point is practical guidance, not a documented facility rule.)
- **Tattoo cover-seals: brands, sizes, where to buy, and the size limit** — For small tattoos, waterproof skin-tone cover seals let you into facilities that 'allow fully covered tattoos'. Real products: in Japan look for CAXEL (film/sticker, multiple sizes), Hada Kakushito (ultra-thin water-resistant sheets), and Secret Sheet (two-tone, edge-blending); US brands to pack ahead include Tat2X 'Tat Skin' (cut-to-size waterproof tape) and TatJacket 'Hide Ink' (pre-cut waterproof stickers). Critical limitation: patches only work up to 'about postcard size' — small wrist, ankle, shoulder or nape pieces. Full sleeves and large areas 'cannot be meaningfully concealed', so those need a tattoo-friendly venue or a private bath. Apply to clean, dry skin; move gently in the water so edges don't peel; carry spares for a long soak. Covering is NOT a guaranteed pass — acceptance is case-by-case, so still confirm at reception.
    - 💴 Patches cost 'a few hundred yen per patch' (roughly ¥200-800, ~₹120-470). Buy in Japan at Don Quijote ('biggest chance' of stocking them), Matsumoto Kiyoshi and other drugstores; search 'タトゥー 隠し シール' (tattoo kakushi seal). Or buy on Amazon/Walmart before you fly.
    - 💡 Measure your tattoo first and buy a seal a bit larger; test-wear one in a hot shower at your hotel to make sure it stays put before you rely on it at the onsen.
    - 🇮🇳 Pack a few correctly-sized seals from home (Amazon India / bring from the US brands) rather than gambling on a specific Don Quijote branch having your size on the day. Skin-tone waterproof bandages are a low-cost backup for a tiny tattoo.
- **Tattoo-welcoming towns and onsen (where ink is simply not a problem)** — Some places skip the drama entirely. Standout: Kinosaki Onsen (Sea of Japan coast, Hyogo) — 'its seven public bathhouses welcome tattooed guests without restriction', so you can do the town's famous bathhouse-hop in a yukata without covering up. Other tattoo-lenient areas repeatedly named: Beppu (Oita) 'known for welcoming tattooed visitors', Hokkaido generally (including Niseko) with 'higher overall acceptance', Hakuba, and Dogo Onsen (Ehime). Neighborhood sento in Tokyo and Kyoto are also generally more accepting than resort onsen (but still vary). For anywhere else, use the tattoo-friendly.jp database, which lists 'over 1,000 tattoo friendly Onsen' searchable by prefecture — named examples include Hyotan Onsen (Oita), Hoheikyo Onsen (Hokkaido), Yamato no Yu (Chiba) and Sueyoshi Onsen Miharashi no Yu (Tokyo).
    - 💴 Kinosaki sells a Yumepa / one-day pass for unlimited hops across its seven baths (individual bath entry is roughly ¥600-800, ~₹350-470 each; confirm current pass price on arrival). Most listed onsen charge normal entry (~¥500-1,500, ~₹300-880).
    - 💡 Even in lenient towns, individual private bathhouses can set their own rule, so a quick reception check still helps. Bookmark tattoo-friendly.jp and filter by the prefecture you're visiting.
    - 🇮🇳 If you have a larger or visible tattoo, build your itinerary around one of these towns (Kinosaki is the easiest 'yes') rather than hoping a random onsen near your hotel will admit you — it turns a source of anxiety into a highlight.
- **Indian-traveler action plan: match the bath to your comfort level + useful phrases** — Decision guide: (a) Comfortable with communal nudity + no visible tattoos → any public onsen, just nail the wash-first ritual and towel rules. (b) Small tattoo → cover-seal + confirm at reception, OR pick a tattoo-friendly town. (c) Not comfortable being nude / traveling as a mixed-gender or multi-gen family / large tattoo / fresh elaborate henna → book a kashikiri-buro or a ryokan room with private in-room rotenburo (solves modesty, family-togetherness and tattoos in one move). What to bring: a small modesty/hand towel (or use the provided one), your own tattoo seals sized ahead, and hair ties for long hair. Useful Japanese: 'Tattoo wa daijoubu desu ka?' (Are tattoos OK?), 'Kashikiri-buro wa arimasu ka?' (Do you have a private bath?), 'Onna/Otoko wa dochira desu ka?' (Which way for women/men?).
    - 💴 Budget snapshot in INR: public day-use onsen ¥500-2,000 (~₹300-1,180); Tokyo sento ~¥550 (~₹324); private/family bath ¥2,000-5,000 (~₹1,180-2,940); tattoo seals a few hundred yen each (~₹120-470). Conversion at ~₹0.59/¥1 (Wise mid-market, 25 Jul 2026) — reconfirm, JPY↔INR moves.
    - 💡 Do a low-stakes first onsen at your ryokan (in-room or during a quiet early-morning slot) before attempting a busy public bathhouse — the ritual feels far easier the second time.
    - 🇮🇳 For most first-time Indian visitors — especially women, honeymooners wanting to bathe together, or families with parents/grandparents — the private/in-room onsen is the sweet spot: full hot-spring experience, zero nudity-among-strangers, no tattoo/henna scrutiny. Reserve it at the time of ryokan booking; the best rooms sell out.

_Sources: https://www.japan-guide.com/e/e2292_how.html · https://tattoo-friendly.jp/ · https://hellonihon.net/tattoo-cover-seals-onsen/ · https://watabi.org/en/articles/japan-onsen-tattoo-guide · https://media.yolo-japan.com/en/559/ · https://traveljapan-together.com/blog/experiences/inked-in-japan-how-to-find-and-enjoy-tattoo-friendly-onsen-and-private-ryokans/ · https://selfguidejapan.com/blog/japan-onsen-etiquette · https://yurutabi.blog/en/onsen-etiquette-guide/ · https://wise.com/us/currency-converter/jpy-to-inr-rate_


### B16. Practical know-how for Japan (trains/IC cards/airport transfers, accommodation, connectivity, etiquette, luggage forwarding, safety, apps & phrases) — for an Indian traveler
_Japan is one of the most traveler-friendly countries on earth once you know the mechanics. The core toolkit: a tap-to-pay IC card (or mobile Suica on iPhone) for all trains/buses/konbini; Google Maps + Japan Travel by NAVITIME for routing; a cheap eSIM for data; and cash for the ~30% of places still card-shy. Airport-to-city is a solved problem via named express trains (Skyliner, N'EX, Haruka). Accommodation spans ¥2,000 capsules to ¥15,000+ onsen ryokan. The unspoken rules matter more than in India — silence on trains, shoes off indoors, no tipping ever, wash before the onsen, carry your trash. Takkyubin luggage forwarding and station coin lockers let you travel hands-free. Japan is extremely safe (lost wallets get returned), emergencies are 110/119. For Indians specifically: a cheap ₹500 e-visa, direct 7.5–8.5 hr flights, ₹0.58-ish per yen, and a genuinely good veg/halal/Indian-food scene in big cities. Note: as of mid-2026 the current JPY→INR mid-market rate is ~₹0.59/¥1; it swings, so treat all INR figures as approximate. Regular physical Suica/Pasmo have been supply-constrained since 2023, so tourists mostly use Welcome Suica or mobile Suica._

- **IC cards (Suica/Pasmo/ICOCA): tap-to-ride + tap-to-pay everywhere** — One rechargeable card taps you through every train, subway and bus gate nationwide, and doubles as a wallet at konbini (7-Eleven, FamilyMart, Lawson), vending machines, coin lockers and many restaurants — look for the fan-shaped IC logo. Suica/Pasmo are the Tokyo cards, ICOCA is the Kansai (Osaka/Kyoto) card, but all are cross-compatible nationwide. IMPORTANT for 2025-26: regular physical Suica/Pasmo have been supply-limited since a 2023 chip shortage, so tourists use 'Welcome Suica' (a physical tourist card, valid 28 days, no ¥500 deposit, but unused balance is NON-refundable — spend it down before flying home). Charge with cash at any station machine or konbini cashier; max load ¥20,000.
    - 💴 Welcome Suica: no deposit; regular Suica normally has a ¥500 (~₹290) refundable deposit. Charge in cash, e.g. ¥3,000 (~₹1,750) to start. No fee to tap-pay.
    - 💡 Grab a Welcome Suica at the JR EAST Travel Service Center in the Narita/Haneda arrivals hall right after landing. Keep ¥1,000-2,000 balance so you never get stuck at a gate.
    - 🇮🇳 iPhone (8 and newer) is the best option for Indians: add a Suica to Apple Wallet and top it up with an Indian credit/debit card via Apple Pay — no counter queue. Android 'Mobile Suica' effectively requires a Japanese card/address, so Indian Android users should get a physical Welcome Suica at the airport instead.
- **Navigation apps: Google Maps as the base, NAVITIME/Yahoo for train precision** — Google Maps works remarkably well in Japan for door-to-door transit — real-time departures, correct lines and fares. Its weak spots: it doesn't always show platform numbers or whether a train is Local/Rapid/Express/Limited-Express (which matters — a 'Rapid' skips stops). Pair it with 'Japan Travel by NAVITIME' (free, English, purpose-built for foreigners, has a JR-Pass filter mode and shows Shinkansen options) or 'Yahoo! Norikae Annai (Yahoo Transit)' which locals swear by for exact platforms, transfer walking times, and which train car to board. Jorudan is another solid English route planner.
    - 💴 All free to download. NAVITIME and Google Maps are free; NAVITIME has optional paid premium features you don't need as a tourist.
    - 💡 In Google Maps, the fare shown is the IC-card fare. When it says a platform, cross-check the big overhead station signs; stations like Shinjuku are huge, so budget 10-15 min to transfer.
    - 🇮🇳 Download Google Maps offline area packs for Tokyo/Kyoto/Osaka before you land so navigation works even before your eSIM activates. Google Translate offline Japanese pack is equally essential.
- **Airport → city: named express trains beat taxis by a mile** — From NARITA (the far airport, ~60-90 min out): Keisei Skyliner is fastest to Ueno/Nippori (~36-40 min); Narita Express (N'EX, JR) runs direct to Tokyo/Shibuya/Shinjuku (~53-80 min) and is free on a JR Pass. From HANEDA (close in): Tokyo Monorail or Keikyu Line, ~15-30 min to central Tokyo, cheap. From KANSAI/KIX (for Osaka/Kyoto): JR Haruka Limited Express to Kyoto (~75 min) or Nankai to Namba/Osaka. Taxis from Narita to central Tokyo are ¥21,500-35,500 (~₹12,500-20,600) — avoid unless jet-lagged with a group.
    - 💴 Skyliner: ¥2,520 (~₹1,460), or ¥2,310 (~₹1,340) with the tourist-only online Skyliner discount ticket. N'EX: ~¥3,070 (~₹1,780). Nankai Airport Express to Namba: ¥970 (~₹565). JR Haruka to Kyoto: ~¥3,000, or ¥2,200 (~₹1,280) with the foreigner-only Haruka discount. Narita↔Haneda direct: ~¥1,787 (~₹1,040) by Keisei Access Express.
    - 💡 You can tap through most of these with your IC card EXCEPT reserved premium trains like the Nankai Rapi:t and reserved Skyliner/Haruka seats, which need a separate ticket.
    - 🇮🇳 Buy the tourist-discount Skyliner/Haruka tickets online before you fly (Klook or the operator site) — cheaper and skips the counter after a long flight from India.
- **Shinkansen & the JR Pass: the math changed in 2023 — usually skip it now** — The bullet train is the joy of Japan travel (Tokyo→Kyoto in ~2h15). The nationwide JR Pass used to be a no-brainer, but after the Oct-2023 hike its value collapsed and it still excludes the fastest Nozomi/Mizuho trains (you pay a supplement or take slower Hikari). For a typical first trip (Tokyo–Kyoto–Osaka round trip) individual tickets are cheaper than the 7-day pass. Only heavy long-distance itineraries (e.g. Tokyo–Hiroshima–back, plus lots more) still justify it.
    - 💴 JR Pass (Ordinary): 7-day ¥50,000 (~₹29,000), 14-day ¥80,000 (~₹46,400), 21-day ¥100,000 (~₹58,000). Reference single fares: Tokyo→Kyoto ~¥13,000-14,000 (~₹7,500-8,100) one way. So a Tokyo-Kyoto-Osaka loop (~¥28,000) is well under the ¥50,000 pass.
    - 💡 Reserve a seat if you have big luggage — the oversized-baggage area at the back of the car must be pre-booked (free) or you risk a fee. Ekiben (station bento boxes) eaten on the Shinkansen are a rite of passage.
    - 🇮🇳 Book Shinkansen seats via the SmartEX app or at green ticket machines; foreign cards work. On the busy Tokaido line you can just walk up and buy — trains run every ~10 min.
- **Accommodation types & realistic nightly costs** — Business hotels (APA, Toyoko Inn, Route Inn, Sotetsu Fresa) are the workhorse: tiny but spotless private rooms with a private bath, great locations — ideal for Indian couples/solo. Capsule hotels: a clean sleeping pod, shared bath, often gender-separated — an experience worth trying once. Hostels/guesthouses: dorm beds, sociable, cheapest. Ryokan: traditional tatami-and-futon inns, often with an onsen and multi-course kaiseki dinner/breakfast — splurge on at least one night (Hakone, Kyoto, Takayama). Minshuku are the budget family-run version.
    - 💴 Capsule ¥2,000-5,000 (~₹1,160-2,900); hostel dorm ¥2,500-6,000 (~₹1,450-3,500); business hotel ¥7,000-15,000 (~₹4,060-8,700, often ~₹5,000-6,000 for two); ryokan with onsen + 2 meals ¥15,000-40,000 per person (~₹8,700-23,200). Peak sakura (late Mar-Apr) and autumn foliage (Nov) prices spike — book early.
    - 💡 Check-in is typically 15:00, checkout 10:00-11:00. Many hotels let you drop bags at reception before check-in and forward luggage — see takkyubin below.
    - 🇮🇳 Rooms are SMALL by Indian standards — a 'double' can be 12-14 sqm. Book a 'twin' or 'semi-double' if two of you want space. Free amenities (toothbrush, razor, yukata, tea) are standard even in budget hotels, so pack light.
- **Airbnb & the minpaku law — book only LICENSED listings** — Legal short-term rentals in Japan run under the 2018 Minpaku Law: a licensed home can host max 180 nights/year and MUST display a registration/notification number on the listing. Plenty of illegal listings get pulled at short notice, stranding guests. If a listing shows no license number, don't book it.
    - 💴 Roughly comparable to business hotels; can be better value for families/groups needing a kitchen. Hosts operating illegally face fines up to ¥1,000,000 (~₹580,000), which is why sketchy listings vanish.
    - 💡 Look for the license/registration number in the listing description; message the host to confirm it before paying. Note self-check-in lockbox instructions and the nearest station carefully.
    - 🇮🇳 For a first Japan trip, business hotels are usually simpler than Airbnb — central, English-friendly check-in, and no license/self-check-in risk. Consider Airbnb mainly for group stays in one apartment.
- **Connectivity: eSIM is the best value; pocket wifi for groups** — Three options. eSIM (best for solo/couple): instant QR activation, no pickup, runs on local Docomo/SoftBank/KDDI 5G. Named providers: Ubigi (Docomo+KDDI, best rural coverage), Sakura Mobile (Docomo, English support), Airalo/Saily/Holafly (Saily is cheap, Holafly's is genuinely uncapped). Pocket wifi (best for families/groups): one rented device shares data across up to 5-10 phones. Physical SIM: for older/locked or eSIM-incompatible phones.
    - 💴 eSIM: ~$4 (~₹350) for 1GB/30d, ~$8 (~₹700) for 3GB, ~$17 (~₹1,500) for 10GB (Ubigi); Saily from $3.99; unlimited ~$65-66/mo (~₹5,700). Pocket wifi ~¥680/day (~₹395) unlimited, shared. Physical SIM ~¥2,300-3,000 (~₹1,340-1,750) for 3-30 days.
    - 💡 Buy a slightly bigger data plan than you think — offline maps help but you'll lean on Maps + Translate + Google Lens constantly. For a family from India, one pocket wifi is cheaper per head than 4 eSIMs.
    - 🇮🇳 Most phones sold in India post-2018 support eSIM (iPhone XR+, Pixel, recent Samsung/OnePlus). Buy the eSIM online from India BEFORE departure and install it on wifi at home; it activates when you land — no airport counter, no Japanese card needed. Confirm your phone is NOT carrier-locked.
- **Etiquette: the unwritten rules that actually matter** — TRAINS: near-silent. Phone on silent ('manner mode'), no calls, keep conversation low — this is the biggest culture gap for Indian travelers. SHOES: off at ryokan/temples/homes/some restaurants (watch for a genkan step and slippers); never step on tatami in slippers. ONSEN: wash and rinse thoroughly at the seated showers FIRST, enter naked (no swimsuit), small towel never touches the water. TIPPING: none — it's confusing or mildly offensive; great service is standard. TRASH: bins are rare on streets — carry a small bag and hold your rubbish till you find one (konbini/station). CHOPSTICKS: never stand them upright in rice and never pass food chopstick-to-chopstick (both are funeral rites). BOWING: a small head nod is plenty; no need to over-bow. Also: don't eat while walking, queue neatly, and stand on the correct escalator side (left in Tokyo, right in Osaka).
    - 💴 Free to follow — but disruptive-behavior fines and refused entry exist at some sites. No tipping saves you money.
    - 💡 TATTOOS can bar you from many onsen/public baths — check ahead, use a tattoo-friendly onsen, book a private (kashikiri) bath, or cover small tattoos with a patch.
    - 🇮🇳 Big adjustments coming from India: silence on public transport, no haggling (fixed prices), strict queuing, and hyper-punctual trains (a 2-min delay gets an apology). Blow-your-nose-in-public is frowned on; sniffing is oddly fine.
- **Luggage forwarding (takkyubin) & coin lockers = travel hands-free** — Takkyubin/takuhaibin is Japan's magic door-to-door luggage delivery — market leader Yamato Transport (the black-cat 'Kuroneko' logo), plus Sagawa and Japan Post. Drop your suitcase at your hotel front desk, a konbini, or an airport counter and it arrives at your next hotel, usually NEXT DAY. Perfect for skipping the Shinkansen with heavy bags, or shipping bags airport→first hotel so you sightsee straight off the plane. Coin lockers at every major station hold bags for the day.
    - 💴 One suitcase (<25kg) Tokyo↔Osaka/Kyoto ~¥2,300-3,200 (~₹1,340-1,860); to/from Narita ~¥3,700 (~₹2,150). Coin lockers ¥300-800/day (~₹175-465) by size; pay by coin or IC card.
    - 💡 Ship 1 day before you move on (delivery is next-day, not same-day for long distances). Airport counters at Narita/Haneda/KIX handle it; ask your hotel to arrange pickup. Lockers fill up by mid-morning at tourist hubs — grab one early or use a manned baggage-storage service.
    - 🇮🇳 Hugely useful given how packed Tokyo trains get and how small hotel rooms are — send the big Indian-sized suitcase ahead and travel with a daypack.
- **Safety, emergencies & lost items** — Japan is among the safest countries anywhere — low crime, safe to walk at night, solo-female-friendly. Emergency numbers: 110 for police, 119 for fire/ambulance (free from any phone, even locked/no-SIM). Koban (small neighborhood police boxes) are everywhere and staff are helpful for directions and lost items. Japan's lost-and-found culture is legendary — dropped wallets and phones are routinely handed in intact.
    - 💴 Emergency calls are free. Travel insurance is cheap from India (~₹1,500-3,000 for a 1-2 week trip) and worth it since Japanese hospital bills are high without it.
    - 💡 Lost something on a train? Note the line + time and ask staff or a koban — recovery rates are astonishing. Say 'Nihongo ga wakarimasen' (I don't understand Japanese) on an emergency call and they connect an interpreter.
    - 🇮🇳 JNTO tourist hotline 050-3816-2787 (24/7, English/Chinese/Korean) for any travel trouble. Carry your passport at all times — police can legally ask foreign visitors to show it.
- **Essential apps & survival phrases** — Load before you fly: Google Maps (+ offline areas), Google Translate (offline Japanese pack; the camera/Lens mode reads menus and signs in real time), Japan Travel by NAVITIME or Yahoo Transit, your IC card in Apple Wallet, and a food app (Halal Navi / HappyCow for veg). Key phrases: Konnichiwa (hello), Arigatou gozaimasu (thank you), Sumimasen (excuse me / sorry / to get attention — the most useful word), Onegaishimasu (please), Hai/Iie (yes/no), Ikura desu ka? (how much?), Eigo no menu wa arimasu ka? (do you have an English menu?), Toire wa doko desu ka? (where's the toilet?), Oishii (delicious), Gochisousama deshita (thanks for the meal).
    - 💴 All free.
    - 💡 Point at menu photos and say 'Kore o kudasai' (this one, please). A polite 'Sumimasen' + small bow smooths almost every interaction.
    - 🇮🇳 Google Translate's live camera mode is the single most useful tool for an Indian traveler decoding Japanese menus, konbini labels (crucial for veg/halal), and station signs. English is limited outside big cities, so it's a lifeline.
- **Money: still a cash country — carry yen, and know the ~₹0.58/¥ rate** — Japan is far more cashless than a decade ago (IC cards, credit cards, PayPay QR widely accepted in cities), but cash is still king at small restaurants, temples, markets, some ryokan, and rural areas. The most reliable ATMs for foreign cards are at 7-Eleven (7-Bank) and Japan Post — open long hours and English-menu'd; many bank ATMs reject foreign cards.
    - 💴 Rough conversion as of mid-2026: ¥1 ≈ ₹0.58-0.59, so ¥1,000 ≈ ₹580-590, ¥10,000 ≈ ₹5,800-5,900. The rate swings (it ranged ~₹0.56-0.61 across 2025), so treat all INR figures here as approximate and re-check before travel.
    - 💡 Keep ~¥5,000-10,000 cash on you at all times for temples, small eateries and lockers. Coins add up fast — dump them into your IC card at a machine.
    - 🇮🇳 Carry an Indian card with low forex markup (or a forex/travel card) plus some cash yen from India for day one. Notify your Indian bank of travel to avoid card blocks. 7-Eleven ATMs reliably dispense yen on Indian Visa/Mastercard.
- **India-specific: the ₹500 e-visa + direct flights** — Since 2024 Indian tourists can get a Japan tourist e-visa: apply via VFS Global India (or the official Japan e-visa portal), and at the airport just show the digital 'visa issuance notice' with its 2D barcode on your phone — no printout needed. Valid for stays up to 90 days. Direct nonstop flights run from Delhi, Mumbai and Bengaluru on Air India, ANA and JAL.
    - 💴 Visa fee ~₹500 (single or multiple entry) + VFS service charge (~₹800+). Direct round-trip DEL/BOM/BLR↔Tokyo typically ~₹48,000-55,000; book 4-8 weeks ahead off-peak, 8-12+ weeks ahead for sakura season. Flight time ~7.5 hrs (Delhi) to ~8.5 hrs (Mumbai).
    - 💡 Keep the e-visa notice saved offline on your phone AND email in case of no signal at immigration. Fill the Visit Japan Web (immigration/customs QR) online before landing to skip paper forms.
    - 🇮🇳 Passport must have 6+ months validity and 2 blank pages. Processing ~5 working days but apply 3-4 weeks early in peak season. Japan is only ~3.5 hrs ahead of IST, so jet lag is mild.
- **Food for Indian travelers: veg, halal & Indian restaurants** — Eating veg/halal is very doable in cities, harder in the countryside. Tokyo (Shinjuku, Shibuya, Ikebukuro), Osaka and Kyoto have the densest halal and Indian-restaurant scenes; shojin ryori (Buddhist temple vegetarian cuisine) is naturally veg and often near temples/Koyasan. Konbini are a lifeline: plain onigiri (tuna/kombu), boiled eggs, salads, fruit, yogurt, instant noodles — many stores have microwaves and hot-water dispensers.
    - 💴 Konbini meal ¥400-800 (~₹230-465); a good Indian/curry-house meal ¥1,000-2,000 (~₹580-1,160); shojin ryori set can run higher at temples.
    - 💡 Learn to spot the kanji for pork (豚), chicken (鶏), beef (牛) and fish (魚). Big-city Indian restaurants (often Nepali/Bangladeshi-run) are everywhere and reliably veg-friendly when you need a familiar meal.
    - 🇮🇳 Beware hidden non-veg: dashi (bonito fish stock) and pork fat are in many 'vegetable' dishes and broths. Carry a 'no pork, no alcohol' or 'no meat/fish, I'm vegetarian' card written in Japanese; use Google Translate on ingredient labels. Apps: Halal Navi and HappyCow to find verified spots. Pure-veg Jain travelers should stick to Indian restaurants or self-cater.

_Sources: https://japantravel.navitime.com/en/area/jp/guide/NTJhowto0063-en/ · https://www.japan-guide.com/e/e2359_003.html · https://www.jrpass.com/blog/the-best-japan-train-apps-for-tourists-in-2026 · https://www.gofarther.blog/blog/best-japan-travel-apps-google-maps-vs-navitime-musthave-tools · https://e-housing.jp/post/tokyo-airport-transfer-narita-and-haneda-airport-to-tokyo-guide · https://www.jrailpass.com/blog/narita-airport-to-tokyo · https://japantravel.navitime.com/en/area/jp/guide/NTJhowto0077-en/ · https://www.klook.com/en-US/blog/kansai-airport-osaka-guide/ · https://www.jrailpass.com/blog/japan-pocket-wifi-vs-esim-vs-sim-card · https://www.japan-wireless.com/column/best-esims-for-japan-travel-2025-comparison-of-top-providers/ · https://gizmodo.com/best-esim-provider/japan · https://stayinjapan.jp/articles/japanese-accommodations-guide_


### B17. Natural-hazard & weather readiness: typhoon season, earthquakes, rainy season, heat, and season-specific packing (incl. winter gear for warm-climate Indians)
_Japan's biggest travel risk for Indian visitors isn't crime (it's very safe) but weather and geology: an Aug–Oct typhoon season that grounds flights and halts the Shinkansen, a humid June "tsuyu" rainy season, record-breaking August heat (2025 was Japan's hottest ever, 41.8°C, 100,000+ heatstroke hospitalisations), and frequent earthquakes. The high-value, thin-today content: (1) build itinerary flexibility + buy weather-inclusive travel insurance BEFORE any typhoon is named (once named it's a "known risk" and no longer covered); ANA and JAL both waive change/refund fees for typhoon-hit domestic flights, and Shinkansen suspends above ~25 m/s wind. (2) A simple earthquake protocol (Drop-Cover-Hold On) plus the free JNTO "Safety Tips" app for J-Alert push warnings, and the reassurance that the Nankai Trough "megaquake" advisory is a 30-year statistical risk, not a reason to cancel. (3) Concrete packing: for summer, light breathable clothes + electrolytes; for winter Hokkaido (Sapporo −5 to −20°C), a 3-layer system with NO cotton/jeans, waterproof snow boots, and cheap fixes like Uniqlo HeatTech (¥990–1,990) and kairo hand-warmers (¥30–50). Warm-climate Indians who own no sub-zero gear can either pre-buy at Decathlon India (Wedze thermals ₹499–999, −29°C down jackets) or rent full snow-wear bundles at resorts (~¥8,000/day). Emergency numbers: 110 police, 119 fire/ambulance, and JNTO's 24/7 Japan Visitor Hotline 050-3816-2787 (English/Hindi not listed but English available). Rate used: 1 JPY ≈ ₹0.59 (24 Jul 2026)._

- **Typhoon season (peak Aug–Sep, runs Jul–Oct) can cancel flights and stop the Shinkansen** — Japan's typhoon season runs roughly July–October with peak activity in August and September; typhoons approach the country about once every 1–2 weeks in peak months (JMA/Weathernews forecasts ~23–28 storms/year, ~14 approaching Japan). The Tokaido Shinkansen (Tokyo–Osaka) and Sanyo Shinkansen (Osaka–Fukuoka) run planned suspensions ('keikaku unkyu') and halt when winds exceed about 25 metres/second; airlines cancel pre-emptively, often 24–48 hours ahead. Inland cities (Tokyo, Kyoto, Osaka) usually get heavy rain rather than destruction; the worst disruption is in the south/Okinawa. Trip-planning implication: don't book back-to-back tight connections in Aug–Sep, keep refundable hotels, and carry ~1 week of cash since a stalled train/airport can strand you.
    - 💴 No direct cost, but a missed non-refundable connection can cost tens of thousands of yen; book refundable/free-cancellation rates for Aug–Sep legs.
    - 💡 Watch JMA (jma.go.jp) and the airline app; if a typhoon is forecast, rebook the FREE waiver flight early before slots fill.
    - 🇮🇳 Indians flying in on peak-season tickets (esp. via Okinawa or to Kyushu) should keep the first/last days buffered before an international flight home, so a domestic typhoon delay doesn't make them miss the India-bound flight.
- **ANA and JAL waive change/refund fees for typhoon-hit domestic flights — use the waiver early** — Both major carriers offer fee-free rebooking or refunds when a domestic flight is disrupted by 'acts of nature' such as typhoons or snow. ANA lets ticket holders change or refund online with no handling fee once a typhoon is expected to affect operations (process from the day after departure, within 10 days). JAL lets you change to another flight within 30 days of the departure date at no extra charge, or refund, when disruption is expected — online or via the JAL Contact Center. WARNING: low-cost carriers (Peach, Jetstar Japan) may NOT offer full refunds or free changes even in a disaster, so their cheaper fares carry more weather risk.
    - 💴 ANA/JAL weather waivers: ¥0 change fee. LCCs: often no free change — factor the risk into the fare gap.
    - 💡 Always get a written delay/cancellation certificate from the airline counter — you need it to claim on travel insurance.
    - 🇮🇳 For domestic hops inside Japan (e.g. Tokyo→Sapporo), an Indian traveller on a tight overall budget may still prefer ANA/JAL over an LCC in Aug–Sep purely because the weather waiver protects the ticket.
- **Buy weather-inclusive travel insurance BEFORE any typhoon is named — the 'known risk' trap** — Comprehensive travel insurance can reimburse trip cancellation, interruption and delay caused by typhoons, tsunami or earthquakes, and cover emergency evacuation. The critical catch for Indian buyers: once a typhoon has been named and widely reported, it becomes a 'known/foreseeable risk' and a policy bought AFTER that point will NOT pay out for cancellations/delays tied to that storm. So insurance must be purchased when you book, not when the forecast turns bad. Check the policy explicitly lists trip cancellation, missed connection, and travel delay for weather/natural-disaster causes (many basic medical-only plans exclude them).
    - 💴 Japan travel insurance from India starts around ₹20/day (Policybazaar aggregation); comprehensive plans reimburse up to 100% of insured non-refundable trip cost.
    - 💡 Screenshot the policy's 'natural disaster / travel delay' clause; save all receipts and the airline's disruption certificate for the claim.
    - 🇮🇳 Buy from India (Policybazaar, Bajaj Allianz, TATA AIG, etc.) at the time of booking; Japan does not mandate insurance for tourist entry, but confirm the plan covers weather cancellation, not just hospitalisation, and keep policy + emergency number offline on your phone.
- **June 'tsuyu' rainy season: light rain but brutal 80–90% humidity (Tokyo ~8 Jun–20 Jul 2026)** — The tsuyu (plum-rain) front covers most of Japan roughly June to mid-July; JMA's typical calendar has Tokyo entering around 8 June and clearing around 20 July, Okinawa starting early May, Tohoku late June. The real problem isn't rain volume (showers rarely last all day) but humidity of 80–90% at 22–28°C, which feels oppressive to anyone not acclimatised. Upside: fewer crowds, cheaper hotels, lush hydrangeas peaking mid-June. Key fact for itinerary design: Hokkaido largely escapes tsuyu — far less rain and humidity in June–July — so it's the smart region if you want good weather in early summer.
    - 💴 Tsuyu = shoulder season: lower hotel rates and thinner crowds than Aug/festival peak.
    - 💡 Pack a compact folding umbrella (or buy a ¥500–700 one at any konbini), quick-dry synthetic/merino clothing, and waterproof walking shoes — the single most useful rainy-season item.
    - 🇮🇳 Monsoon-savvy Indians will find the rain manageable, but the sticky heat-plus-humidity combo is worse than most Indian cities for walking sightseeing — plan air-conditioned indoor backups (museums, aquariums, depachika food halls).
- **Record August heat and heatstroke — 2025 was Japan's hottest summer ever** — Summer 2025 was Japan's hottest on record: nationwide temps ~2.36°C above normal, a national record 41.8°C at Isesaki (Gunma) on 5 Aug, and Tokyo hitting 35°C+ for 10 straight days by late August. Over 100,000 people were hospitalised for heatstroke nationwide May–Sep 2025 (a record), 8,341 by ambulance in Tokyo alone. The hidden danger is humidity making sweat ineffective. Authorities' coping advice: use air-conditioning, wear a hat or use a parasol, avoid direct sun midday, and sip water before you feel thirsty. This matters for trip design — outdoor-heavy August itineraries (Fushimi Inari climbs, temple walks) should be front-loaded to early morning.
    - 💴 Free cooling: konbini and department stores are strongly air-conditioned refuges; a handheld/neck fan (~¥1,000–2,000, ₹590–1,180) and cooling wipes are cheap relief.
    - 💡 Buy chilled Pocari Sweat / Aquarius (electrolyte drinks) from vending machines everywhere; carry a small towel — locals use them constantly to wipe sweat.
    - 🇮🇳 Indians handle dry heat well, but Japan's high humidity + heat is a different, more dangerous combo; pack ORS/electrolyte sachets from India (Japanese equivalent is 'OS-1', sold at pharmacies). Loose light cotton/linen, a cap and sunglasses are essential.
- **Earthquakes: learn Drop–Cover–Hold On and install the free JNTO 'Safety Tips' app** — Japan is highly seismic; tremors are routine (a Tokyo tremor in Nov 2025, a Hokkaido quake in Dec 2025). The standard drill INDOORS is Drop–Cover–Hold On: drop to the floor, take cover under a sturdy table (or crawl beside an interior wall and protect head/neck), hold on until shaking stops. Stay away from windows/glass/heavy furniture; do NOT use elevators; outdoors move away from buildings, trees and power lines; near the coast, if shaking is strong, evacuate to high ground immediately without waiting for an official tsunami call. Modern Japanese buildings are engineered for quakes, and hotels post evacuation info. The essential tool is the government-supervised free 'Safety Tips' app (iOS/Android), which pushes earthquake early warnings, tsunami and typhoon alerts and J-Alert in 15 languages (English, Nepali, etc.) with an evacuation flowchart and GPS shelter maps.
    - 💴 Safety Tips app: free. No cost, high value — the single most useful safety download.
    - 💡 Keep phone charged (carry a power bank) and shoes near the bed; after shaking, follow hotel staff/app announcements, don't rush outside during the tremor.
    - 🇮🇳 Most Indian travellers have never felt a real earthquake; brief your family on Drop-Cover-Hold On before the trip and keep the app's alerts sound ON. Locate your hotel's marked evacuation point on arrival.
- **Nankai Trough 'megaquake' advisory — real long-term risk, NOT a reason to cancel** — Headlines about a Nankai Trough 'megaquake' can scare first-time visitors. The facts: Japan's earthquake committee (Jan 2025) puts the chance of a massive Nankai Trough quake at ~80% within the next 30 years — a long-term statistical assessment, not a prediction of any specific date. Japan's first-ever megaquake advisory (Aug 2024, after a M7.1) was lifted after a week with no follow-on event and people told to 'return to normal life.' Official position: the warning does not mean travel is unsafe. Include this to defuse family anxiety rather than to deter the trip.
    - 💴 No cost implication; do not pay for 'earthquake-specific' add-ons beyond a normal comprehensive plan that already covers natural-disaster interruption.
    - 💡 Still do the basics (Safety Tips app, know the drill); preparedness, not cancellation, is the correct response.
    - 🇮🇳 Useful reassurance line for Indian readers whose relatives worry after seeing scary news clips: the advisory is a 30-year preparedness statistic, and Japan is among the world's best-prepared countries for quakes.
- **Winter packing for warm-climate Indians: the 3-layer system, and what NOT to wear** — This is the biggest gap for Indians from warm regions who own no sub-zero clothing. Tokyo/Kyoto/Osaka winters are cold-dry (day 5–12°C, nights near freezing) and manageable with a good coat over layers. Hokkaido/snow country is serious: Sapporo −5 to 0°C, dropping to −16°C (Furano) and below −20°C in spots, with wind chill worse. Use a 3-layer system: (1) base = merino or synthetic thermals (NOT cotton — it holds sweat and chills you); (2) mid = fleece or a light down jacket; (3) outer = waterproof/windproof insulated shell. Critical 'do-not-wear' list for snow: NO jeans (soak up melted snow like a sponge — pack waterproof pants), NO sneakers/trainers or leather shoes (waterlog and slip), NO knitted gloves (get waterproof ski gloves). Add a beanie covering the ears, a neck warmer (better than a scarf), thick wool socks, and — because cold-dry air cracks skin unused to it — moisturiser and lip balm.
    - 💴 Cheap in-Japan fixes: Uniqlo HEATTECH thermals ¥990–1,990 each (≈₹584–1,174); disposable kairo hand/foot warmers ¥30–50 each (≈₹18–30) at any konbini — stick them in pockets and boots.
    - 💡 Waterproof snow boots with grippy soles are only needed for Hokkaido/snow country; for the Tokyo–Kyoto 'golden route' warm water-resistant walking shoes suffice — don't over-buy.
    - 🇮🇳 Pre-buy in India to save money and avoid arriving cold: Decathlon India stocks Wedze ski thermal tops/pants ₹499 (BL100) to ₹999 (BL500) and packable down jackets rated to about −29°C; merino wool thermals regulate heat and beat polyester for smell/warmth. Buy proper waterproof snow boots at home — Indian sizes/stock in Japan can be limited.
- **Don't want to buy heavy gear? Rent snow-wear in Hokkaido/Niseko** — For a one-off winter or ski trip, renting avoids lugging bulky sub-zero clothing you'll never use again in India. Hokkaido/Nagano resorts (Niseko, Kiroro, Rusutsu, Hakuba) rent full 'wear and gear' bundles — jacket, snow pants, plus skis/board/boots/poles — and standalone clothing. Shops like Amuse Rental (Niseko) and Rhythm Japan deliver to your accommodation. Note gloves, goggles and hats aren't always included, so bring your own (or buy cheap) to save money, and size boots up if wearing thick socks.
    - 💴 Full wear-and-gear bundle ~¥8,000/day (≈₹4,720), ~¥13,000 for 2 days (≈₹7,670); individual items ~¥1,000/day (≈₹590) at Kiroro. Bringing your own gloves/hat/goggles cuts the bill.
    - 💡 Book rental online in advance (KKday/Klook/resort site) for peak Dec–Feb; confirm exactly which clothing items are in the bundle.
    - 🇮🇳 For an Indian family doing just 2–3 snow days, renting outerwear at the resort is often cheaper and lighter than buying full sub-zero kit at home — but still pack thermals (HeatTech/Wedze) and gloves yourself, as those are the least-included and most-personal items.
- **Emergency numbers and the 24/7 Japan Visitor Hotline to save offline** — Core numbers: 110 = Police, 119 = Fire and Ambulance (same number for both; say 'kyukyu' for ambulance). For tourists specifically, JNTO runs the Japan Visitor Hotline 050-3816-2787 (from overseas +81-50-3816-2787), 24 hours a day, 365 days a year, covering accidents, illness, natural-disaster information and general tourist help, with support in English, Chinese, Korean and Japanese. Save these plus your insurer's emergency line and the Indian Embassy (Tokyo) offline before you land.
    - 💴 All emergency numbers are free to call; the Visitor Hotline is a standard-rate call (use hotel wifi/VoIP if roaming is costly).
    - 💡 Download the Safety Tips app AND save 110/119/050-3816-2787 as offline contacts — mobile data can drop during major quakes/typhoons.
    - 🇮🇳 Add the Embassy of India, Tokyo and Consulate (Osaka) numbers to your phone; carry a printed copy of passport + visa + insurance in case the phone dies in a disaster.

_Sources: https://livejapan.com/en/in-tokyo/in-pref-tokyo/in-tokyo_train_station/article-a0005317/ · https://www.japanhighlights.com/japan/typhoon-season · https://en.japantravel.com/guide/typhoons-in-japan/60540 · https://www.japanhighlights.com/japan/rainy-season · https://thisis-japan.com/japan-rainy-season-guide-tsuyu-2026/ · https://www.japantimes.co.jp/news/2025/10/02/japan/science-health/japan-record-heat-stroke-2025/ · https://weather.com/news/news/2025-08-31-heat-wave-japan-records-broken · https://www.cnn.com/2025/08/05/asia/japan-record-high-temperatures-intl-hnk · https://arigatojapan.co.jp/earthquake-safety-japan/ · https://www.japantimes.co.jp/japan-disaster-information/earthquake-preparation/ · https://www.jnto.go.jp/safety-tips/eng/index.html · https://www.jnto.go.jp/safety-tips/eng/app.html_


### B18. Multi-generational India→Japan travel (elderly parents + kids): accessibility, logistics, and Indian school-holiday timing
_Japan is one of the most barrier-free countries in Asia, which makes a mixed-mobility Indian family trip (grandparents + kids) far easier than most Indians assume — but only if you plan around three levers the existing research never covers: (1) accessibility mechanics — 90%+ of big-city stations have elevators, station staff give FREE portable ramps and phone ahead to your arrival station, Shinkansen have wheelchair spaces and private compartments you reserve ~2 days out, and you can rent wheelchairs/scooters at Narita/Haneda/Kansai; (2) luggage-forwarding (Yamato TA-Q-BIN, ~¥3,000-3,700 a suitcase) so seniors and kids never haul bags up station stairs; and (3) timing that fits Indian school vacations. The India-specific verdict on timing: the Diwali/autumn break (Oct-Nov) is the single best fit (mild, dry, spectacular foliage, comfortable for seniors); the summer break sweet spot is mid-to-late May (avoid Golden Week's early-May peak; June is rainy — pivot to Hokkaido); and the Dec-Jan winter break unfortunately collides head-on with Japan's New Year shutdown (Dec 29-Jan 3 closures, peak crowds, ice-fall risk for elders) so lean to early-mid December instead. Every family member — child and grandparent alike — needs their own Japan visa (Indians are NOT visa-exempt; an eVISA for up to 90 days tourism now exists)._

- **Barrier-free stations + FREE station-staff ramp assistance (works for both a wheelchair grandparent and a stroller)** — Over 90% of major-city stations are wheelchair-accessible and the vast majority now have elevators (sometimes inconveniently at the far end of the platform). The key service Indians don't know about: tell the station attendant your destination and ask for a 'slope' (ramp) — staff place a free portable ramp to bridge the platform-train gap AND phone ahead to your arrival/transfer stations so assistance is waiting there too. Trains have designated wheelchair spaces (no bench) near the doors, plus priority seats. Almost every station also has a multipurpose accessible toilet sited between the men's and women's toilets, with handrails, a spacious layout and a full-size changing table — the same room serves a wheelchair-using grandparent and a diaper-changing toddler.
    - 💴 Ramp/boarding assistance is completely free. Standard subway/JR ride ¥180-320 (~₹100-180).
    - 💡 Head to the staffed gate/attendant booth on arrival at every station; allow extra time as elevators are often at platform ends. Learn the word 'slope' (ramp).
    - 🇮🇳 Don't count on Indian-Railways-style senior concessions: Japan's disability/senior discounts require the Japanese 'shogaisha techo' resident ID; foreign IDs are generally not accepted and 'train companies never give a discount for foreign ID holders.' Budget full fares.
- **Shinkansen wheelchair-accessible seats & private compartments — reserve ~2 days ahead** — The bullet train offers (a) standard accessible seats where one seat is removed so a wheelchair parks beside a companion's reserved seat, and (b) lockable private compartments (ko-shitsu) that fit a wheelchair plus two companions on a convertible bench/bed — ideal for a tired senior. A wheelchair-accessible toilet sits in the same car. JR recommends booking accessible seats and private rooms roughly two days in advance at a 'Midori no Madoguchi' (green ticket window); some lines now sell reserved seating online, but JR Pass holders must still reserve at the window. Arrive 15 minutes before departure and station staff bring the ramp to board you.
    - 💴 Whole-of-Japan JR Pass (valid through 30 Sep 2026): 7-day adult ¥50,000 (~₹28,500), 14-day ¥80,000 (~₹45,600), 21-day ¥100,000 (~₹57,000); children 6-11 are half price (7-day ~¥25,000 / ₹14,250). Green (first-class) 7-day ¥70,000 (~₹39,900). Pass excludes the fastest Nozomi/Mizuho (supplement applies).
    - 💡 Book the private compartment early — there are very few per train. Reserve at the green window the moment you arrive in Japan for the whole trip.
    - 🇮🇳 For a slow-paced senior itinerary, a 7-day pass typically covers a Tokyo-Kyoto-Osaka-Hiroshima loop; children under 6 generally ride free (no pass needed) if not taking their own reserved seat.
- **Luggage-forwarding (Yamato TA-Q-BIN / takkyubin) — the single biggest multi-gen game-changer** — Japan's door-to-door next-day parcel service lets you send suitcases hotel-to-hotel or airport-to-hotel so grandparents and small kids ride trains hands-free — no wrestling heavy bags up/down station stairs or onto the Shinkansen. Yamato Transport pioneered it and leads the market (competitors: Sagawa, Japan Post). Drop bags at your hotel front desk, an airport counter, a convenience store or a service centre; next-day delivery is standard for most of the country (Hokkaido/Okinawa 2+ days).
    - 💴 Suitcase under 25 kg: ¥3,000 within Greater Tokyo (~₹1,710), Tokyo→Kyoto/Osaka ¥3,200 (~₹1,824), to major airports ¥3,700 (~₹2,109). Smaller parcel under 10 kg: ¥1,500 local (~₹855), Tokyo→Kyoto/Osaka ¥1,700 (~₹969).
    - 💡 Send bags the morning you check out (they arrive next day) and pack a small overnight bag for the gap night. Confirm the receiving hotel can store deliveries — some small ryokan/business hotels can't.
    - 🇮🇳 For a big Indian family with 5-6 large suitcases, forwarding them all for a few thousand yen is trivially worth it versus dragging them through Tokyo/Kyoto transfers with elders and toddlers in tow.
- **Rent a wheelchair or mobility scooter for a senior who tires easily (walks short distances but not all day)** — You don't need a full-time wheelchair user to benefit. 'MoVA by ANA' counters rent from a single day at Haneda (Terminal 2, 1F, 10:00-18:00), Narita (Terminal 1 South Wing, 1F, 6:00-21:00) and Kansai (Terminal 1, 6:00-21:00): manual wheelchairs (AC Next Core), WHILL power chairs, JOYcart mobility scooters and even Aprica strollers. Mobility scooters are recommended for long outdoor sightseeing days and are intuitive via joystick. Getting around door-to-door: Toyota 'JPN Taxi' universal-design cabs with a side ramp are everywhere in Tokyo (fit one wheelchair + one companion), but larger power chairs may not clear the low roof — pre-book a lift-equipped van (providers include Welhire, TokudAw, Wilgo, International Travel Support) for those.
    - 💴 Airport rentals from one day (per-item pricing on provider pages). WHILL power-chair hotel delivery: 4-day minimum and ~10 days' notice.
    - 💡 Book the WHILL hotel-delivery well in advance; for airport pickup, note the different counter opening hours (Haneda closes at 18:00).
    - 🇮🇳 A rented scooter/wheelchair lets an active-but-slow Indian parent keep up with grandchildren at big sites (Fushimi Inari base, Tokyo DisneySea, Osaka Castle grounds) instead of sitting out — reserve before the trip.
- **Family lodging: choose Western beds/accessible rooms over floor futons for elders** — Traditional ryokan (¥15,000-30,000 per person/night incl. kaiseki dinner + breakfast, ~₹8,550-17,100) give a memorable large tatami room where grandparents, parents and kids all sleep together on futons — sociable and culturally rich. BUT sleeping on floor futons and getting up/down is hard on senior knees/hips, and tatami rooms rarely have grab bars. For a mixed-mobility group, default to hotels/ryokan that explicitly offer Western beds, an elevator, and 'barrier-free'/accessible rooms, or book connecting/triple rooms in a Western chain. Onsen ryokan with a private/family bath (kashikiri) avoid the mobility and modesty issues of public baths.
    - 💴 Ryokan ~¥15,000-30,000 pp/night with two meals (~₹8,550-17,100).
    - 💡 Filter hotel sites for 'accessible room' + 'elevator'; for the one 'authentic' ryokan night, pick one with beds or chair-height seating and a private bath.
    - 🇮🇳 Many Indian families are large and prefer staying together — ask specifically for family/connecting rooms and 'bed-type' (not floor) rooms when booking, and flag any wheelchair need in the reservation.
- **TIMING WINDOW 1 — Indian summer break (mid-May to June): aim late May, dodge Golden Week, treat June as rainy** — The Indian summer vacation is the widest family window but spans very different Japan conditions. EARLY MAY = Japan's Golden Week (national holidays Apr 29 / May 3-4-5, Children's Day May 5): the busiest and priciest domestic travel period — trains and hotels book out; avoid. MID-TO-LATE MAY is one of the best times to visit all year: lush greenery, comfortable daytime highs ~17-26°C, and uncrowded once Golden Week ends — genuinely easy on seniors. JUNE brings the rainy season (tsuyu) over most of Japan — overcast, wet and increasingly humid (highs ~21-29°C), which is tiring for elders, though it means fewer tourists and lower prices. Hokkaido is barely touched by tsuyu, making it the smart June choice.
    - 💴 Late May = shoulder pricing (post-Golden-Week dip); June = lower prices due to rain, but Golden Week itself is peak-priced.
    - 💡 Carry compact umbrellas/rain covers for a June trip; slippery station floors are a fall risk for seniors — non-slip shoes.
    - 🇮🇳 Indian school summer dates vary by board/state (North/CBSE roughly mid-May to end-June; much of South India mid-April to end-May) — verify your child's school calendar. The reliable overlap for most Indian families is late May; if only June is free, route the trip to Hokkaido to escape the rain.
- **TIMING WINDOW 2 — Diwali / autumn break (Oct-Nov): the BEST overall fit for an Indian multi-gen trip** — Many schools (especially North India) give a ~1-2 week Diwali/autumn break, and it lands on Japan's finest travel season. October is one of the most pleasant months — warm but no longer hot/humid (highs ~16-28°C). November is dry and mild with spectacular autumn foliage (koyo) across the country. Crowds are generally low-to-moderate except at famous leaf-viewing spots (Kyoto's temples). The comfortable temperatures suit seniors, the colours delight kids and make great family photos, and there's no big domestic-travel shutdown — the strongest alignment of Indian holidays with easy, beautiful Japan conditions.
    - 💴 Moderate pricing; the exception is mid-to-late-November foliage peak in Kyoto, where lodging spikes and sells out early.
    - 💡 Book Kyoto/Nikko/Hakone accommodation months ahead for mid-late-November foliage; days shorten through autumn, so start sightseeing early with elders.
    - 🇮🇳 Diwali shifts on the lunar calendar (it fell ~20 Oct in 2025 and falls ~8 Nov in 2026), so the exact break moves year to year — check the year's date. A Nov-Diwali year lines up even better with peak koyo than an Oct one.
- **TIMING WINDOW 3 — Dec-Jan winter break: clashes with Japan's New Year shutdown — prefer early-mid December** — The Indian winter break (roughly late December to early/mid January, ~2 weeks especially in North India) unfortunately overlaps Japan's New Year (oshogatsu) — one of the country's THREE major domestic travel peaks. Many attractions, restaurants and shops CLOSE from Dec 29 through Jan 3, transport books out, and prices rise; New Year (Dec 29-Jan 4) sees intensive travel. It's also cold (Tokyo highs ~10°C but Sapporo below freezing), with snow/ice in the north that raises slip-and-fall risk for elders, and very short days (Tokyo sunset ~4:30 pm) that shrink sightseeing time. EARLY-TO-MID DECEMBER (before ~26 Dec) is far better: generally dry, low crowds, winter illuminations, and good value.
    - 💴 Early December = low season value; late-Dec-to-Jan-4 New Year = peak crowds and higher prices with widespread closures.
    - 💡 If travelling around Jan 1-3, pre-book restaurants and check attraction New Year closing dates; pack proper winter wear (Indian families often under-pack for Japanese cold).
    - 🇮🇳 Because the Indian winter break peaks exactly over Japan's New Year closures, either travel in early-mid December (leave before the 26th) or, if you must go over New Year, reframe it as a snow/ski trip (Hokkaido, Nagano) that kids love — while weighing icy-surface fall risk for grandparents.
- **India-specific pre-trip logistics: everyone needs their own visa, plus flights & airport assistance for elders** — Indian passport holders are NOT visa-exempt for Japan. An eVISA is now available for residents of India for tourism, single-entry, stays up to 90 days, applied online (expanded to India around 2024). Critical operational detail: on arrival the eVISA must be shown on a mobile device — screenshots, printouts and files are NOT accepted as valid proof — and you must enter by air or scheduled passenger ferry. EVERY traveller needs an individual visa: each child and each grandparent, no exceptions. For elderly comfort, favour a nonstop flight (Delhi-Tokyo is roughly 7.5-8 hours; verify current airlines/routes) over a long layover, and request free wheelchair/airport-assistance from your airline in advance for BOTH the Indian departure airport and the Japanese arrival airport (Narita/Haneda have long corridors).
    - 💴 eVISA fees and any service charges vary by processing route — confirm on the official Japan eVISA portal / Embassy of Japan in India / VFS Global before applying.
    - 💡 I could not load a live Japan-eVISA/embassy page in this session, so verify the current eVISA process, fee and single-vs-multiple-entry rules on the official Embassy of Japan in India / MOFA eVISA site before booking.
    - 🇮🇳 Start the group's visa applications well ahead (a family of 6 = 6 applications); make sure each traveller can display their eVISA on a phone at immigration; pre-arrange airline wheelchair assistance for seniors on both legs.
- **Pacing & practical glue for a mixed grandparents-and-kids group** — Build a base-and-explore itinerary — 2-3 cities (e.g. Tokyo → Kyoto → Osaka) with luggage forwarded between them — rather than daily hotel hops that exhaust elders and toddlers. Plan roughly one major site plus rest per day; the multipurpose accessible toilets in almost every station double as a wheelchair loo and a baby-change room; convenience stores (konbini) are everywhere for water, snacks, and simple meals for fussy kids and seniors. Use the free station-staff assistance and reserved wheelchair/priority seats to make each hop low-stress.
    - 💴 Konbini meals ~¥300-700 (~₹170-400) keep group food costs low between sit-down meals.
    - 💡 Keep a daily 'anchor + rest' rhythm; schedule the slowest sightseeing early when elders have most energy and days are short in autumn/winter.
    - 🇮🇳 Vegetarian/Jain elders and picky kids: carry ready-to-eat Indian meals and snacks — note many konbini onigiri and 'veg-looking' dashi-based dishes contain fish/bonito, so read carefully (Indian veg/food availability is a distinct topic worth its own planning).

_Sources: https://www.accessible-japan.com/ · https://www.accessible-japan.com/shinkansen-wheelchair-accessibility/ · https://www.accessible-japan.com/wheelchair-accessible-trains-and-subways-in-japan/ · https://www.accessible-japan.com/renting-wheelchairs-hoists-japan/ · https://www.accessible-japan.com/accessible-taxis-tokyo/ · https://www.accessible-japan.com/japan-disability-discounts/ · https://www.accessible-japan.com/wheelchair-accessible-travel-in-japan-general-information/ · https://www.japan-guide.com/e/e2278.html · https://www.japan-guide.com/e/e2361.html · https://www.japan-guide.com/e/e2273.html · https://www.japan-guide.com/e/e2280.html · https://www.japan-guide.com/e/e2029.html_


### B19. Medications you can't bring into Japan (Yakkan Shoumei) + travel insurance + pharmacies
_Japan bans several active ingredients that sit inside everyday Indian OTC and prescription medicines: pseudoephedrine (in many Indian cold/sinus/allergy combos and in Sudafed/Actifed/Vicks inhalers) and codeine (in India's best-selling cough syrups like Corex and Phensedyl, and some painkillers) are prohibited even with a prescription, and multiple governments confirm travellers have been detained, jailed and deported for carrying them. Amphetamine-based ADHD meds (Adderall) are outright banned; methylphenidate (Ritalin/Concerta) and atomoxetine (Strattera) need advance permission. You may hand-carry up to a 1-month supply of prescription medicine and up to a 2-month supply of OTC medicine for personal use; beyond that (or for anything narcotic/stimulant, injectables like EpiPens/insulin syringes, or CPAP) you must obtain a Yakkan Shoumei (import certificate, aka yunyu kakuninsho) in advance from Japan's MHLW. Carry a doctor's letter plus meds in original packaging. Japanese healthcare is world-class but you pay 100% upfront as a tourist, and single events run into millions of yen (JNTO cites ¥7.5M for a collapsed-lung surgery and ¥10M for a heart attack, roughly ₹42-56 lakh), so comprehensive travel insurance is effectively essential though not legally mandatory for the Japan e-visa. On the ground, well-stocked drugstore chains (Matsumoto Kiyoshi, Welcia, Sundrug, Tomod's, Don Quijote, Cocokara Fine) sell Japan-legal equivalents, and JNTO's guide gives a free multilingual OTC-search tool, the 119 ambulance number and the 24/7 Japan Visitor Hotline 050-3816-2787._

- **The two ingredients that get Indian travellers detained: pseudoephedrine and codeine** — Japan bans pseudoephedrine and codeine outright. Wikivoyage names the exact banned products - 'pseudoephedrine (Actifed, Claritin-D, Sudafed, Vicks inhalers) and codeine' - and the UK FCDO Japan health advice lists 'Vicks inhalers, allergy and sinus medications, cold and flu drugs containing pseudoephedrine, and over-the-counter painkillers with codeine,' warning that 'foreign nationals have been detained and deported for these offences.' Canada's government advice states these 'cannot be brought into the country even with a prescription.' The catch for Indians is that the banned molecule hides inside familiar brands: many Indian cold/sinus tablets are pseudoephedrine combos, and India's top-selling cough syrups (Corex, Phensedyl, Codistar and similar codeine-linctus products) contain codeine. It is the active ingredient - not the brand - that is illegal, so read the composition on the strip/bottle before packing.
    - 💴 No fee - but the cost is confiscation at customs plus possible detention/deportation; 'ignorance is not an excuse' (Wikivoyage/Smartraveller).
    - 💡 Leave these at home and buy a Japan-legal equivalent at any drugstore on arrival (see the pharmacies item). If you medically need a codeine/pseudoephedrine product, you must get a Yakkan Shoumei first.
    - 🇮🇳 Check the label of any Indian cold/allergy tablet (Sudafed, Actifed, D'Cold, Sinarest, Cheston Cold-type combos) for 'pseudoephedrine' and any cough syrup/painkiller for 'codeine'/'codeine phosphate' - if present, do NOT carry it. Vicks VapoRub balm is fine; it is the Vicks nasal INHALER stick that is flagged.
- **Banned/restricted stimulants, ADHD meds, and injectables (Adderall, Ritalin, EpiPens)** — Amphetamine and methamphetamine are absolutely prohibited: Canada's advisory explicitly lists 'Adderall and amphetamines' and 'methamphetamines' as banned even with a prescription, and Japan Customs bans 'stimulants, psychotropic substances and other narcotic drugs.' Wikivoyage clarifies the nuance for ADHD patients: 'amphetamine is completely prohibited; methylphenidate (Ritalin/Concerta) and atomoxetine (Strattera) may require permission,' and 'EpiPens and other drug-filled syringes may need authorization.' Cannabis and all CBD/THC products are banned. Strong prescription painkillers (narcotic analgesics) need a Yakkan Shoumei regardless of quantity.
    - 💴 No fee for the permission itself; penalty for carrying banned stimulants is criminal - jail and deportation.
    - 💡 For anything narcotic, psychotropic or a stimulant, start the paperwork months ahead - this is a separate, stricter track than the ordinary Yakkan Shoumei.
    - 🇮🇳 Indian travellers on ADHD medication (methylphenidate is sold in India as Inspiral/Addwize/methylphenidate) cannot carry Adderall at all, and should apply for a narcotic/psychotropic import certificate for methylphenidate well in advance. Diabetics carrying insulin syringes and anyone carrying an EpiPen should list them and be ready to show a doctor's letter.
- **Yakkan Shoumei: when you need it and the quantity thresholds** — Yakkan Shoumei (import certificate; also written yunyu kakuninsho / 'import confirmation') is Japan's advance permission to bring in medicine. You do NOT need it for personal-use amounts under the limits: Canada's government advice and Japan Customs put the thresholds at up to a 1-month supply of prescription medicine and up to a 2-month supply of non-prescription/OTC medicine (external-use items and cosmetics are limited to ~24 pieces/applications). You DO need a Yakkan Shoumei if you exceed those amounts, or for any quantity of narcotics/psychotropics/stimulants, injectables (syringes/needles, EpiPens), or medical devices like CPAP machines. Apply in advance to Japan's Ministry of Health, Labour and Welfare (MHLW) / the Regional Bureau of Health and Welfare covering your entry airport (Narita and Haneda fall under the Kanto-Shinetsu bureau); the official forms and the current contact address are on the MHLW pharmaceuticals import page linked from Japan Customs.
    - 💴 The certificate itself is free of charge.
    - 💡 Canada's advisory says to apply 'several months prior to arrival' for larger supplies/narcotics; even the routine certificate can take days, so email the bureau at least 2-4 weeks before you fly. I could not open the MHLW page live (it blocks automated fetches), so confirm the exact current e-mail/fax address and PDF form directly on mhlw.go.jp before applying rather than relying on a third-party email.
    - 🇮🇳 For a normal 1-2 week India-Japan holiday with ordinary meds within the limits, most travellers need NO certificate - the certificate is mainly for long stays, large quantities, or restricted drugs.
- **Carry a doctor's letter and keep meds in original packaging** — Even for allowed quantities, bring documentation. Canada's government guidance says to carry 'medical documentation from your doctor stating your name, address, reason for use and dosage,' keep 'the original prescription medication in its container,' and be ready to provide 'a detailed listing of the contents.' The UK advice adds: if unsure about a medicine's legal status, 'check its status with the Japanese Embassy' before travelling.
    - 💴 No cost - just your GP's time for a signed letter/prescription copy.
    - 💡 Photograph the letter and the drug packaging composition panel and keep copies on your phone and email as backup.
    - 🇮🇳 Ask your Indian doctor for a typed, signed prescription/letter in English with the generic (molecule) names, not just Indian brand names, since customs officers identify drugs by active ingredient. Keep tablets in their original strip/box with the composition printed, not loose in a pill organiser.
- **Buy Japan-legal equivalents at drugstores - what to look for** — Rather than risk banned ingredients, buy replacements after you land. Japan has dense drugstore/pharmacy chains - Matsumoto Kiyoshi, Welcia, Sundrug, Tomod's, Cocokara Fine and the discount chain Don Quijote (Donki) - most in shopping districts and stations, many open late. JNTO's official medical guide provides a free 'OTC medication search' tool (search by symptom, body part or product name) and advises you 'consult a pharmacist or registered sales clerk if necessary.' Common, widely available Japanese OTC brands include EVE, Loxonin S and Bufferin (pain/fever), Pabron and Lulu (cold), Allegra FX and Claritin EX (allergy - non-pseudoephedrine formulations), Rohto (eye drops), Salonpas (pain-relief patches) and Ohta's Isan/Seirogan (stomach). Note that Japanese OTC doses are often milder than Indian ones.
    - 💴 Typical OTC items run roughly ¥600-¥1,500 (about ₹340-₹850); many drugstores offer tax-free (10% off) for tourists on purchases over ¥5,000 - carry your passport. (Prices indicative; I could not open a live price list.)
    - 💡 Use Google Translate camera on the box, or point at the JNTO OTC-search result; staff at big-city Donki/Matsukiyo often have some English and multilingual signage.
    - 🇮🇳 Indians who rely on codeine cough syrup or pseudoephedrine decongestants will find legal Japanese cold/allergy medicines at any Matsumoto Kiyoshi/Welcia - just show the pharmacist your symptoms via the JNTO tool or a translation app.
- **Why travel insurance is effectively essential - Japan's medical bills are enormous** — Japan does not make travel insurance a legal condition of the tourist e-visa for Indians, but every government advisory treats it as essential. Uninsured/foreign patients 'are responsible for paying 100% of their medical fees' (Wikipedia, Healthcare in Japan), and hospitals often require foreigners to 'pay in advance or provide a document proving the bill will be paid prior to discharge' (Canada). The numbers are eye-watering: JNTO's official guide cites real examples of ¥7.5 million for pneumothorax (collapsed-lung) surgery and ¥10 million for heart-attack treatment - roughly ₹42 lakh and ₹56 lakh (about USD 48,000 and USD 64,500) at 2025-26 rates. The UK advice warns hospitals 'may check your insurance, which could delay your treatment.'
    - 💴 ¥7.5M pneumothorax surgery ≈ ₹42 lakh (~$48k); ¥10M heart-attack care ≈ ₹56 lakh (~$64.5k). Rate used ¥1≈₹0.56 (₹1≈¥1.8, 2025-26, volatile).
    - 💡 Compare on Policybazaar and buy the highest medical-and-evacuation cover you can - the premium difference between USD 50k and USD 250k cover is small versus a ₹50 lakh bill. Confirm the policy covers cashless claims in Japan and lists a Japan assistance helpline.
    - 🇮🇳 Get an India-issued travel policy before you fly. Indian insurers (Tata AIG, ICICI Lombard, Bajaj Allianz, HDFC Ergo, Digit, Reliance, Care) all sell Japan/Asia plans; Bajaj Allianz's Japan page lists tiers of USD 50,000 / 100,000 / 200,000 / 500,000 with premiums 'starting at ₹13/day' and worldwide cashless hospitalisation plus emergency evacuation. Given the ¥7.5-10M examples, choose at least USD 100,000-250,000 medical cover with evacuation.
- **Practical safety net: emergency numbers, on-arrival insurance, and the Visitor Hotline** — Keep the essentials handy. Ambulance/fire is 119 across Japan (Wikivoyage/Smartraveller). JNTO runs a 24/7 multilingual Japan Visitor Hotline on 050-3816-2787 (English, Chinese, Korean) for medical help, and its site has a searchable hospital/clinic finder filterable by area, language and department. JNTO also notes travellers can buy travel insurance even after arriving in Japan, with 'coverage of up to ¥10 million' plus interpretation and cashless-payment services - a useful fallback, though pre-departure cover from India is cheaper and broader.
    - 💴 119 and the 050-3816-2787 hotline are free to call; on-arrival Japanese policies cover up to ¥10M (~₹56 lakh).
    - 💡 For non-emergencies use a drugstore or the JNTO clinic finder first; dial 119 only for genuine emergencies. Ask for a hospital that accepts foreign travel insurance to use cashless service and avoid paying the full bill upfront.
    - 🇮🇳 Save 050-3816-2787 and your India insurer's 24/7 assistance number in your phone before landing; if you skipped insurance in India, buy an on-arrival Japanese travel policy rather than travelling uninsured.
