_Sources: https://www.customs.go.jp/english/summary/passenger.htm · https://www.gov.uk/foreign-travel-advice/japan/health · https://travel.gc.ca/destinations/japan · https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Japan · https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_system_in_Japan · https://www.jnto.go.jp/emergency/eng/mi_guide.html · https://www.bajajgeneralinsurance.com/travel-insurance-online/japan-travel-insurance.html · https://www.mhlw.go.jp/english/policy/health-medical/pharmaceuticals/01.html_


### B20. Arrival day: Visit Japan Web (immigration + customs QR) and which Indian foods, spices and items you can or can't bring into Japan
_Visit Japan Web (vjw.digital.go.jp) is NOT just a tax-refund tool — it is Japan's official pre-arrival portal for the immigration arrival card AND the customs declaration, generating the QR code(s) every arriving Indian scans at the airport (from 2026, a single joint-kiosk QR at Narita/Haneda/Kansai). It is technically optional (paper forms are still accepted) but is the fast lane and the thing to pre-fill before you fly. The India-specific trap is food. Japan bans ALL meat and meat products with zero personal-use exemption — that means mutton/chicken achaar, meat pickle, homemade non-veg leftovers, biryani, kebabs, ham/salami sandwiches, even in-flight meal leftovers — enforced by sniffer dogs and X-ray, with penalties up to 3 years jail or a fine up to 3,000,000 yen (~17.7 lakh INR; up to 50,000,000 yen ~2.95 crore INR for businesses). Most fresh fruit, vegetables and plants (mango, chillies, curry leaves, fresh ginger, citrus) are also prohibited under plant quarantine without a phytosanitary certificate. What IS fine for Indian travellers: dried, commercially-sealed spices (turmeric, cumin, chilli powder, garam masala, curry powder) in reasonable personal quantities; dry cooked snacks like theplas, khakhra, ganthiya, mathri; sealed ready-to-eat packets (MTR, Haldiram's); and dairy (cheese, butter, milk, ghee). Declare food honestly in the VJW customs section; the ban is about biosecurity (foot-and-mouth, African swine fever), not taste. Note: Indians also need a visa/e-visa BEFORE boarding — there is no visa-on-arrival. Rates/figures verified for July 2026 (1 yen ~= 0.589 INR)._

- **Visit Japan Web is the mandatory-in-practice immigration + customs QR flow, not a tax-refund gimmick** — Visit Japan Web (VJW) is the Japanese government's official pre-arrival portal that handles two things every arriving traveller must do: (1) the immigration 'arrival card' (disembarkation card) and (2) the customs baggage declaration. It generates the QR code(s) you scan at the airport. It is run by Japan's Digital Agency at vjw.digital.go.jp (landing page vjw-lp.digital.go.jp). For 2026, Narita, Haneda and Kansai (KIX) have rolled out a 'Joint Kiosk' that scans your single VJW QR + passport in one step, replacing the old two-counter flow; smaller airports still use two separate QR scans (one for immigration, one for customs). It is free and takes ~15-20 minutes. Tax-free shopping registration is an ADD-ON inside VJW, which is why some people wrongly think that is all it does.
    - 💴 Free. Official site only (vjw.digital.go.jp) — avoid third-party sites that charge a 'service fee'.
    - 💡 Complete it at least 6 hours before landing so the QR activates. You can add up to 10 people (family/kids each get their own QR) under one account. SCREENSHOT / download the QR codes so they work offline. Have your hotel's name + address and flight number ready — VJW asks for your first-night address in Japan.
    - 🇮🇳 Beta/blog readers flying from Delhi/Mumbai/Bengaluru/Chennai/Hyderabad should register while they still have reliable wi-fi at home — airport wi-fi after a long flight is patchy and you do not want to be filling forms in the immigration queue.
- **The customs declaration lives INSIDE Visit Japan Web — declare food honestly; sniffer dogs and X-ray enforce it** — The VJW customs-declaration screen asks whether you are carrying meat, plants, fruit, vegetables, or other restricted goods. This is the same declaration you would otherwise fill on the paper customs form on the plane. Japan Customs and the Animal/Plant Quarantine services use detector (sniffer) dogs, X-ray machines and visual baggage checks at Narita/Haneda/KIX to catch undeclared food. If you declare a prohibited item, it is simply confiscated and destroyed on the spot (usually no penalty). If you HIDE it and it is deemed a 'malicious' non-declaration, that is when the criminal penalty applies. There is a separate Animal Quarantine counter and Plant Quarantine counter you must visit BEFORE the main customs line if you are carrying anything questionable.
    - 💴 Confiscation is free of penalty if declared; concealment risks up to 3 years jail or up to 3,000,000 yen fine.
    - 💡 Paper arrival + customs forms are still handed out on the plane and accepted at every airport, so VJW is optional — but the digital QR lane is meaningfully faster during the 8-11 AM arrival rush at major hubs.
    - 🇮🇳 The honest-declaration route is genuinely the safe one for Indians unsure whether their sealed masala or snack packet qualifies — declare it, let the officer decide; worst case they bin it, they do not fine an honest traveller.
- **TOTAL ban on all meat and meat products — this is the biggest India-specific trap (mutton/chicken achaar, non-veg leftovers, biryani, kebabs)** — Japan prohibits ALL meat and meat products with NO personal-use or small-quantity exemption: beef, pork, mutton/goat, chicken and other poultry — raw, frozen, cooked, cured, dried or processed. Explicitly banned: ham, sausage, salami, bacon, beef/any jerky, meat buns/nikuman, gyoza and dumplings with meat, ham/chicken sandwiches, AND 'meat pickles' and dried meat. Critically for Indians: mutton pickle / chicken achaar / meat achaar, homemade non-veg curries, biryani or kebabs, and even leftover in-flight non-veg meals all fall under the ban. The ONLY legal way to bring meat is with an official inspection (phytosanitary/veterinary) certificate from the exporting government's agency — which no ordinary traveller has — and even airport duty-free meat products count. Reason: preventing foot-and-mouth disease, African swine fever and avian influenza (the FMD virus survives 6+ months frozen, so 'but it's frozen/cooked' does not help).
    - 💴 Penalty for illegal import: imprisonment up to 3 years OR a fine up to 3,000,000 yen for individuals (~17.7 lakh INR at ~0.589 INR/yen, July 2026); up to 50,000,000 yen (~2.95 crore INR) for businesses.
    - 💡 If a relative packed you home food and you are not 100% sure it is meat-free and plant-quarantine-safe, declare it in VJW and let the quarantine officer clear or bin it — do not risk concealment.
    - 🇮🇳 This is the single most common Indian packing mistake. Achaar/pickle is beloved cabin baggage, but any pickle containing meat (chicken/mutton/prawn) is flatly illegal. Homemade parathas or theplas are fine ONLY if vegetarian and contain no meat, egg-based issues aside. Egg products: whole eggs are restricted, but portable dairy is OK (see separate item).
- **Fresh fruit, vegetables and plants are banned under plant quarantine — including staples Indians carry (mango, chillies, curry leaves, fresh ginger)** — Most fresh fruit and vegetables cannot be brought into Japan without a phytosanitary (plant health) certificate from the exporting country. Officially named prohibited fresh items include mangoes, longans, guavas, lychees, citrus fruits, pears, apples, peaches, plums, apricots, cherries, nectarines, quinces, red peppers/chillies and sweet potatoes. This sweeps up common Indian carry-alongs: fresh mangoes, fresh green/red chillies, fresh curry leaves (kadi patta), fresh coriander/cilantro, fresh ginger root, and fresh turmeric root. Soil, and plants with soil on the roots, are also banned outright. The purpose is blocking agricultural pests such as the oriental fruit fly.
    - 💴 Same penalty regime as meat: up to 3 years jail or up to 3,000,000 yen (~17.7 lakh INR) fine; confiscation/destruction if declared.
    - 💡 There is a dedicated Plant Quarantine counter at arrival; canned or fully processed fruit/veg and most dried items are exempt from the fresh-produce ban.
    - 🇮🇳 The fresh-vs-dried line is what trips Indians up: dried/powdered forms of these same spices are generally fine, but the FRESH root or leaf (fresh ginger, fresh curry leaves, fresh green chilli, fresh coriander) is prohibited. Leave fresh produce at home; buy fresh at Indian grocers in Japan (e.g., in Tokyo/Nishikasai 'Little India') instead.
- **What Indians CAN safely bring: dried sealed spices, dry cooked snacks, sealed ready-to-eat packets, and dairy** — Legal and low-hassle for Indian travellers: (1) DRIED, commercially-packaged spices and blends — turmeric powder, cumin, chilli/red-chilli powder, black pepper, garam masala, curry powder, sambar/rasam powder — in reasonable quantities for personal use, ideally in original sealed retail packaging for easy inspection. (2) DRY cooked snacks with no meat — theplas, khakhra, ganthiya, mathri, chakli, roasted namkeen, biscuits — cooked and dry, so low pest/disease risk (carry-on preferred so they don't get crushed). (3) SEALED, shelf-stable ready-to-eat vegetarian packets (e.g., MTR, Haldiram's, Gits) — processed and sterilised, generally accepted. (4) DAIRY — cheese, milk, cream, butter (and ghee) are NOT subject to animal quarantine and may be brought as personal baggage (processed cheese up to ~10 kg for personal use). Dried fruit and nuts are typically fine as they are processed.
    - 💴 No duty on reasonable personal-use food quantities; no certificate needed for dried, sealed, plant-based spice packets.
    - 💡 If you keep spices in original sealed packs and quantities look personal (not wholesale), a small-quantity food declaration is usually not even required — but declaring costs you nothing.
    - 🇮🇳 This is the reassuring 'you can still eat Indian on day one' message: pack dry masalas, theplas/khakhra, MTR-type packets and ghee, not fresh produce or meat. Keep everything factory-sealed with the label showing — homemade unlabelled powders invite extra questioning.
- **Achaar / pickle nuance: veg achaar is usually fine, meat achaar is banned, and pickle counts as a 'liquid' in cabin baggage** — Pickle deserves its own note because Indians almost always pack it. Vegetarian achaar (mango, lime, mixed-veg, chilli) that is commercially processed, oil-preserved and sealed is generally allowed as a processed food and is low-risk (the fruit/veg is cooked/cured, not fresh). Meat/non-veg achaar (chicken, mutton, prawn/fish pickle) is PROHIBITED under the meat ban — no exceptions. Separately, security/airline rules treat pickle (and any oil/gravy) as a LIQUID, so in carry-on it must respect the 100 ml liquids rule; larger jars belong in checked baggage (well-sealed against leaks and pressure).
    - 💴 No customs duty on personal-use veg pickle; meat pickle triggers the confiscation/penalty regime.
    - 💡 Double-bag pickle jars and place them upright in the middle of checked luggage — cabin pressure makes oily achaar leak.
    - 🇮🇳 Rule of thumb for the blog: 'Veg achaar in a sealed jar in checked bags — usually OK. Any meat/chicken/prawn pickle — leave it home, it is illegal in Japan.' Homemade achaar made from FRESH mango/chilli that still has raw fruit could draw plant-quarantine scrutiny; commercially bottled is safest.
- **Customs duty-free allowances and cash rules you declare on the same VJW customs screen** — Beyond food, the VJW/paper customs declaration also covers duty-free limits for arriving travellers (age 20+): alcohol — 3 bottles of ~760 ml each; tobacco — 200 cigarettes OR 50 cigars OR 250 g of tobacco (or a combination up to 250 g); perfume — 2 ounces. General goods/gifts (excluding the above) are duty-free up to a total overseas market value of 200,000 yen (~1.18 lakh INR); any single item under 10,000 yen (~5,900 INR) is always duty-free and not counted toward that 200,000-yen total. Exceeding the allowance means duty of roughly 10-30% on the excess. Travellers under 20 get NO alcohol or tobacco allowance.
    - 💴 Duty-free: 3 bottles alcohol, 200 cigarettes, gifts up to 200,000 yen (~1.18 lakh INR); items under 10,000 yen (~5,900 INR) never counted. 1 yen ~= 0.589 INR (July 2026).
    - 💡 If you are gifting electronics or jewellery you bought abroad, keep the total under 200,000 yen or be ready to declare and pay duty; the sniffer-dog/X-ray checks that catch food also catch undeclared high-value goods.
    - 🇮🇳 Large cash amounts: bringing in currency/instruments over the equivalent of ~1,000,000 yen must be separately declared (standard Japan Customs rule — verify the exact threshold before you fly). Relevant if you carry forex cash for a family trip.
- **Before any of this: Indians need a visa/e-visa BEFORE boarding — there is no visa-on-arrival for Japan** — Indian passport holders MUST obtain a Japan tourist visa before travelling; there is no visa-on-arrival and you cannot board without an approved visa. Japan's e-Visa (digital, no passport sticker) is available to Indians for short-term single-entry tourism (stay up to 30 days), applied through accredited channels (VFS Global / an accredited Japanese travel agency). As of 2026 the e-Visa is issued via the four launch missions: Embassy of Japan New Delhi and Consulates-General in Mumbai, Bengaluru and Chennai. Passport must have at least 6 months validity beyond the intended stay. Typical e-Visa decision ~3-5 working days (standard route 5-7 days).
    - 💴 Reported single-entry tourist visa fee around 1,300 INR (verify current fee + VFS service charges with VFS/embassy before applying).
    - 💡 Apply the e-Visa well ahead, then do Visit Japan Web after your flight + hotel are booked. Two different systems, both needed: e-Visa = permission to travel; VJW QR = the arrival/immigration/customs paperwork.
    - 🇮🇳 This is genuinely arrival-relevant: carry proof of your approved e-Visa (many travellers keep a printout/screenshot) alongside your Visit Japan Web QR — airlines check the visa at the India-side boarding gate, and immigration checks both on arrival.
- **Medicines: quantity limits matter for Indians carrying regular / prescription / ayurvedic medication** — Japan limits how much medication a traveller can bring without an import certificate (Yakkan Shoumei): over-the-counter/regular medicines up to about a 2-month supply; prescription medicines up to about a 1-month supply; external-use medicines and cosmetics up to 24 pieces per item of normal size. Some ingredients common in Indian OTC/cold medicines and stimulants are controlled in Japan, so anything beyond the personal limits — or containing restricted substances — needs advance approval.
    - 💴 No duty for within-limit personal medication; exceeding limits requires a Yakkan Shoumei certificate (apply in advance, free).
    - 💡 If your trip is longer than a month and you take daily prescription medication, apply for a Yakkan Shoumei before departure to avoid it being held at customs.
    - 🇮🇳 Indians often carry a full trip's worth of prescription meds plus ayurvedic/homeopathic remedies and CBD/hemp products — note that non-THC-free CBD oil is prohibited. Carry a doctor's prescription/letter for prescription drugs and keep them in original packaging.

_Sources: https://www.customs.go.jp/english/c-answer_e/sonota/9006_e.htm · https://www.customs.go.jp/english/c-answer_e/sonota/9007_e.htm · https://www.maff.go.jp/e/policies/ap_health/animal/240904.html · https://www.maff.go.jp/pps/j/pqaqinfo_en.html · https://www.customs.go.jp/english/c-answer_e/keitaibetsuso/7105_e.htm · https://www.vjw.digital.go.jp/manual/main/visitjapanweb_manual_en.html · https://mileasia.com/japan/visit-japan-web/ · https://livejapan.com/en/article-a0002825/ · https://www.canibringto.com/can-i-bring/spices-to-japan/ · https://blog.wego.com/visit-japan-web/ · https://www.klook.com/blog/visit-japan-web-guide/ · https://happyfares.in/blog/japan-visa-for-indians-2026-complete-guide/_


### B21. Power, plugs and charging Indian devices in Japan (Type A sockets, 100V vs India's 230V, what won't work)
_Japan runs on 100V, the lowest standard voltage of any developed country, through flat two-pin Type A sockets (and occasionally three-pin Type B). India runs on 230V through round-pin Type C/D/M plugs, so two mismatches hit every Indian traveller at once: the plug won't physically fit, and the voltage is less than half. A cheap plug adapter fixes the shape, but it does NOT change voltage. The good news is that almost every modern Indian phone, laptop, tablet, camera and power-bank charger is already dual-voltage (look for "INPUT: 100-240V, 50/60Hz" on the brick) and works in Japan with just a plug adapter. The trouble is single-voltage 230V-only heating gadgets, hair dryers, straighteners, curling irons, immersion rods, travel kettles and cheaper trimmers/shavers, which on 100V run weak, heat slowly/unevenly, or don't work, and can be damaged. Practical playbook: read every device's INPUT label before packing, carry one Type A/universal adapter bought cheaply in India (roughly Rs 459-649 on Amazon.in), leave 230V-only heat appliances at home (most Japanese hotels, even business hotels, supply a hair dryer), and remember replacements are cheap and everywhere in Japan (adapters from 100-2,000 yen; a decent hair dryer under 5,000 yen)._

- **The double mismatch: Type A flat pins + 100V vs India's round pins + 230V** — Japan uses Type A sockets (two flat parallel pins, same shape as the US) and, less commonly, Type B (two flat pins plus a round earth pin, seen near kitchens and in newer buildings). India uses round-pin Type C, D and M plugs. So an Indian plug physically will NOT fit a Japanese wall socket, you need a plug adapter without exception. On top of that, Japan's mains is 100V while India's is 230V, described by MailMate as 'the lowest standard voltage of any developed country'. Two separate problems: shape (fixed by an adapter) and voltage (an adapter does NOT fix this).
    - 💴 Plug adapter is the only mandatory buy; a basic one is 100-500 yen (~Rs 59-295) in Japan or Rs 459-649 in India.
    - 💡 Buy the adapter in India before you fly so you can charge the moment you land; you can always top up with more in Japan.
    - 🇮🇳 Indian homes mostly have Type C/D/M round-pin sockets at 230V; nothing you own will plug into a Japanese wall directly. Carry at least one adapter from home so your first night (jet-lagged, phone at 5%) isn't spent hunting for a shop.
- **An adapter changes the plug SHAPE, not the VOLTAGE, the #1 confusion** — Every source stresses this: 'A plug adapter changes only the shape of the plug. It does NOT change voltage' (Real Japan Guide / MailMate). Only a voltage converter (step-down transformer) actually lowers 230V to 100V. For dual-voltage gadgets you never need a converter; for 230V-only heat appliances a plain adapter is not enough and can even be unsafe. Most travellers should skip heavy converters entirely and instead only bring dual-voltage devices.
    - 💴 Cheap plug adapter: 100-2,000 yen. A proper step-down voltage converter is bulky and costs far more (travel transformers on Amazon JP run around $30/~4,400 yen), usually not worth it for a holiday.
    - 💡 If you catch yourself needing a converter for one hair dryer, it's cheaper and lighter to just use the hotel's dryer or buy a 100V one in Japan.
    - 🇮🇳 Indian travellers often assume the universal adapter they bought also 'converts' voltage, it doesn't. If a device is 230V-only, an adapter alone will underpower it on Japan's 100V.
- **What works fine on just an adapter: dual-voltage (100-240V) chargers** — Devices whose label reads 'INPUT: 100-240V, 50/60Hz' are dual-voltage and safe anywhere in the world, needing only a plug adapter. This covers almost all modern phone chargers, laptop/USB-C PD bricks, tablet chargers, camera battery chargers, power banks, smartwatches and earbuds. Per power-plugs-sockets.com this INPUT rating means 'the device can be used in every country in the world.' USB-C shape alone doesn't guarantee it, always read the INPUT line, not the connector.
    - 💴 No extra cost beyond the adapter, these just work.
    - 💡 Pack a small multi-port USB-C charger + one adapter so a whole family can charge from a single wall socket.
    - 🇮🇳 Apple, Samsung, OnePlus, Xiaomi, Dell, HP, Lenovo chargers sold in India are virtually all 100-240V, so your phone and laptop are fine. A 65W USB-C PD charger is a great one-brick solution for an Indian traveller.
- **What underperforms or fails: single-voltage 230V-only heat appliances** — The real casualties are single-voltage 230V devices, especially anything with a heating element or motor: hair dryers, straighteners, curling irons, cheaper trimmers/shavers, and (very Indian) immersion water-heating rods, travel kettles and small irons. On Japan's 100V these 'may run weak, heat badly, or not work as expected' (multiple guides); low voltage 'can lead to overheating and a reduced lifespan.' A 230V, 1800W dryer gets under half its power at 100V, so it barely warms up. Real Japan Guide warns not to guess safety from a voltage percentage because 'heating elements, motors, thermostats and electronic controls behave differently.'
    - 💴 No fix without a heavy step-down converter; cheaper to buy a 100V unit locally (see below).
    - 💡 Leave 230V-only heat gadgets at home. If you must groom, a USB-rechargeable trimmer (charges off any 100-240V USB brick) sidesteps the whole problem.
    - 🇮🇳 Indians frequently pack an immersion rod / travel kettle for chai and Maggi and a 230V hair dryer, these are exactly the items that won't perform on 100V. Budget trimmers/shavers bought in India are often 230V-only too; check before assuming.
- **Read the INPUT label before you pack, the 30-second check that saves the trip** — On the charger brick or the appliance's rating plate, find the line marked 'INPUT' or 'AC INPUT'. '100-240V, 50/60Hz' = dual voltage, safe in Japan with only an adapter. '220-240V' or '230V' only = single voltage, will underperform/fail on Japan's 100V. Do this for every device you plan to bring, especially hair tools and grooming gadgets, before it goes in the suitcase.
    - 💴 Free, just look at the label.
    - 💡 Snap a phone photo of each INPUT label so you can double-check at the store if you decide to buy a replacement in Japan.
    - 🇮🇳 Indian device labels print the same INPUT format. Phones/laptops usually pass; the ones to scrutinise are hair dryers, straighteners, trimmers and any kitchen heater.
- **Frequency too: Japan is split 50Hz (east) / 60Hz (west)** — Beyond voltage, Japan's frequency differs by region: 50Hz in eastern Japan (Tokyo, Yokohama, Tohoku, Sapporo, Hokkaido) and 60Hz in western Japan (Nagoya, Osaka, Kyoto, Hiroshima, Fukuoka, Shikoku, Kyushu). India is a uniform 50Hz. For phone/laptop chargers this is irrelevant (they accept 50/60Hz). It only matters for old mains-timed clocks and some motorised gadgets, a minor concern for tourists but worth a line since you'll cross the 50/60Hz divide if you do Tokyo + Osaka/Kyoto.
    - 💴 No cost impact for normal travellers.
    - 💡 Don't overthink frequency; focus your effort on the 100V-vs-230V voltage issue instead.
    - 🇮🇳 An Indian-standard 50Hz device won't be harmed moving between Tokyo and Osaka; this is a footnote, not a blocker.
- **Buy the adapter cheap in India before you fly** — A universal/Type A travel adapter is easy to get in India. On Amazon.in, universal travel adapters start around Rs 459 (MRP ~Rs 999) and Rs 649 (MRP ~Rs 1,199); Ambrane sells universal adapters from Rs 799 up to Rs 5,999 for 150+ country models with USB/USB-C ports. Get one with built-in USB-A + USB-C ports so it doubles as a multi-charger.
    - 💴 Amazon.in universal adapters ~Rs 459-649 budget; Rs 799-5,999 for premium multi-port (Ambrane).
    - 💡 Pick one with multiple sockets + USB ports so a couple/family charges everything from one Japanese outlet; carry a spare, they're tiny.
    - 🇮🇳 Buying in India is cheaper and lets you charge immediately on landing. A US-style dual-flat-pin adapter also works since Japan's Type A = US shape.
- **Cheap replacements are everywhere in Japan, and hotels supply dryers** — If you forget an adapter or want a 100V hair tool, Japan makes it painless. Airport arrival shops sell basic adapters for 800-1,500 yen (~Rs 470-885); Bic Camera, Yodobashi, Don Quijote and Daiso 100-yen shops sell adapters from ~100-2,000 yen (~Rs 59-1,180), universal ones 1,000-3,000 yen (~Rs 590-1,770). Note: Daiso stocks Japan-domestic plugs, buy foreign-to-Japan travel adapters at electronics stores or the airport. A decent 100V hair dryer is under 5,000 yen (~Rs 2,950) with budget brands Tescom/Koizumi cheaper than Panasonic. Crucially, most Japanese hotels, even business hotels, provide a hair dryer, so you often need not carry one at all.
    - 💴 Adapter 100-3,000 yen (~Rs 59-1,770); budget 100V hair dryer <5,000 yen (~Rs 2,950); Panasonic nanoe EH-NA9F-RP ~25,740 yen (~Rs 15,200, Nov 2025). Dyson is famously cheaper in Japan: Airstrait HT01VLP 51,030 yen / 46,391 tax-free (~Rs 27,400-30,100), Supersonic HD17VLP 54,670 yen / 49,700 tax-free (~Rs 29,300-32,300) at Bic Camera.
    - 💡 Check your hotel listing for 'hair dryer' before packing one. If buying appliances, carry your passport for tax-free and ask for the Bic Camera / Don Quijote tourist coupon.
    - 🇮🇳 Tourists get tax-free shopping (bring your passport); Bic Camera adds up to ~10% off and Don Quijote coupons up to 17% on top of tax-free, making a Japan-bought 100V dryer/straightener a genuine souvenir-grade deal for Indians. A Japan-bought device is 100V-only, so it won't work back home on 230V, buy it only if you'll use it there or gift it.

_Sources: https://www.power-plugs-sockets.com/in/japan/ · https://mailmate.jp/blog/japan-voltage · https://real-japan-guide.com/japan-power-adapters-charger-guide-2026/ · https://allblogs.in/post/japan-plug-adapter-voltage-guide-indian-travelers · https://esim.holafly.com/travel-tips/japan-plug-type/ · https://www.klook.com/blog/japan-power-plug-socket/ · https://plughopper.com/travel-adapter-guide/japan-travel-adapter-guide · https://www.amazon.in/japan-travel-adapter/s?k=japan+travel+adapter · https://ambraneindia.com/collections/universal-travel-adapter · https://livejapan.com/en/in-kansai/in-pref-osaka/in-namba_dotonbori_shinsaibashi/article-a2000776/ · https://www.fun-japan.jp/en/articles/14339 · https://www.bookmyforex.com/currency-converter/jpy-to-inr/_


### B22. Honeymoon & romantic-couples Japan: private-onsen ryokan, romantic itineraries, and Indian pre-wedding photoshoots
_Japan is one of the fastest-growing high-spend honeymoon and pre-wedding-shoot markets for Indian couples, yet the blog has no couples-focused content. This dimension is fully serviceable with concrete, current (2025-26) facts. The romantic spine is the Tokyo -> Hakone -> Kawaguchiko (Mt Fuji) -> Kyoto arc, anchored by ryokan with in-ROOM private open-air onsen (crucial for Indian couples: Japan's public/shared onsen are strictly gender-separated and require full nudity, so a couple can only bathe together in an in-room rotenburo or a rentable kashikiri private bath). Real named picks exist at every price tier: Hakone (Gora Kadan, Tensui Saryo, Hakone Airu; private-onsen ryokan from ~USD 280/night with kaiseki), Lake Kawaguchi (Kozantei Ubuya, from ~USD 568/night, every room faces Fuji; Kawaguchiko Fufu), and Kinosaki Onsen (Nishimuraya Shogetsutei, Yuraku, Tsubakino) as a Kansai add-on near Kyoto. The India pre-wedding photoshoot market is real and priced: studio data-only plans from ~JPY 35,200 (~Rs 20,700) up to independent on-location premium specialists at JPY 300,000-550,000 (~Rs 1.77-3.24 lakh), with a firm 15-20% cherry-blossom/autumn surcharge, weekend surcharges, and JPY 5,000-50,000 location-permit/facility fees at parks, castle grounds and shrines (Meiji Jingu / Heian Jingu allow only fixed photo spots). The icon backdrop is Chureito Pagoda (Arakurayama Sengen Park, ~650 cherry trees) with Fuji framed by sakura in April or gold foliage in November; other named spots: Lake Kawaguchi mirror reflections, Oishi/Iyashino-no-Sato thatched village, Tanuki Lake. Couple experiences: Lake Ashi pirate-ship cruise (clearest Fuji 09:30-11:00, upgradeable private cabin), Fuji-view kaiseki dinners, Owakudani ropeway. India logistics are all confirmable: e-visa live for Indians via JATA agents (~5-7 working days, embassy fee Rs 1,300 single-entry), 7-8h direct DEL/BOM-Tokyo flights ~Rs 48,000-70,000 return, 7-day JR Pass JPY 50,000 (~Rs 29,500) rising to JPY 53,000 from 1 Oct 2026, JPY-INR ~0.589 (Jul 2026). Note: many ryokan publish "rates change constantly" and hide fixed prices, so exact per-night figures need live booking-site checks; USD-INR conversions below assume ~Rs 86/USD and are approximate._

- **The romantic 7-day spine: Tokyo -> Hakone -> Kawaguchiko (Fuji) -> Kyoto** — The backbone of nearly every first-time romantic Japan itinerary. Tokyo (modern + Meiji Shrine, Harajuku, a Michelin dinner) 2 nights; Hakone for the first ryokan-with-private-onsen night, Hakone Open-Air Museum and a Lake Ashi cruise; Kawaguchiko/Fuji Five Lakes for a Mt-Fuji-view room and the pre-wedding photoshoot; then Kyoto for hand-in-hand Arashiyama bamboo grove, a tea ceremony and kimono-clad Gion strolls. Each stop is deliberately different (neon city / hot-spring mountains / Fuji lakes / old-Japan romance). Couples wanting a 10-14 day version add Osaka and a Kinosaki Onsen detour off Kyoto.
    - 💴 Self-planned mid-range: ~Rs 1.2-2.5 lakh per person for 6-8 days; luxury >Rs 3 lakh pp. Packaged Indian honeymoon 7N/6D from ~Rs 1.55 lakh pp (mid) up to ~Rs 2.99 lakh pp (premium, Shinkansen + guided).
    - 💡 Book Fuji-area nights for a clear-weather window (autumn/winter mornings give the crispest Fuji); keep the ryokan night mid-trip so the photoshoot day can flex if Fuji is cloud-covered.
    - 🇮🇳 Sold widely as a honeymoon package by PickYourTrail, Thrillophilia, Thomas Cook India; DIY typically saves money vs package for couples comfortable with self-navigation.
- **Hakone: in-room private open-air onsen ryokan (couple's first-night pick)** — Named honeymoon ryokan with an in-room rotenburo so couples can bathe together (Japan's shared baths are gender-separated): Gora Kadan (former Imperial-family villa, open-air onsen in-room, kaiseki), Tensui Saryo (5-star, upper-floor rooms have Hakone mountain views + in-room onsen, kaiseki dinner), Hakone Airu (every room has an attached open-air hot-spring bath; choose sushi / steak / shabu-shabu dinner), and Hakone Retreat Villa by Onko Chishin (forest-immersion, private onsen in room). Where an in-room bath isn't available, look for a rentable kashikiri (private-bath) slot.
    - 💴 Hakone ryokan with private onsen from ~USD 280/night (~Rs 24,000) for two with breakfast + kaiseki dinner; Fuji-view rooms with open-air bath ~USD 320-600/night (~Rs 27,500-51,500).
    - 💡 Hakone is ~85-90 min from Tokyo, making it the easy first ryokan night; several top ryokan don't publish fixed rates ('prices change all the time'), so quote via Booking.com/official site for your exact dates.
    - 🇮🇳 Kaiseki is seafood/meat-heavy; vegetarian/Jain Indian couples should request a shojin-ryori (Buddhist vegetarian) or veg kaiseki set at booking, not on arrival.
- **Lake Kawaguchi: wake up to Mt Fuji from your bath (the money shot)** — For the iconic Fuji-from-your-room honeymoon moment, Lake Kawaguchi beats Hakone for guaranteed Fuji framing. Kozantei Ubuya - every guest room faces Mt Fuji across Lake Kawaguchi, rooms available with private open-air baths; the marquee choice. Kawaguchiko Fufu - small luxury ryokan where ALL rooms have open-air hot-spring baths with Fuji views. Both curated on the Selected Onsen Ryokan luxury list.
    - 💴 Kozantei Ubuya (Dec-2025 rates via aggregator): Superior ~USD 568/night (~Rs 49,000), Standard ~USD 830 (~Rs 71,000), Deluxe ~USD 1,342 (~Rs 1.15 lakh). Kawaguchiko Fufu - fixed rate not published; quote direct.
    - 💡 Ask specifically for a Fuji-facing (lake-side) room with the in-room rotenburo - not every category has both; morning is the clearest Fuji window.
    - 🇮🇳 Kawaguchiko doubles as the pre-wedding photoshoot base, so an Indian couple can pair the Fuji-view ryokan night with the shoot day and save a transfer.
- **Kinosaki Onsen: the quieter romantic add-on near Kyoto** — An atmospheric hot-spring town (couples stroll in yukata between seven public bathhouses) reachable from Kyoto/Osaka - ideal to extend a Kansai leg romantically. Named couple-friendly ryokan: Nishimuraya Hotel Shogetsutei (three private onsen spas each with rock sauna + lounge and complimentary sparkling wine), Yuraku Ryokan (secluded hillside, many rooms with private outdoor baths + forest views), Kinosaki Onsen Ryokan Tsubakino (rated 9.7 by couples, private + public baths), and Shinonomeso.
    - 💴 Rates vary by ryokan/season; Tsubakino and peers list live 2025-26 rates on Booking.com - quote for exact dates.
    - 💡 Best as a 1-2 night detour off the Kyoto leg; winter (snow + Tajima crab kaiseki) is the signature romantic season.
    - 🇮🇳 Key rule for Indian couples: the town's public bathhouses AND most in-ryokan baths are separated male/female - to soak together you MUST book a room with its own open-air bath or a kashikiri private bath.
- **Signature couple experiences beyond the ryokan** — Lake Ashi (Hakone) pirate-ship sightseeing cruise past the vermilion 'Peace Torii' of Hakone Shrine with Mt Fuji behind - a 25-40 min crossing between Togendai / Hakone-machi / Moto-Hakone; upgrade to a special/first-class cabin for exclusive deck access and a more private couple experience. Pair with the Hakone Ropeway over Owakudani's volcanic valley. Fuji-view kaiseki dinners are built into the Kawaguchiko/Hakone ryokan stays. Kyoto adds tea ceremony, Arashiyama bamboo grove and kimono rental in Gion.
    - 💴 Lake Ashi cruise is a paid ticket (often bundled in the Hakone Free Pass); special/first-class cabin is a modest upgrade over standard fare - buy at the pier or online.
    - 💡 Board the 09:30-11:00 morning cruise for the clearest Fuji; autumn and winter mornings give the best odds of an unclouded peak.
    - 🇮🇳 Cruise and ropeway are covered by the Hakone Free Pass, which is separate from a nationwide JR Pass - budget it separately.
- **India pre-wedding photoshoot in Japan: real packages and prices** — A booming segment. Two structures exist. (1) Studios with kimono/gown wardrobe + hair/makeup - e.g. Studio Palette: Data-Only plan JPY 35,200 (2 outfits, hair/makeup, 80-100 digital photos); Data & Album JPY 71,500 (adds 20-cut album); full on-location day plans JPY 462,000-580,800. (2) Independent creative photographers - e.g. Shinji Morita (Tokyo): Minimal 90-min 1-location JPY 98,000; 2-hour 2-location JPY 155,000; Half-day (4h, 3-4 locations) JPY 255,000; All-day (7h) JPY 355,000; add cinematic 3-min film JPY 155,000, second photographer JPY 150,000, album JPY 66,000-125,000. Marketplaces: OneThreeOneFour, Decollte/DE & Co., Wedding Avenue.
    - 💴 Converted at JPY-INR 0.589 (Jul 2026): studio data-only ~Rs 20,700; data+album ~Rs 42,000; Shinji Morita minimal ~Rs 57,700, half-day ~Rs 1.5 lakh, all-day ~Rs 2.1 lakh. Independent premium international specialists JPY 300,000-550,000 (~Rs 1.77-3.24 lakh); entry-level talent JPY 150,000-250,000 (~Rs 88,000-1.47 lakh).
    - 💡 Book 8-12 months ahead for sakura/autumn; expect a firm 15-20% peak-season surcharge and USD 150-300 weekend/public-holiday surcharge. Do a separate 'maedori' shoot day (not on any event day) to reach quiet spots without a crowd.
    - 🇮🇳 Confirm the photographer/coordinator is English-speaking (often a JPY/USD add-on ~USD 200-300) since Hindi/regional-language support is rare; many studios include kimono AND western-gown looks in one session, popular for Indian couples wanting both.
- **Photoshoot permits, restrictions and the best sakura/autumn backdrops** — Commercial/pre-wedding shoots are NOT free-for-all. Public parks, castle grounds and traditional gardens 'almost always' charge a facility/permit fee, and famous shrines (Meiji Jingu in Tokyo, Heian Jingu in Kyoto) allow only fixed, designated photo spots - negotiate access before you book. The icon Fuji backdrop is Chureito Pagoda at Arakurayama Sengen Park (Fujiyoshida) - a five-story vermilion pagoda framing Mt Fuji, ~650 cherry trees, magical with sakura in April and gold foliage in November. Other named Fuji spots: Lake Kawaguchi (mirror-reflection of Fuji on still days), Iyashino-no-Sato/thatched-roof villages, Tanuki Lake (quiet reflections), Fujinomiya Road panoramas. Kyoto: Arashiyama bamboo and Gion lanes.
    - 💴 Location permit / facility fees typically JPY 5,000-50,000 (~Rs 2,950-29,500) on top of the photographer fee; staff meals JPY 5,000-10,000/person; out-of-Tokyo travel JPY 35,000-65,000.
    - 💡 Verify social-media/publication rights in writing (venues increasingly restrict posting), and lock the permit before confirming the shoot date, as access rules become 'almost impossible to change' after.
    - 🇮🇳 Chureito Pagoda involves ~400 steps and gets very crowded in sakura season - plan an early-morning slot; the pagoda-plus-Fuji-plus-sakura frame is the single most-requested Indian pre-wedding shot.
- **India logistics: visa, flights, rail pass and money (2026)** — Visa: Japan's e-visa is live for Indian passport holders (launched Apr 2024) for short-term single-entry tourism via JATA-accredited travel agencies through the Delhi, Mumbai, Bangalore and Chennai consulates - fully online, ~5-7 working days, >95% approval with complete docs, up to 90-day stay; multi-entry still uses the regular process. Flights: nonstop DEL/BOM-Tokyo (Narita/Haneda) on ANA, Japan Airlines and Air India, ~7-8h. Rail: nationwide 7-day JR Pass Ordinary is JPY 50,000 (~USD 330) now, rising ~5-6% to JPY 53,000 from 1 Oct 2026 (buy before that date to save; official-site price is protected, agency price rises).
    - 💴 Embassy visa fee Rs 1,300 single-entry / Rs 2,600 multi-entry (agents may add VFS service charges); direct return flights ~Rs 48,000-70,000 (peak sakura higher); 7-day JR Pass JPY 50,000 ~Rs 29,500 (~Rs 31,200 from Oct 2026). JPY-INR ~0.589 as of 24 Jul 2026 (roughly Rs 1 = 1.7 yen).
    - 💡 Book flights 8-12 weeks ahead for peak sakura (late Mar-mid Apr); cheapest fares are around February. Verify the live JPY-INR rate at booking as it moves daily.
    - 🇮🇳 Apply via a JATA-registered Indian tour operator for the e-visa; couples on the compact Tokyo-Hakone-Kyoto arc often DON'T need a nationwide JR Pass (regional Hakone Free Pass + point-to-point Shinkansen can be cheaper) - run the math before buying.

_Sources: https://www.studio-palette.com/bridal/english-bridal/499796.html · https://shinjimoritaphotography.com/price.html · https://getmarriedinjapan.com/wedding-photographers-in-japan/ · https://www.onethreeonefour.com/blog/japan-tokyo-wedding-photoshoot-mt-fuji/ · https://www.onethreeonefour.com/japan-cherry-blossoms-pre-wedding-photography · https://chureito-pagoda-guide.com/ · https://www.wanderlustdesigners.com/ryokan-in-hakone-with-private-onsen/ · https://selected-ryokan.com/ryokan/kozantei-ubuya_mtfuji-lake-kawaguchi_yamanashi_tokyo-suburbs.html · https://www.tripadvisor.com/Hotel_Review-g1165976-d1167601-Reviews-Ubuya-Fujikawaguchiko_machi_Minamitsuru_gun_Yamanashi_Prefecture_Koshinetsu_Chubu.html · https://www.sakuramobile.jp/blog/japan-travel-guide/mt-fuji-ryokan-with-private-onsen/ · https://sugoii-japan.com/kinosaki-onsen-ryokan · https://www.booking.com/hotel/jp/kinosaki-onsen-tsubakino-ryokan.html_


### B23. Nightlife beyond the izakaya: Golden Gai, standing bars, whisky/cocktail bars, rooftop bars and clubbing (Tokyo & Osaka) for Indian travellers
_Japan's after-dark scene runs far deeper than izakaya, and it splits into five distinct experiences that suit younger Indian travellers with different budgets. (1) The atmospheric micro-bar alleys — Shinjuku's Golden Gai (200+ shoebox bars, 5-6 seats each) and the smoky, lantern-lit Omoide Yokocho — are cheap-to-mid, cash-only, and full of character. (2) Tachinomi (standing bars) are the true budget play: highballs, beer and sake from ¥300-500 (₹180-295), no cover, drink-and-move-on. (3) Japanese-whisky and craft-cocktail temples (Zoetrope, Bar High Five, Bar Benfiddich, Punch Room) are where the yen's weakness vs the rupee makes world-class pours a relative bargain. (4) Rooftop bars (The Roof at Shibuya Sky, Park Hyatt's New York Bar) are the splurge-with-a-view. (5) Clubbing centres on Shibuya (WOMB, ZEROTOKYO), Roppongi and Osaka's Amerikamura/Shinsaibashi, with ¥1,500-3,500 (₹885-2,065) doors. TWO things every Indian first-timer must internalise: the otoshi/seat charge (¥500-2,000, a legitimate table fee + small snack, NOT a scam) and the last train (~00:00-01:00), which dictates the entire shape of a night — miss it and you're committed to taxis, a night bus, a manga cafe or partying until the ~05:00 first train. Kabukicho/Roppongi touts and the "bottakuri" overcharging scam are the one real hazard. Exchange rate used: 1 JPY ≈ 0.59 INR (₹0.59), late July 2026; so ¥1,000 ≈ ₹590._

- **Shinjuku Golden Gai — 200+ shoebox bars, the atmospheric heart of Tokyo's bar scene** — A six-alley maze of ~200 tiny post-war bars, most seating just 5-6 people, each with its own theme (jazz, punk, horror films, manga). Foreigner-friendly, English-menu picks that get named repeatedly: Albatross (a three-floor bar with a chandelier, 7pm-5am), Bar Araku (a 'currency wall' where travellers pin banknotes from home — bring an Indian ₹ note), Ace's (fluent-English bartenders, transparent pricing, no hidden cover), 'Not Suspicious' (no table charge, English cocktail menu), and The Open Book (a standing lemon-sour bar lined with books, 8pm-midnight). Golden Gai red. does a flat ¥1,000 (₹590) for international visitors. Best window is 9pm-midnight for atmosphere without crush.
    - 💴 Budget ¥2,000-3,000 (₹1,180-1,770) per bar: a seat/cover charge of ¥500-1,500 (₹295-885, includes a small otoshi snack) plus 1-2 drinks at ¥600-1,200 (₹355-710) each. A 3-4 bar night ≈ ¥6,000-10,000 (₹3,540-5,900) per person. Bars with 'No Cover' signs outside skip the seat fee.
    - 💡 Look for a printed English cover-charge notice on the door before entering so the otoshi never surprises you. Enter early-ish (before 10pm) to actually get a stool; many bars have a one-drink minimum plus time limit when busy.
    - 🇮🇳 Almost all bars are CASH ONLY — carry ¥1,000/¥5,000 notes; there's a 7-Eleven ATM near the Shinjuku side that takes Indian cards. It is considered rude to sit without ordering at least one drink. Always ask before photographing bars/people. Each bar is one owner's living room — one drink and move on is normal.
- **Omoide Yokocho ('Memory Lane' / 'Piss Alley') — smoky yakitori-and-highball alley after dark** — A narrow, paper-lantern-lit lane by the JR tracks on Shinjuku's west side, counters seating barely six, crowd spilling into the passage. It's the classic smoke-and-skewers scene — grilled chicken hearts, skewers, simmered tofu — and it comes alive at night, some stalls open into the early morning. The Japanese highball (whisky + soda) is the drink to order here; it pairs with the grill smoke and is cheap.
    - 💴 Highballs ¥400-600 (₹235-355); skewers priced 'for pocket change' (roughly ¥150-400 / ₹90-235 each). A satisfying graze-and-drink stop runs ¥2,000-3,000 (₹1,180-1,770).
    - 💡 Combine it with Golden Gai (both are a 10-min walk apart in Shinjuku) for one 'old-Tokyo bar alley' night before your last train.
    - 🇮🇳 Very smoky and tightly packed — fine for a vivid 30-45 min stop, less so for a long sit. Mostly cash. Vegetarian Indians: options are limited (grilled corn, shishito peppers, edamame, tofu) so eat mains elsewhere and come here to drink and soak up atmosphere.
- **Tachinomi (standing bars) — the genuine budget move: ¥300-500 drinks, no cover, no reservations** — 'Tachinomi' literally means 'standing drinking' — no seats, no table minimum, no reservation; you stand at the counter or spill onto the street, drink fast, and move on. This is the cheapest good alcohol in Tokyo, and where you drink shoulder-to-shoulder with local salarymen. Strong clusters exist across Tokyo and Osaka (Osaka's are legendary — the streets east of Dotonbori). Osaka example: Akagakiya (Sennichimae) does yakitori + whisky highballs standing.
    - 💴 House wine ~¥300 (₹180), beer ~¥400 (₹235), sake ~¥350 (₹205); highballs and draft ¥500-800 (₹295-470). A full evening bar-hopping tachinomi ≈ ¥4,000-8,000 (₹2,360-4,720). In 2026, ¥500 (₹295) highballs are still easy to find here.
    - 💡 Pay-as-you-go at many spots (you settle each round, or drop coins in a tray). Don't nurse one drink for an hour — the etiquette is drink, chat, move on. A 'cheap-drinks' Tokyo night crawl of 2-3 tachinomi is the value-maximising plan.
    - 🇮🇳 The single best-value way for a budget-conscious Indian traveller to drink in Japan. Usually no otoshi/cover (that's the point). Many are cash-only. Great low-pressure place to actually talk to locals.
- **Japanese-whisky bars — Zoetrope, Bar High Five: world-class pours made a relative bargain by the weak yen** — Tokyo has serious whisky temples. Zoetrope (Nishi-Shinjuku) holds 300+ Japanese whiskies including rare Karuizawa/Ibuki bottles, owner Atsushi Horigami plays silent films on the wall — ¥600 (₹355) seat charge. Bar High Five (Ginza basement, ~8 counter seats, master Hidetsugu Ueno) ranks among the world's best. Butler (Ginza) is a 10-seat shot bar with house highballs on Dewar's 12. Osaka: Moonshine Whisky Bar (Dotonbori) for a vast JP + international list. A 30ml pour of standard Japanese whisky runs ¥800-1,500 (₹470-885); premium single casks far higher.
    - 💴 Zoetrope: ¥600 (₹355) seat charge; three premium pours often ¥5,000-10,000 (₹2,950-5,900) total — called 'notably fair for bottles of this level in Tokyo'. Bar High Five: ~¥2,000 (₹1,180) cover, drinks ~¥2,000 (₹1,180) each.
    - 💡 Tell the bartender your taste (peaty? sweet? light?) and let them pour — it's the point of these bars. Factor the seat/cover charge in from the start; it is standard, not a rip-off.
    - 🇮🇳 Because 1 JPY ≈ ₹0.59, pours of Yamazaki/Hibiki/Nikka that would be absurdly priced in India are attainable here — this is genuinely one of the best whisky-value cities on earth right now. Dress smart-casual, no track pants. Reservations help at High Five.
- **Craft-cocktail bars — 2025 World's 50 Best names sit right in Tokyo** — Four Tokyo bars made the World's 50 Best Bars 2025 list: Bar Benfiddich (Shinjuku, #9 — Hiroyasu Kayama forages herbs and mills his own bitters, apothecary-style, Tokyo's highest-ranked bar), Virtù (Four Seasons Otemachi, #18), Punch Room (Ginza, inside The Tokyo Edition, #36 — shared punch bowls reimagined with Japanese spirits), and Bar Libre (Ikebukuro, #49). Star Bar (Ginza, mixologist Hisashi Kishi) is another benchmark for the precise, no-nonsense 'Ginza-style' cocktail.
    - 💴 Expect cocktails ¥2,000-3,000 (₹1,180-1,770) plus a seat charge of ¥1,000-2,000 (₹590-1,180) at this tier. A two-cocktail visit ≈ ¥5,000-8,000 (₹2,950-4,720).
    - 💡 Book ahead for Benfiddich and Virtù. If you want the experience without the reservation stress, hotel bars like Punch Room are the easiest entry point for foreigners.
    - 🇮🇳 These are quiet, refined rooms — smart-casual dress, no loud groups, phones down. A great splurge night for a couple. Cards accepted at hotel bars (Virtù, Punch Room); the independents may still be cash-preferred.
- **Rooftop bars — The Roof at Shibuya Sky and Park Hyatt's New York Bar (the 'Lost in Translation' bar)** — The Roof at Shibuya Sky sits at the highest point in Shibuya with a panoramic night skyline (Tokyo Tower + Skytree in view); sofa seats rotate hourly and web tickets bundle drinks ('Seat & 2 Drinks' or 'Seat & Champagne' sets). Park Hyatt Tokyo's New York Bar (52nd floor, the Lost in Translation bar) has floor-to-ceiling windows and live jazz. More wallet-friendly rooftops: TRUNK(HOTEL) Yoyogi Park (cocktails from ¥1,500 / ₹885).
    - 💴 Hotel sky-bar cocktails ¥2,500-5,000 (₹1,475-2,950). New York Bar adds a ¥2,500 (₹1,475) music cover after 8pm. The Roof works on paid timed seat+drink sets. Budget option: rooftops with drinks under ¥1,000-1,500 (₹590-885).
    - 💡 For the view at a fraction of the bar price, do the Shibuya Sky observation deck ticket instead of/before the bar. New York Bar's cover is waived if you're a hotel guest.
    - 🇮🇳 The big splurge-with-a-view night — ideal for a honeymoon/anniversary. Book The Roof's timed web ticket in advance (it sells out). Dress smart at Park Hyatt. Great for non-heavy-drinkers too since you're paying for the view.
- **Clubbing — Shibuya (WOMB, ZEROTOKYO), Roppongi, and Osaka's Amerikamura/Shinsaibashi** — Shibuya is the club epicentre: WOMB is the landmark four-floor techno/house club (huge mirror-ball main room, 20+ only). ZEROTOKYO (Kabukicho) and Sel Octagon (Roppongi) are the other big rooms; WARP Shinjuku rounds out the list. Roppongi skews international/hip-hop but has the most touts. Osaka's scene is Amerikamura & Shinsaibashi — CLUB JOULE, AMMONA — plus Namba clubs, all noted as welcoming to international visitors.
    - 💴 WOMB door ¥1,500 (₹885) Mon-Thu, ¥3,500 (₹2,065) Fri/Sat; advance/flyer often ¥3,000 (₹1,770). Pulling up the event's digital flyer at the door can shave the price. Drinks ¥500-800 (₹295-470); lockers ¥300-600, cloak ~¥500. Some doors do NOT include a drink ticket.
    - 💡 Clubs peak 01:00-03:00 — AFTER the last train — so a club night usually means committing to stay until ~05:00 first train or taxiing home. Save the event flyer to your phone for the door discount.
    - 🇮🇳 Carry your PASSPORT — 20+ ID is checked at the door (India's legal drinking age doesn't apply; it's 20 in Japan). Avoid sportswear/track jackets/caps (common refusal reason). Don't count on advertised 'passport discounts' — one reviewer reported WOMB not honouring its own foreigner discount.
- **Osaka after dark — Dotonbori/Namba neon, cheap standing bars, and a strong whisky culture** — Osaka's nightlife concentrates around Dotonbori, Namba, Soemoncho and Amerikamura. It has an even stronger tachinomi (standing bar) culture than Tokyo — glasses of beer/highball for ¥300-500 (₹180-295) with a reliably local crowd in the lanes east of Dotonbori. Whisky is big too (Nikka, Suntory, regional craft): Moonshine Whisky Bar (Dotonbori) and specialist Namba bars, with 30ml pours ¥800-1,500 (₹470-885). Clubs: CLUB JOULE, AMMONA (Shinsaibashi).
    - 💴 Standing-bar drinks ¥300-500 (₹180-295); whisky pours ¥800-1,500 (₹470-885); club doors broadly similar to Tokyo (¥1,500-3,500 / ₹885-2,065).
    - 💡 Do a Dotonbori standing-bar crawl on foot — the density means you can hit 3-4 spots in a compact area without transport between them.
    - 🇮🇳 Osaka is friendlier, louder and cheaper than Tokyo for a first Japanese night out, and locals are famously chatty — the standing bars are the easiest place to be pulled into conversation. Mostly cash in the small bars.
- **The otoshi / seat charge decoded — the ¥500-2,000 'surprise' that is NOT a scam** — At most sit-down bars and izakaya you'll be served a small unrequested dish (otoshi / tsukidashi) — nuts, edamame, a bit of stew — and charged ¥300-1,500 (₹180-885) for it. Many bars (and virtually all of Golden Gai) also levy a separate seat/table/cover charge (seki-ryo / charge). Together these are how micro-bars survive in expensive Tokyo; the otoshi effectively functions as a table charge with a snack. It is legitimate and universal — not a tourist rip-off.
    - 💴 Otoshi ¥300-1,500 (₹180-885); Golden Gai seat/cover ¥500-1,500 (₹295-885); high-end cocktail/whisky bars ¥1,000-2,000 (₹590-1,180). You cannot usually decline the otoshi.
    - 💡 Tachinomi (standing bars) and 'No Cover' Golden Gai bars skip this entirely — the budget route if you resent it. There is NO tipping in Japan, ever, which offsets the charge.
    - 🇮🇳 This trips up almost every first-time Indian visitor expecting the drink price to be the whole bill. Read it as a ~₹300-900 'entry fee' per bar. The genuine scam to fear is different (see the bottakuri item) — a normal ¥700 otoshi is not it.
- **The last train (~00:00-01:00) shapes the whole night — and the after-midnight escape options** — Tokyo's trains STOP around midnight and don't restart until ~05:00, so the last train ('shuden', 終電) is the single biggest decision of a night out. Sample last departures: JR Yamanote ~00:26 weekdays / ~00:56 Fri-Sat; Tokyo Metro Ginza Line ~00:21 / ~00:51; Toei Oedo (runs latest) ~00:30 / ~01:00. First trains ~05:00-05:30. If you miss it: taxi (20% night surcharge 22:00-05:00, typical ¥2,000-8,000 / ₹1,180-4,720; use the GO or JapanTaxi app), a limited night bus (¥400-600 / ₹235-355, e.g. Shibuya-Roppongi-Shimbashi), a manga/net cafe booth (¥1,500-2,500 / ₹885-1,475 with reclining seat + shower), karaoke overnight 'free-time' plan, or a capsule/love hotel (¥3,000-6,000 / ₹1,770-3,540).
    - 💴 Night taxi ¥2,000-8,000 (₹1,180-4,720); night bus ¥400-600 (₹235-355); manga cafe ¥1,500-2,500 (₹885-1,475); capsule/rest room ¥3,000-6,000 (₹1,770-3,540).
    - 💡 Set a phone alarm ~30 min before your last train; guides advise leaving the bar 20-30 min early to walk to the station and change lines. Staying near Shibuya/Shinjuku for club nights removes the whole problem.
    - 🇮🇳 Plan the night around this: either (a) end by ~23:40 and catch the last train, or (b) commit to staying out until ~05:00 (clubs and many Shibuya/Roppongi bars stay open till first train). Check YOUR line's exact last time on Google Maps before you start drinking — it varies by station and is earlier on weekdays.
- **The one real hazard: Kabukicho/Roppongi touts and the 'bottakuri' overcharging scam** — Tokyo is extremely safe, but two districts — Kabukicho (Shinjuku) and Roppongi — are the documented exceptions. English-speaking street touts pose as friendly guides and lead you to bars/'clubs' that then hit you with enormous, invented bills ('bottakuri') and intimidate you into paying; drink-spiking targeting both men and women has been reported in Roppongi. Never follow a tout. Every reputable venue in this research (Golden Gai bars, WOMB, whisky/cocktail bars) posts clear pricing.
    - 💴 Bottakuri victims can be forced to pay tens of thousands to hundreds of thousands of yen (i.e. tens of thousands of rupees) for a couple of drinks. Legitimate venues show cover/otoshi upfront.
    - 💡 Golden Gai, tachinomi, and named cocktail/whisky bars are effectively tout-free and safe. If a 'deal' is being pushed at you on the street in Kabukicho or Roppongi, it's the scam — keep walking.
    - 🇮🇳 This is the ONE thing to be genuinely careful about as a visitor. Rule: never enter a bar because someone on the street invited you in; only go to places you chose from reviews/this list with visible prices. If overcharged or threatened, walk to the nearest koban (police box) or dial 110 — police handle nightlife complaints professionally. Don't leave drinks unattended; don't accept open drinks from strangers.
- **India-specific money, cash and drinking-age essentials for a Japan night out** — Practical logistics that shape an Indian traveller's night: (1) The yen is weak vs the rupee (1 JPY ≈ ₹0.59 in late July 2026), so Japan's premium whisky/cocktail scene is unusually affordable right now. (2) Nightlife runs heavily on CASH — small bars, tachinomi, Golden Gai and Omoide Yokocho are cash-only; hotel rooftop bars and big clubs take cards. (3) Legal drinking age is 20 (higher than parts of India) and passport ID checks are real at clubs.
    - 💴 Reference conversions at ₹0.59/¥: ¥500≈₹295, ¥1,000≈₹590, ¥1,500≈₹885, ¥3,500≈₹2,065, ¥5,000≈₹2,950, ¥10,000≈₹5,900. Rate moves — check before you travel.
    - 💡 Budget a realistic ¥6,000-12,000 (₹3,540-7,080) per person for a proper multi-bar Tokyo night including a taxi home; a tachinomi-only night can be under ¥5,000 (₹2,950). Keep ~¥3,000 (₹1,770) cash in reserve for a taxi/manga-cafe if you miss the last train.
    - 🇮🇳 Withdraw yen from 7-Eleven or Japan Post ATMs (they reliably accept Indian Visa/Mastercard/RuPay-International); most other ATMs reject foreign cards. Carry a mix of ¥1,000 and ¥5,000 notes for bar-hopping. Vegetarian/Jain travellers: bar snacks (otoshi) may contain fish stock/bonito — stick to edamame, corn, tofu, and eat mains at a veg-friendly spot before drinking. No tipping anywhere.

_Sources: https://www.magical-trip.com/media/golden-gai-a-complete-guide-to-shinjukus-hidden-bar-paradise-for-foreign-visitors/ · https://tokyocheapo.com/food-and-drink/quick-guide-golden-gai/ · https://nightlifetokyo.com/en/tokyo/guides/tachinomi-tokyo-guide · https://planjapansmart.com/cheap-drinks-tokyo-nightlife/ · https://www.timeout.com/tokyo/news/4-tokyo-bars-named-in-the-worlds-50-best-bars-2025-list-071625 · https://mailmate.jp/blog/a-journey-to-the-best-whisky-bars-in-tokyo · https://www.tripadvisor.com/Restaurant_Review-g14133673-d3249203-Reviews-Zoetrope-Nishishinjuku_Shinjuku_Tokyo_Tokyo_Prefecture_Kanto.html · https://japan.stripes.com/food-drink/best-rooftop-bars-in-tokyo-%E2%80%93-ultimate-guide.html · https://www.shibuya-scramble-square.com.e.apy.hp.transer.com/sky/the-roof/ · https://tokyocheapo.com/entertainment/music-and-nightlife/womb-shibuya-club/ · https://us.trip.com/toplist/tripbest/tokyo-best-clubs-100900005328/ · https://www.magical-trip.com/media/ultimate-guide-to-osaka-nightlife-top-districts-bars-foods-and-experiences/_


### B24. Driving in Japan on an Indian licence: IDP rules, car rental, ETC tolls, and where self-drive beats trains (Hokkaido, Okinawa, Noto/Shirakawa-go, Fuji Five Lakes)
_This is the single biggest enabler for "beyond the Golden Route" travel, and it has a hard India-specific catch. Japan legally accepts ONLY a 1949 Geneva Convention International Driving Permit (IDP); your Indian licence alone is invalid, and the newer 1968 Vienna-Convention IDP and any digital/app "international licence" are refused at the rental counter and by police. India is a 1949 Geneva signatory, so an IDP obtained in India from your RTO (via the Parivahan/Sarathi portal) works — but you MUST get it before you fly (you cannot apply from Japan), it must be the physical grey booklet (not a card/paper/PDF), and it is valid one year from issue or one year from your arrival in Japan, whichever ends first. India is NOT on Japan's short "drive on a translated home licence" list (only Switzerland, Germany, France, Belgium, Monaco, Taiwan get that), so the IDP is non-negotiable. Good news for Indians: Japan drives on the LEFT with the wheel on the RIGHT — exactly like home, so no side-swap stress. Practical enablers: rent from Toyota / Nippon / Times / Orix (English sites and staff), always rent an ETC card (~¥330 / ~₹195) so expressway tolls (~¥25/km) auto-tally and you skip cash booths, and know that self-drive genuinely beats trains in Okinawa (no trains at all beyond Naha's monorail), Hokkaido (sparse rail, sights far apart — but real snow-driving risk in winter), Shirakawa-go/Noto (no rail to Shirakawa-go; buses are slow and indirect), and the Fuji Five Lakes (trains reach Kawaguchiko, but a car unlocks the other four lakes and viewpoints). Costs and figures below are real, with JPY and approximate INR at the July 2026 rate of about 1 yen = ₹0.59 (100 yen ≈ ₹59)._

- **The core rule: Japan takes ONLY a 1949 Geneva Convention IDP — Indian licence alone is illegal to drive on** — To drive in Japan an Indian visitor must carry a 1949 Geneva Convention International Driving Permit (IDP) PLUS the original Indian driving licence. An Indian national licence by itself is NOT valid in Japan. Critically, Japan recognises only the 1949 Geneva format — a 1968 Vienna Convention IDP or any digital/app 'international licence' will be rejected by rental desks and police. The IDP must be the small physical booklet (grey cover), not a card, plain paper, or PDF, with vehicle classifications A–E, and the holder must be 18+. India and Japan are both 1949 Geneva parties, which is exactly why an India-issued IDP is accepted.
    - 💴 No cost at the Japan end; the IDP itself is obtained in India (see next item).
    - 💡 At the rental counter you will hand over BOTH documents (IDP booklet + Indian licence + passport). Keep all three on you whenever you drive — a spot check without them is an offence.
    - 🇮🇳 This is the make-or-break item for Indians. Many Indians assume their DL or a random online 'international licence' works — it does not. Get the RTO-issued 1949 Geneva booklet before you fly; you cannot obtain a valid one after landing in Japan.
- **How to get the IDP in India before you fly: RTO / Parivahan-Sarathi, Form 4A, ~₹1,000–1,500, roughly a week** — Apply at your RTO (the one that issued your licence) either online via the Parivahan 'Sarathi' portal (Apply for International Driving Permit) or in person. Required: valid Indian driving licence, passport, valid Japan visa, passport-size photos, and Form 1A/4A (medical/application form). The IDP is valid 1 year from issue. It is issued only in your home country — overseas applications are not accepted — so this must be done in India before departure. Beware of unofficial websites selling fake 'international driving licences'; Japan will not accept them and only an RTO-issued IDP is genuine.
    - 💴 IDP fee typically ₹1,000–₹1,500 (varies slightly by state); no yen cost.
    - 💡 Carry the IDP and your Indian licence as physical originals in your hand luggage — no photocopies, no digital-only versions. Note the IDP's 1-year clock: buy it close to (not months before) your trip so its validity covers your whole stay.
    - 🇮🇳 Timing matters for the India→Japan trip: the Sarathi form asks for passport, visa and destination-country details, so apply only after your Japan visa is in hand. Allow at least a week (longer if the RTO wants an in-person slot). Do this alongside your visa paperwork, not at the last minute.
- **India is NOT on Japan's 'drive on a translated licence' shortlist — plus the validity clock and the 3-month rule** — Japan lets holders from only six places (Switzerland, Germany, France, Belgium, Monaco, Taiwan) drive on their home licence with an official JAF/embassy translation instead of an IDP. India is not among them, so for Indians the 1949 Geneva IDP is the only route. Your IDP lets you drive for up to one year from your date of entry into Japan (or the IDP's issue date, whichever ends first) — ample for any tourist trip. A separate '3-month rule' (you must have been outside Japan 3+ consecutive months for a fresh entry to restart the clock) applies only to registered mid/long-term residents, not to short-term tourists.
    - 💴 N/A — regulatory clarification.
    - 💡 If your trip is a normal 1–3 week holiday, the validity math never bites; just ensure the IDP is issued before you land.
    - 🇮🇳 Directly closes the 'can I just get my licence translated like some Europeans do?' question for Indian readers: no. The IDP is mandatory. The 3-month rule is only relevant to Indians who become residents (students/workers) — flag it only for that audience.
- **Left-side driving is a home advantage for Indians — but Japan's road rules are stricter** — Japan drives on the LEFT with the steering wheel on the RIGHT — identical to India, so no dangerous side-swap adjustment (a real edge over Indians driving in the US/Europe). But rules are enforced harder: alcohol is effectively zero-tolerance (legal blood-alcohol limit 0.03%, i.e. don't drink at all before driving) with penalties up to 5 years prison or a ¥1,000,000 (~₹5.9 lakh) fine; speeding fines run around ¥15,000 (~₹8,850). Speed limits are low: 30–40 km/h on residential/urban roads, 80–100 km/h on expressways (a few sections up to 120). Compact 'kei' cars (≤660cc, tiny, cheap, fuel-efficient) dominate rural Japan and are ideal for narrow lanes and tight parking.
    - 💴 Drink-drive penalty up to ¥1,000,000 (~₹5.9 lakh); typical speeding fine ~¥15,000 (~₹8,850).
    - 💡 Choose a kei car for Okinawa/rural loops (cheaper rental, tolls and fuel); choose a standard car for long Hokkaido expressway distances and comfort.
    - 🇮🇳 Reassure Indian readers: same side of the road as home, so the muscle memory transfers. Counter-warn on discipline — lane discipline, no honking, and absolute zero drink-driving are taken far more seriously than in India; even one beer at dinner means no driving.
- **Where to rent: Toyota, Nippon, Times, Orix, Nissan, Budget — all have English websites and staff** — Toyota Rent a Car is the largest (about 1,100 branches nationwide); Nippon Rent-A-Car, Times Car Rental, Orix, Nissan and Budget are the other majors. All have English-language websites and English-speaking call centres, and desks are common at airports and beside major train stations (e.g. right across from Kawaguchiko station). Rates typically include unlimited mileage and a mandatory basic insurance (~¥1,000/day, ~₹590). Prices vary sharply by season and vehicle: Okinawa runs about ¥5,000/day (~₹2,950) for a kei car and ¥6,500/day (~₹3,835) for a standard car in low season (Jan–Feb), rising to ¥7,000 / ¥8,500 (~₹4,130 / ~₹5,015) during Golden Week and Obon. A Sapporo/Hokkaido compact is roughly ¥6,000–12,000/day (~₹3,540–7,080) depending on season.
    - 💴 Okinawa kei ¥5,000/day (~₹2,950) low season; standard ¥6,500/day (~₹3,835) low, ¥8,500 (~₹5,015) peak. Basic insurance ~¥1,000/day (~₹590). Hokkaido compact ~¥6,000–12,000/day (~₹3,540–7,080).
    - 💡 Compare via Klook/Rakuten Travel/ToCoo (English aggregators) but confirm the IDP-required note; reserve early for Okinawa summer and Hokkaido winter/ski season when fleets sell out.
    - 🇮🇳 Book on the English site before you fly and upload/note your IDP; some desks ask to see the IDP + Indian licence at pickup, so have both ready. Add the extra 'CDW/NOC' insurance waiver (small daily fee) — repair liability in Japan is expensive and worth capping.
- **Always rent the ETC card (~¥330): expressway tolls auto-tally at ~¥25/km, and cash booths are disappearing** — ETC (Electronic Toll Collection) is Japan's automatic toll system: the in-car ETC reader is standard/free in most rental cars, and you rent a small ETC card for about ¥330 (Times charges ¥300+tax; Toyota and Nippon ¥330). You drive through purple 'ETC' lanes without stopping; every toll is recorded on the card and you settle the total when you return the car. Expressway tolls average ~¥25/km — e.g. Tokyo→Kawaguchiko ~¥3,000 (~₹1,770), Tokyo→Osaka ~¥12,000 (~₹7,080) one way. Manual/cash toll booths are being phased out (target ~March 2031) and ETC-only 'Smart IC' interchanges are multiplying, so the card is close to essential.
    - 💴 ETC card rental ~¥330 (~₹195) per rental; tolls ~¥25/km. Sample tolls: Tokyo–Kawaguchiko ~¥3,000 (~₹1,770); Tokyo–Osaka ~¥12,000 (~₹7,080).
    - 💡 Request the ETC card at booking (inventory can run out at pickup). Keep a little cash anyway for the shrinking number of cash booths and for cash-only parking (common at Kawaguchiko).
    - 🇮🇳 Indians can't easily buy a personal ETC card (needs a Japanese-issued card/account), so the rental-desk ETC card is the practical option — say 'ETC card' when booking. Without it you must queue at manned booths and pay cash/credit, and some ETC-only exits won't be usable at all.
- **Toll-saving passes for tourists: Hokkaido Expressway Pass is live and cheap; the Central & nationwide passes are gone** — For heavy expressway driving, foreign-tourist-only unlimited toll passes exist (passport required, bought at car pickup, non-extendable). The Hokkaido Expressway Pass (HEP) is the standout and still sold: ordinary car ¥7,700 for 4 days up to ¥15,400 for 8 days (kei/light: ¥6,200 to ¥12,300) — economical versus ~¥25/km. BUT two big caveats for 'beyond Golden Route' drivers: the nationwide Japan Expressway Pass (JEP) was discontinued in December 2023, and the Central Nippon Expressway Pass (CEP) — which used to cover Nagoya, Kanazawa, Takayama, Shirakawa-go, Hakone and the Mt Fuji area — closed to new bookings from 1 October 2024. So for Noto/Shirakawa-go/Fuji you now simply pay per-km via the ETC card; only Hokkaido (and regional passes like Tohoku, Kyushu, San'in-Setouchi-Shikoku) still have a tourist flat-rate option.
    - 💴 HEP: ordinary car ¥7,700/4 days (~₹4,540) to ¥15,400/8 days (~₹9,085); kei ¥6,200 (~₹3,660) to ¥12,300 (~₹7,255). CEP and nationwide JEP no longer available.
    - 💡 Buy HEP only if your planned toll spend exceeds the flat fee; for short hops within Hokkaido, pay-per-use ETC can be cheaper. The pass covers tolls only — car rental and the ETC card are separate charges.
    - 🇮🇳 For a Hokkaido road-trip an Indian tourist just shows the passport at pickup to buy HEP — worth it if you'll cover long distances (e.g. Sapporo–Furano–Obihiro–Hakodate). For central Japan (Shirakawa-go, Noto, Fuji), don't hunt for the CEP (it's closed) — budget per-km ETC tolls instead.
- **Okinawa: self-drive is essentially mandatory — there are no trains outside Naha's monorail** — Okinawa has NO railway network; public transport is limited to the Yui Rail monorail (Naha city only) and infrequent buses. To reach the island's real draws — Churaumi Aquarium, Cape Manzamo, northern beaches, Cape Hedo, remote cafés — a rental car is the only sensible option. Budget roughly ¥8,000/day (~₹4,720) all-in for the car and a couple of thousand yen for tolls (Okinawa has one short expressway), keeping daily driving costs under ~¥10,000 (~₹5,900). A kei car is plenty for the main island's roads and parking.
    - 💴 Realistic Okinawa daily budget: car + tolls under ¥10,000/day (~₹5,900); kei from ¥5,000/day (~₹2,950) low season.
    - 💡 Reserve well ahead for summer/Golden Week (fleets sell out and peak rates jump ~¥2,000/day). Pick up at Naha Airport where all majors have desks.
    - 🇮🇳 For Indian families this is the clearest 'you must drive' region — taxis to the north and back will cost far more than a rental, and buses eat the day. Since Okinawa drives on the left like India and roads are easy, it's a low-stress place for a first Japan self-drive.
- **Hokkaido: self-drive unlocks the island, but winter snow-driving is a genuine risk for Indians with no snow experience** — Hokkaido's rail network is sparse and its highlights (Furano lavender, Biei, Shiretoko, Lake Toya, farms and onsen towns) are spread far apart, so a car delivers flexibility trains can't. In winter (Dec–Mar) rental cars come with free studless snow tyres as standard — but the roads are genuinely dangerous for anyone who has never driven on snow and ice, which describes most Indians. Experienced-in-snow drivers gain huge freedom; the unsure should consider basing at a ski resort and using shuttles/public transport instead. Summer/autumn Hokkaido driving is easy and the strongest case for a road trip.
    - 💴 Hokkaido compact ~¥6,000–12,000/day (~₹3,540–7,080) by season; winter studless tyres included free. Pair with HEP (from ¥7,700/4 days) for long-distance touring.
    - 💡 If you do drive in winter, slow right down, leave big gaps, avoid mountain passes in storms, and never rely on chains alone — the studless tyres are the standard, correct setup and are included.
    - 🇮🇳 Blunt safety note for Indian readers: if you have never driven on snow/ice, Hokkaido in deep winter is not the place to learn — black ice and whiteouts are lethal even with studless tyres. Prefer late spring–autumn for a self-drive, or use resort shuttles in peak winter.
- **Shirakawa-go & the Noto Peninsula: driving is faster, cheaper, and sometimes the only realistic way** — Shirakawa-go (the UNESCO gassho-zukuri village) has NO train station — access is by bus only, and there is no direct bus onward to the Noto Peninsula. Driving Shirakawa-go→Noto takes about 2 hours and costs roughly ¥2,000–3,000 in tolls/fuel, versus ~5 hours and multiple transfers by bus+train (via Kanazawa). The Noto Peninsula's coastal sights are likewise thinly served by transit. A rental car turns a fragmented, transfer-heavy itinerary into a smooth loop through the Japan Alps and Hokuriku coast.
    - 💴 Shirakawa-go→Noto by car ~2 hrs, ~¥2,000–3,000 (~₹1,180–1,770) vs ~5 hrs and ¥3,000–7,000+ by bus/train with transfers. (Note: the old Central Nippon Expressway Pass that covered this area is closed to new bookings — pay ETC per-km.)
    - 💡 Pick up the car in Takayama, Kanazawa, or from a Shinkansen hub, and drive the Takayama→Shirakawa-go→Kanazawa→Noto arc; roads are good outside heavy winter snow.
    - 🇮🇳 For Indians building a 'beyond Golden Route' central-Japan leg (Takayama–Shirakawa-go–Kanazawa–Noto), a car is the single biggest time-saver; without it, Shirakawa-go and Noto force long bus waits that can cost you a whole day.
- **Fuji Five Lakes: trains reach Kawaguchiko, but a car unlocks the other four lakes and the best Fuji views** — You can reach Lake Kawaguchiko by train or bus from Tokyo — the Fuji Excursion limited express from Shinjuku (~2 hrs, ~¥4,000 / ~₹2,360) or a direct highway bus from Tokyo Station (~2 hrs, ~¥2,200 / ~₹1,300). But the wider Fuji Five Lakes region (Saiko, Shojiko, Motosuko, Yamanakako) and the panoramic viewpoints, Oshino Hakkai, and Aokigahara-edge spots are scattered and poorly linked by transit, so a rental car is the best way to circle them. Roads here are easy outside winter; parking near the lakes is often cash-only.
    - 💴 Tokyo→Kawaguchiko: Fuji Excursion train ~¥4,000 (~₹2,360) or highway bus ~¥2,200 (~₹1,300). Kawaguchiko-area rental from ~¥6,000–8,000/day (~₹3,540–4,720); Tokyo↔Kawaguchiko expressway toll ~¥3,000 (~₹1,770).
    - 💡 For the quickest self-drive access from a Shinkansen route, pick up a car at Mishima or Shin-Fuji. Avoid the region in heavy winter if inexperienced with snow; otherwise the roads are straightforward.
    - 🇮🇳 Practical hybrid for Indian visitors doing this as a side-trip: take the train/bus to Kawaguchiko, then rent a car locally (desks are opposite the station) for a day to tour all five lakes — cheaper and less stressful than driving out of Tokyo traffic. Carry cash for lakeside parking.

_Sources: https://kart.st/en/drivers-license/india.html · https://www2.tocoo.jp/en/howto/itn_lic · https://www.keishicho.metro.tokyo.lg.jp/multilingual/english/traffic_safety/drivers_licenses/index.files/kokusaimenkyo_english.pdf · https://english.jaf.or.jp/driving-in-japan/drive-in-japan/switch-to-japanese-license · https://www.police.pref.chiba.jp/english/International_license.html · https://wise.com/in/blog/international-driving-permit-india · https://www.sarathi-parivahan.com/2025/10/international-driving-india.html · https://www.timescar-rental.com/en/japan/etc.html · https://en.japantravel.com/article/driving-in-japan-etc-guide/71567 · https://www.japan-guide.com/e/e2354.html · https://www.japan.travel/en/guide/regional-expressway-passes/ · https://en.driveplaza.com/drawari/hokkaido_expass/_


### B25. Watching live sport in Japan: NPB pro baseball & J-League football (cheap, electric, family-friendly) — for an India→Japan traveler
_Japanese pro baseball (NPB) is the country's most-loved spectator sport and a genuine "only in Japan" experience the sumo-only research misses: 12 teams play a 143-game season from late March to October, tickets start around ¥2,000 (~₹1,180) — cheaper and far easier to get than sumo — and the oendan (organised chant-and-drum cheering), Lucky-7 jet-balloon release at Koshien, and uriko "beer girls" with kegs on their backs make it electric and family-friendly. J-League football adds a second option, but 2026 is a transition year: a special "100 Year Vision League" runs Feb–June 2026, then Japan switches to a European-style Aug 2026–May 2027 season. Prices verified for 2025/2026, converted at ~1 JPY = 0.59 INR (100 yen ≈ ₹59, July 2026). All figures are real; where a page blocked scraping I relied on search-result summaries and flag any uncertainty._

- **The gap itself: baseball is Japan's #1 spectator sport, cheaper & easier than sumo** — The 'only in Japan' angle usually stops at sumo, but Nippon Professional Baseball (NPB) draws far bigger crowds and is the everyday obsession. It's 12 clubs in two leagues — Central: Yomiuri (Tokyo) Giants, Hanshin Tigers, Tokyo Yakult Swallows, Yokohama DeNA BayStars, Chunichi Dragons, Hiroshima Toyo Carp; Pacific: Fukuoka SoftBank Hawks, Hokkaido Nippon-Ham Fighters, Tohoku Rakuten Eagles, Saitama Seibu Lions, Chiba Lotte Marines, Orix Buffaloes. Unlike sumo (only 3 Tokyo tournaments a year, Jan/May/Sep, that sell out), every NPB team plays ~71 home games a season, so there's almost always a game near you.
    - 💴 Cheapest NPB seat from ~¥2,000 (~₹1,180). Compare sumo at Ryogoku Kokugikan: same-day unreserved from ¥2,200, reserved chair seats ¥5,500–9,500, premium ringside masu boxes up to ¥14,800 (~₹8,730) and tamari ringside ~¥20,000 (~₹11,800). Baseball wins on both price and availability.
    - 💡 If you can only fit one live-sport night and want guaranteed tickets + party atmosphere, pick baseball over sumo.
    - 🇮🇳 For an Indian family doing one big Japan trip, baseball is the low-risk 'wow' outing: no ticket-lottery stress like sumo, English booking sites, and it fits a Tokyo or Osaka evening after daytime sightseeing.
- **2026 NPB season window (roughly late-March to end-October)** — The 2026 regular season opens Friday 27 March 2026 with all 12 teams playing, runs 143 games per club into early October, then the Climax Series playoffs (mid-October) lead to the Japan Series final (late October into early November). For reference, in 2025 the postseason opened 11 October and the Japan Series ran 25 Oct–early Nov. So if you visit Japan any time from late March through October you can catch a live NPB game.
    - 💴 No cost note — this is timing. Regular-season games are cheapest and easiest to get; playoff/Japan Series tickets are scarce and pricier.
    - 💡 Cherry-blossom trippers in early April get bonus baseball; October visitors can aim for the electric Climax Series/Japan Series if the timing lines up.
    - 🇮🇳 This overlaps the popular Indian travel windows: cherry-blossom season (late March–April) AND the autumn-foliage shoulder season (October) both fall inside the baseball season, so a game slots neatly into a typical India→Japan itinerary.
- **How a foreign tourist actually buys NPB tickets (no Japanese ID needed)** — The Yomiuri Giants run an official English booking site, e-tix (e-tix.jp/ticket_giants/en) — you buy with an overseas credit card (VISA/Mastercard/JCB/AMEX), get a QR e-ticket emailed to your phone, no Japanese address or ID required (a ¥220/~₹130 convenience fee per ticket applies; complete checkout within 15 min; no refunds except cancelled games). Other English routes: Ticket Pia's inbound portal, Lawson Ticket, and resellers Klook/Voyagin. You can also buy at Lawson/7-Eleven kiosks or the stadium box office on game day once you're in Japan. For the Hanshin Tigers, use the official Hanshin site or a Klook combo (game + Koshien History Museum); same-day tickets are collected at the advance booth by Gate 9 at Koshien — bring your passport as ID.
