    - 💴 Book within the first day or two of release (tickets drop in stages ~4–6 weeks before each game) for the best seats and prices. Convenience fee ¥220 (~₹130) per ticket on e-tix.
    - 💡 Popular games (Giants vs Hanshin, weekends, Osaka's Hanshin at Koshien) sell out fast — book before you land, not on the day.
    - 🇮🇳 Your Indian credit card works on e-tix/Klook; you don't need a Japanese phone number or My Number. Klook is already familiar to many Indian travellers and shows prices you can compare before you fly.
- **NPB ticket prices by seat (Tokyo Dome / Giants as the benchmark)** — For 2026 Giants games at Tokyo Dome, approximate seat pricing: outfield seats ¥2,000–3,500 (~₹1,180–2,065); upper-level/balcony ¥1,800–3,000; infield Reserved A/B ¥4,000–6,000 (~₹2,360–3,540); premium Reserved S / Orange seats ¥6,000–8,500 (~₹3,540–5,015). Prices flex by day of week, opponent and promo dates. Note: at Tokyo Dome the right-field section is reserved for Giants (home) supporters only — sit there if you want to be in the loudest cheering block, or pick the opposite side for the visiting team.
    - 💴 Sweet spot for first-timers: outfield/cheer-zone seats around ¥2,000–3,500 (~₹1,180–2,065) put you inside the drum-and-chant oendan section for the least money.
    - 💡 Outfield 'cheer' seats are the best value AND the most fun; the pricey infield seats are actually the quieter, calmer option.
    - 🇮🇳 A family of four in decent infield seats runs roughly ¥16,000–24,000 (~₹9,400–14,000) total — comparable to a night out, and cheaper than most bucket-list Japan experiences.
- **Iconic venues: Tokyo Dome and Hanshin Koshien (plus the standouts)** — Tokyo Dome ('Big Egg', ~46,000, home of the Giants) is a climate-controlled indoor dome, 5-min walk from Suidobashi Station (JR Chuo-Sobu line) inside the Tokyo Dome City amusement complex — easy for families, cashless-only inside. Hanshin Koshien Stadium (opened 1924, ~47,000, home of the Hanshin Tigers) in Nishinomiya between Osaka and Kobe is the spiritual home of Japanese baseball — open-air, ivy-covered, and the most passionate crowd in the country (a 'sea of yellow'). Other memorable ballparks: ES CON Field Hokkaido (a brand-new stadium with an onsen and craft-beer hall), Mazda Zoom-Zoom Stadium (Hiroshima Carp), Meiji Jingu Stadium (Tokyo, open-air, central), and ZOZO Marine (Chiba, seaside and breezy).
    - 💴 Koshien's game+Koshien History Museum Klook combo bundles in original team goods; a good-value keepsake package for tourists.
    - 💡 Rainy-season (June) trip? Choose a domed stadium (Tokyo Dome, Kyocera Dome Osaka, Fukuoka PayPay Dome) so rain can't wash out your one game.
    - 🇮🇳 Tokyo Dome is the most convenient for a first-timer based in Tokyo (indoor = no weather risk during the June rainy season); Osaka-based travellers should target Koshien on the Hanshin line for the wildest atmosphere.
- **The atmosphere: oendan chants, Lucky-7 jet balloons, and uriko beer girls** — This is why first-timers fall in love. Organised cheer squads (oendan) lead near-nonstop singing for the entire game — a custom trumpet-and-drum chant for every single batter, colour-coordinated towels, flags and umbrellas. At the '7th-inning stretch' (Lucky 7) each team sings its anthem; Hanshin fans famously release ~10,000 screaming 'jet balloons' into the sky at Koshien during their 1936 anthem 'Rokko Oroshi' (this iconic balloon release was revived in 2026). Selling beer in the aisles are the uriko — young women who sprint the stands with ~13 kg (30 lb) kegs strapped to their backs, pouring fresh draft to your seat with a smile. It's loud, joyous, alcohol-fuelled but non-aggressive, and completely safe.
    - 💴 No entry cost beyond your ticket — the oendan show is free and runs all nine innings.
    - 💡 Sit in the outfield home-supporters' section to be handed a cheer towel/megaphone and swept into the chants; buy a team happi coat or towel outside so you look the part.
    - 🇮🇳 For Indian fans used to boisterous cricket-stadium energy, NPB's coordinated chanting feels instantly familiar — but cleaner, friendlier and more choreographed; great for kids and grandparents alike.
- **Food & drink at the ballpark — cheap, tasty, and options for veg/halal** — Ballpark food is a highlight and cheap by global standards. A large draft beer is about ¥900 (~₹530) at Tokyo Dome and most Central-League parks (¥800–900 range league-wide; ~10 teams nudged prices up ¥50–100 in 2025 but Tokyo Dome held). A double-cheese hot dog ~¥1,000 (~₹590), soft drinks ¥400 (~₹235), plus kimchi noodles, garlic-pepper chicken, gyoza and dozens more. Bento boxes and team-themed dishes are everywhere.
    - 💴 Total for a beer + a hot dog + a soft drink ≈ ¥2,300 (~₹1,360) — a fraction of MLB stadium prices, which is exactly what surprised visiting reporters during the 2025 MLB Tokyo Series.
    - 💡 Inside Tokyo Dome is cashless — load a Suica/PASMO IC card or carry a tap card; you can't pay stadium vendors in cash.
    - 🇮🇳 Veg/halal is doable: Marugame Seimen at Tokyo Dome City serves vegetarian udon, tofu udon and veg tempura, and Tokyo Dome City has halal-certified dining across its 1st–3rd floors. Belluna Dome (Seibu Lions) even has a vegan stall, 'Backyard Butchers'. Options are limited vs a normal restaurant, so pure-veg/Jain travellers should eat a proper meal beforehand and treat the ballpark as snacks-and-beer.
- **J-League football — the second option, but note the 2026 calendar shake-up** — J-League is high quality and passionately supported (Urawa Red Diamonds pack the ~60,000-seat Saitama Stadium 2002, Japan's biggest football-only ground, with European-level tifos and chants; other big draws include FC Tokyo, Kawasaki Frontale and Gamba Osaka). BUT 2026 is a transition year as Japan switches from a spring–autumn to a European-style autumn–spring calendar: a special 'MEIJI YASUDA J.LEAGUE 100 Year Vision League' runs 6 Feb–23 May 2026 (regional groups) with playoffs 30 May–7 Jun, then the new 2026–27 J1 League runs 7 Aug 2026–6 Jun 2027 (winter break mid-Dec to mid-Feb). So a 2026 visitor finds football Feb–June, a gap in summer, then league football again from August. J1 matches are usually Saturday afternoons.
    - 💴 J-League tickets ¥2,000–8,000 for adults (~₹1,180–4,720) and ¥500–2,000 for children — plus dynamic pricing, so tickets are cheapest on the first day of sale and climb toward match day: buy early.
    - 💡 If you're in Japan July–early August, league football is between seasons — check the 100 Year Vision League fixtures (Feb–June) or the new season opener (from 7 Aug) rather than assuming a game is always on. Baseball fills the summer gap.
    - 🇮🇳 Buy in English at the official J.League site (jleague.co) or the J.League app; you register an ID and can collect at a convenience store, or buy over the counter/at the gate on match day. No Japanese ID needed.
- **India-specific logistics: getting there, visa, money, connectivity** — Getting there: Delhi–Tokyo (Haneda/Narita) is served daily by Air India, ANA and Japan Airlines; Air India launches a new nonstop Mumbai–Tokyo Haneda service from 15 June 2026 (4x/week on a Boeing 787-8, evening departure, next-morning arrival). Over 315,000 Indians visited Japan in 2025, a record. Visa: Indian passport-holders need a short-stay tourist visa arranged in advance (stays up to 90 days). Money: as of late July 2026, 1 JPY ≈ 0.59 INR (100 yen ≈ ₹59) — handy mental math for all the yen prices above.
    - 💴 Round-trip India–Japan fares have been seen from roughly US$400+ (~₹34,000+) on comparison sites, though summer/peak fares run higher — book early. A whole day at the baseball (mid-tier seat + food/beer) lands around ¥5,000–7,000 (~₹3,000–4,100) per person.
    - 💡 Line up your visa 3–4 weeks ahead, and if flying Air India's new Mumbai–Haneda nonstop (from June 2026) you land Haneda mid-morning — enough time to catch an evening Tokyo Dome game the same day.
    - 🇮🇳 Confirm current visa rules on the official Japan MOFA/embassy or VFS Global India portal before booking — Japan has been expanding an eVisa route for Indian tourists, but requirements change, so verify rather than rely on this note. Carry a Suica/PASMO IC card (or Welcome Suica) for cashless stadium spend and trains; many Indian Visa/Mastercards work but IC cards avoid per-vendor card issues.

_Sources: https://www.e-tix.jp/ticket_giants/en/ · https://japantravelplanning.com/tokyo-giants-baseball-tickets/ · https://japanball.com/japanese-baseball-news/npb-newsletter/2026-npb-season-begins-this-week/ · https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Nippon_Professional_Baseball_season · https://www.wbsc.org/en/news/hanshin-tigers-and-fukuoka-softbank-hawks-headline-2026-npb-season-as-teams-to-beat · https://x.com/WBSC/status/1905229670427037806 · https://pacificleague.com/en/news/2025/5/72436 · https://soranews24.com/2025/03/21/american-reporter-blown-away-by-cheap-food-prices-at-tokyo-baseball-stadium-locals-less-impressed/ · https://vinepair.com/wine-blog/at-japanese-baseball-games-beer-salesgirls-pour-brews-for-fans-from-kegs-strapped-to-their-backs/ · https://hyogojapan.com/baseball-game-in-japan/ · https://www.klook.com/en-US/activity/149782-hanshin-tigers-game-the-museum-of-hanshin-koshien-stadium-combo-ticket/ · https://ticketx.com/magazine/sports/When-Is-Japanese-Baseball-Season-Key-Dates-for-2026_

## Part C — Food: exploring Japanese cuisine (deep-dive)

# Trying Japanese Food & EXPLORING (Adventurous-Eater Edition)

**Currency note:** prices are as published (2025–2026). Rough conversions: **¥100 ≈ ₹57** (¥1 ≈ ₹0.57) and **US$1 ≈ ₹86 ≈ ¥150**. Rates float; treat INR as "approx." Japan is still heavily cash-friendly at small shops — carry a Suica/PASMO IC card and some ¥1,000 notes.

## 1. Immersive food experiences

**Guided food tours (fast-track to "exploring")**
- **Tsukiji Outer Market food & culture walking tour** — most reliable choice; fresh seafood + market history. Approx **US$95–115** (~₹8,200–9,900); cheaper on **Wednesdays** (wholesale market closed).
- **Shinjuku night food tour** (e.g. tokyo-food-tour.com) — 15 dishes across ~4 hidden eateries + 2 drinks for ~**US$74** (~₹6,350). Threads through **Omoide Yokocho** and **Golden Gai**.
- **Osaka Dotonbori** street-food crawls — takoyaki, okonomiyaki, kushikatsu along the canal.

**Grazing at Tsukiji Outer Market (self-guided):** thick **tamagoyaki** ¥300–500 (~₹170–285)/piece; fresh **oysters** and **uni (sea urchin)** ¥300–600 (~₹170–340)/piece. Go early morning.

**Omoide Yokocho ("Memory Lane" / "Piss Alley")** — two lantern-lit alleys of ~**80 tiny izakayas** by Shinjuku Station's west exit, against the JR tracks.
- **Yakitori (chicken skewers)** ¥100–200 (~₹57–115)/stick: *negima* (thigh+scallion), *tsukune* (meatball), *kawa* (skin), *reba* (liver).
- **Yakiton (pork offal)** ¥100–200/stick: *kashira* (cheek), *shiro* (intestine), *tan* (tongue w/ lemon).
- **Motsu nikomi** (offal simmered in miso) ¥400–500; chilled tomato ¥300; draft beer ¥500–600; lemon sour/sake ¥400–500.
- **Otoshi** (compulsory seat charge + small appetizer) ¥300–500. Realistic spend **¥2,000–3,000** (~₹1,140–1,710)/person. Mostly 4pm–midnight, **cash only**, counter seating, no reservations. Best 6–8pm; arrive 4–5:30pm to avoid waits.

**Izakaya hopping:** casual gastropubs of small plates. Expect an **otoshi** cover ¥200–1,000/person. Cross fingers into an "x" to ask for the bill at casual spots.

**Tachinomi (standing bars)** — chest-high counters, **no stools**, ~20–40 min per spot so you bar-hop. Drinks/snacks often just a few hundred yen each. Origin: *Toshimaya* sake shop in Kanda (Edo period). In a full **renaissance** (natural-wine, craft-beer, women-owned spots). Best hunting grounds: **Shinjuku Golden Gai**, **Yokohama's Noge**, **Osaka's Ura-Namba**, **Sapporo's Susukino**.

**Sake / Japanese-whisky / tea tastings**
- **Sake tasting in central Tokyo** with a sommelier — ~12 sakes + light bites.
- **Toshimaya Shuzo** (Tokyo's oldest brewery, est. **1596**) — 3-hr all-you-can-drink sake + fermented snacks.
- **Ishikawa Brewery** (est. 1863) — sake *Tamajiman* + craft beer, guided tours by reservation.
- **Japanese whisky tasting** at a **Ginza** bar — rare/aged bottles; also a "Luxury Tokyo Sake/Cocktail/Whisky pairing tour" in Shimbashi.
- **Matcha + wagashi**: tea ceremony (*sado*) + **nerikiri** sweet-making in Tokyo's Setagaya, or Kyoto (Maikoya). Kyoto/Uji matcha desserts at **Nakamura Tokichi** (~US$5–15).

## 2. Cooking classes & hands-on

Price bands: home-style ¥5,000–8,000; pro schools ¥8,000–15,000; **sushi/nigiri** ¥9,000–15,000 (2–4 hrs); **ramen** ¥8,000–12,000 (3–4 hrs); **wagashi** ¥6,000–10,000 (1.5–2 hrs).

- **YUCa's Japanese Cooking** (Tokyo) — home-style **Ramen & Gyoza**, ¥16,500 incl. VAT (~₹9,400).
- **Tsukiji Fish Market sushi-making** with a pro chef — from **US$68** (~₹5,850).
- **Roll & authentic sushi-making, Asakusa** — US$56–81.
- **Shibuya ramen class** (3 ramen styles) — US$93–186.
- **Osaka: Ramen & Gyoza in Dotonbori** (3 hrs) from US$68; plus **Takoyaki & Okonomiyaki** classes on the griddle.

## 3. Uniquely-Japanese dining formats to try

- **Kaiten (conveyor-belt) sushi:** plates circle on a belt; colour/pattern = price (¥100–500+). Chains (**Sushiro, Kura Sushi, Hama Sushi, Kappa Sushi**) use a **touch-screen** (English) with an express belt/train for your order. **Pay by counting stacked plates.** Self-serve green tea, ginger, soy. Etiquette: take only what you'll eat, **one plate at a time, never return a plate to the belt**.
- **Ticket-vending-machine ramen shops (食券機):** machine by the door — insert cash/card, press the button (photos/LED), it prints a ticket; hand to the chef and sit. Water self-serve. Order a *kaedama* (noodle refill) at tonkotsu shops.
- **Depachika (department-store basement food halls):** **Isetan Shinjuku B1** is the benchmark — 200+ stalls, wagyu, jewel-like bentos, patisserie, sake/wine/cheese. Free samples; **premium items discounted early evening**. Great picnic haul.
- **Themed cafes:** **Akihabara** is ground zero. **Maid cafes** (*@Home cafe*), **animal cafes** (cat/rabbit/hedgehog/reptile), **character/collab cafes** — **Kirby Cafe** (near Skytree/Asakusa), Pokémon Cafe, rotating anime tie-ins; **book ahead, they sell out**.
- **Grill-it-yourself & hot-pot:** **Yakiniku** (BBQ) — grill bite-size meat, then dip (beef tongue ¥2,300, loin ¥1,200, premium salted tongue ¥5,500). **Shabu-shabu** — swish paper-thin beef in dashi, dip in ponzu/sesame (AYCE premium wagyu ~¥8,500 / ~₹4,850). **Sukiyaki** — sweet soy-sugar braise dipped in raw egg. **Teppanyaki** — chef cooks on an iron griddle before you.
- **Tabehodai / Nomihodai:** set price for a set time (1–2 hrs) to order all-you-can-eat / all-you-can-drink — an izakaya staple.

## 4. Konbini (convenience-store) food adventure

- **7-Eleven egg sando (*tamago sando*)** ¥200–250 — the definitive konbini sandwich.
- **Onigiri (rice balls)** ¥110–190 — tuna-mayo, *umeboshi*, salmon (7-Eleven best overall).
- **FamilyMart FamiChiki** ¥180–250 — legendary hot fried chicken (FamilyMart wins hot fried food).
- **Karaage** ¥180–250; **melon pan** ¥120–190.
- **Lawson** wins desserts — *Uchi Café* pudding & cheesecake, matcha cups ¥200–300; **oden** (winter) ¥150–300.
- **7-Eleven purin (custard pudding)** & cream pancakes ¥120–200.
- **Seasonal limited editions:** sakura mochi (spring), citrus jellies (summer), roasted sweet potato (autumn), chocolate fondant (winter).
- **The game:** a "konbini blind taste-off" across all three — 7-Eleven (overall), Lawson (desserts), FamilyMart (hot fried).

## 5. Vending machine & drink culture

**~4–5 million machines** — ~one per 25 people, highest density on earth. Late Oct–mid Nov they flip to "warm mode": **red label = hot (~55°C), blue = cold**. A hot can doubles as a hand-warmer.
- **Hot canned coffee:** **Boss, Georgia, Wonda** — black, café au lait, sweet milk.
- **Hot oddities:** corn soup, cocoa, **amazake** (sweet fermented sake-kasu).
- **Green tea:** unsweetened *sencha*, roasted *hojicha*, matcha blends.
- **Cold curiosities:** **Ramune** (marble soda), frozen Coke slush, yuzu/melon soda, peach tea; regional flavours.
- **Novelty machines:** oden-in-a-can, pancakes-in-a-can, some ramen machines.
- Most drinks **¥100–160** (~₹57–91).

## 6. Regional dish-hunting — a "food map"

| City / Region | Signature dish(es) | What it is | Named spots / price |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Tokyo** | Edomae sushi; shoyu ramen; **monjayaki**; tonkatsu; tempura | Hand-pressed nigiri; runny savory pancake (Tsukishima's *Monja St*) | Sushi Saito, Sukiyabashi Jiro ($30–200+); Maisen tonkatsu ($12–25); Tempura Kondo |
| **Osaka** ("Japan's kitchen") | **Takoyaki**, **okonomiyaki**, **kushikatsu**, negiyaki, butaman | Octopus batter balls; savory pancake; deep-fried skewers | Aizuya & Wanaka (takoyaki $5–8); Mizuno/Chibo/Fukutaro (okonomiyaki $10–18); Daruma & Yaekatsu (kushikatsu ¥100–200/skewer, **no double-dipping**); 551 Horai (butaman) |
| **Kyoto** | **Kaiseki**, **yudofu** (tofu hot pot), matcha sweets, soba | Haute cuisine, 12–20 seasonal courses | Kikunoi (kaiseki $150–500+); Nakamura Tokichi (matcha); Honke Owariya (soba, 500+ yrs) |
| **Fukuoka / Hakata** | **Tonkotsu ramen**, **yatai**, **motsunabe**, mentaiko | Milky pork-bone broth, thin noodles, *kaedama* refills | Ippudo ($8–15); ~150 street stalls |
| **Hokkaido / Sapporo** | **Miso ramen**, **soup curry**, **Genghis Khan** (lamb), uni & crab | Rich fermented-soybean broth; Hokkaido seafood | Otaru seafood markets; **Uni Murakami** uni-don; ramen lunch ~¥950 vs dinner ~¥2,500 |
| **Nagoya** | **Hitsumabushi**, **misokatsu**, **tebasaki**, kishimen | Grilled eel 3 ways; miso-glazed pork; double-fried wings; flat noodles | Yabaton (misokatsu) |
| **Hiroshima** | **Okonomiyaki (Hiroshima style)** | **Layered** (not mixed) with fried noodles | — |
| **Kobe** | **Kobe/wagyu beef** | Intensely marbled beef | Steakhouse Satou ($80–400) |
| **Sendai (Tohoku)** | **Gyutan** | Grilled beef tongue | — |
| **Kumamoto (Kyushu)** | **Basashi** | Raw horse sashimi (40% of Japan's supply) | see Bucket List |
| **Kagawa (Shikoku)** | **Sanuki udon** | Chewy thick udon | — |
| **Okinawa** | **Goya champuru**, taco rice | Bitter-melon/tofu/pork stir-fry | — |

## 7. Street food, festival (matsuri) yatai & seasonal timing

**Fukuoka yatai** — Japan's last great open-air stall culture: **~150 stalls** on **Nakasu island**, around **Tenjin**, and **Nagahama**; Hakata ramen, yakitori, oden, mentaiko; evenings into the small hours.
**Osaka** — **Dotonbori Yatai Mura** food hall + Shinsekai's kushikatsu under Tsutenkaku Tower.
**Matsuri stalls** — takoyaki, yakisoba, ikayaki (grilled squid), taiyaki, choco-bananas, kakigori. Big ones: **Gion Matsuri** (Kyoto, all July), **Tenjin Matsuri** (Osaka, **Jul 24–25**), **Awa Odori** (Tokushima, **Aug 12–15**).

**Seasonal foods to time a trip around:**
- **Spring:** sakura mochi, hanami bento, sakura shrimp, chirashizushi, bamboo shoots.
- **Summer:** **unagi** (peak *Doyo no Ushi no Hi*, late July), somen, kakigori, hiyashi chuka.
- **Autumn:** **matsutake**, sanma (Pacific saury), chestnuts, roasted sweet potato.
- **Winter:** **crab** (Nov–Mar; Hokkaido & Sea of Japan), **fugu**, nabe hot pots, oden, ozoni.

## 8. Foodie neighborhoods to wander & graze

- **Tokyo:** **Shinjuku** (Omoide Yokocho + **Golden Gai**, 200+ micro-bars), **Ebisu** (quiet yokocho), **Nakameguro** (river + wine bars), **Tsukiji Outer Market**, **Ameyoko** (Ueno).
- **Osaka:** **Dotonbori**, **Shinsekai**, **Kuromon Ichiba Market** (580 m, 150+ stalls — go **before 11am**), **Tenma / Tenjinbashisuji** (Japan's longest arcade, 2.6 km, thick with standing bars).
- **Kyoto:** **Nishiki Market** ("Kyoto's kitchen"), **Pontocho** alley, **Gion**.

## 9. Budget-friendly gourmet (lunch is the hack)

- **Gyudon (beef bowl) chains:** **Yoshinoya** ~¥450; **Sukiya** from ~¥330 (Japan's largest chain); **Matsuya** *gyumeshi* ¥380–430 **with free miso soup**. Under ¥500 (~₹285).
- **Teishoku set meals** ¥1,000–1,500 (main + rice + miso + pickles).
- **Standing soba** (*tachigui soba*) at stations — a few hundred yen, minutes.
- **Lunch value hack:** high-end **sushi & kaiseki cost a fraction at lunch** vs dinner (e.g. ¥950 lunch vs ¥2,500 dinner). ¥1,000 buys a genuinely good meal almost anywhere.

## 10. How to be adventurous, practically

- **Ticket machines:** machine at door → insert cash/card → press button (use photos/LED) → add toppings → print ticket → hand to staff → water self-serve.
- **Plastic food models (*sampuru*)** in windows: point to what you want.
- **Useful phrases:** *sumimasen* (excuse me), *kore o kudasai* (this one, please — pointing), *omizu o kudasai* (water, please), *o-kaikei onegai shimasu* (the bill), *oishii!* (delicious), *itadakimasu* (before eating), *gochisōsama deshita* (after).
- **Etiquette that frees you up:** **slurping noodles is good manners**; **no tipping** anywhere; use the **oshibori** towel for hands only; **otoshi** seat-charge is normal; pay cash/IC card at small shops.
- **Kaiten rule:** one plate at a time, never return a plate to the belt.

## 11. Brave-eater bucket list

- **Uni (sea urchin):** creamy, sweet-briny; nigiri or **kaisendon**. Pilgrimage: **Uni Murakami** in Hokkaido.
- **Natto (fermented soybeans):** sticky, pungent; a Japanese breakfast rite — over rice with soy + mustard + scallion.
- **Fugu (pufferfish):** prepared only by **licensed chefs**, so it's safe; a **winter** delicacy and **Osaka** specialty. **Guenpin** (#1 fugu chain, 130+ licensed chefs), **Zuboraya** (sashimi from ¥1,000, set to ¥8,400), 1-Michelin **Yoshikou** (Osaka), **Torafugu no Kai**. **Shimonoseki** (Yamaguchi) is the fugu capital. Lunch ¥6,000–10,000; premium dinner ¥10,000–80,000.
- **Basashi (raw horse sashimi):** **Kumamoto** is home (40% of supply); *toro*, lean, crunchy *futaego*; with grated garlic/ginger + soy.
- **Shirako (cod/fugu milt):** creamy winter delicacy — poached with ponzu, tempura, or grilled on sushi.
- **Motsunabe / horumon:** offal hot-pot / grilled innards — a **Fukuoka** specialty.
- **Further edge:** *shiokara* (fermented squid guts), *hoya* (sea pineapple), *odori-ebi* (live shrimp), *shirasu* (baby whitebait), *inago* (candied grasshoppers, Nagano).

**Iconic "start here" dishes** (safe on-ramps, still authentic): sushi, ramen, tempura, **tonkatsu**, **katsu curry**, **gyoza**, yakitori, unagi, soba/udon, sukiyaki, okonomiyaki, takoyaki, tamagoyaki, and any **donburi** (gyudon/katsudon/oyakodon/kaisendon).

### Sources (food)
tomogo-travel.com · tokyo-food-tour.com · selfguidejapan.com · japan.travel · byfood.com · japantruly.com · japan-atlas.com · yjc.tokyo · foodtoursjapan.com · japambience.com · kanko-map.com · toptenjp.com · arigatojapan.co.jp · japan-guide.com · gofarther.blog · jocjapantravel.com · japannook.com · savorjapan.com · japanichibantours.com · gogonihon.com · eatinjapan.jp · livejapan.com · magical-trip.com · rethinktokyo.com · jasumo.com · japantravelplanning.com · japanhandbook.com · bokksu.com · voyapon.com · tokyotreat.com

## Part D — October in Japan (deep-dive)

# October in Japan — Research Notes (October 2026)

**Currency reference:** ~1 JPY = ₹0.57–0.59 (mid-2026); round figure ≈ **¥100 = ₹58**. INR figures approximate.

**The one big caveat that shapes everything:** In 2025 a very hot summer pushed foliage roughly **10 days later than average nationwide**, and 2026 is again forecast "around the usual time or slightly later." So for October 2026, treat lowland/city color as **"likely not yet turned"** and check live koyo reports (japan-guide, JMA) ~2 weeks before travel.

## 1. Weather — by region and by early / mid / late October

October is Japan's drying, cooling sweet spot: the rainy/typhoon front recedes, humidity eases, skies clear. But it spans a real range — warm (even muggy) in the first week down south, to frost at altitude by month-end.

| Region / City | Early Oct (day / night) | Late Oct (day / night) | Rain & notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Tokyo** | ~23–28°C / ~17°C | ~19–20°C / ~10–13°C | More rainy days early; dries out late Oct |
| **Kyoto** | ~24°C / ~17°C | ~19°C / ~11°C | Muggy early, crisp & clear late; basin traps heat midday |
| **Osaka** | ~28°C / ~23°C | ~23°C / ~16–20°C | ~111 mm total, ~5 rainy days; warm start |
| **Hiroshima** | 25–30°C / ~20°C | 20–25°C / ~15°C | ~110 mm; leaves only *begin* turning very late Oct |
| **Sapporo / Hokkaido** | ~16–17°C / ~9°C | single digits / ~5°C (mtns near freezing) | First snow dusts high peaks; autumn-into-winter |
| **Japanese Alps (Kamikochi, Murodo)** | cool days / single-digit nights | can drop **below 0°C**; snow on peaks late Oct | Murodo (2,450 m) already wintry; Kamikochi road closes ~Nov 15 |
| **Mt Fuji / Fuji Five Lakes** | cool, clear | crisp | **Fuji typically gets its first snow cap ~early October**. Climbing season CLOSED (ends early–mid Sept) → October = viewing only |

**Typhoon risk (Sept–Oct tail):** average **3.4 typhoons form in October**; **2–3 approach Honshu/Hokkaido/Kyushu/Shikoku**. South/west (Okinawa, Kagoshima, Kyushu, Shikoku) hardest hit; Tokyo/Kyoto/Osaka can get strong wind/rain; **Hokkaido least affected**. A typhoon can cancel flights, halt Shinkansen/local trains, shut attractions for a day.
- **Plan around it:** keep **buffer days**; book **refundable/flexible** flights & hotels; monitor JMA; front-load must-do outdoor items early; keep an indoor rain-day plan (museums, food, depachika); risk drops in the **second half of October**.

## 2. Autumn foliage (koyo) — the October timing map

**October is prime koyo — but only in the north and up high.** Colour sweeps north→south and high→low. The classic Kyoto/Tokyo red maples are a **mid-to-late NOVEMBER** phenomenon and will still be **green** in October.

### PEAK *IN* October (chase these)
- **Hokkaido – Daisetsuzan NP** (earliest in Japan): first reds ~**Sept 15**; **Asahidake & Kurodake peak late Sept**; **Sounkyo Onsen ~early Oct; Jozankei (near Sapporo) ~mid-Oct.**
- **Japanese Alps – Tateyama Kurobe Alpine Route:** **Murodo & Daikanbo peak ~late Sept–early Oct** (Murodo peaked Oct 2, 2025); **Midagahara/Bijodaira mid-Oct**; lower **Kurobe Dam / Shomyo Falls / Unazuki Onsen late Oct–early Nov.** Best overall: **mid-October.**
- **Japanese Alps – Kamikochi:** broadleaf maples **mid–late Oct**; **golden larch (karamatsu) ~Oct 25–Nov 5**. Rare "**leaves + first snow on peaks**" contrast late Oct. Road closes ~Nov 15.
- **Tohoku** (Oirase Gorge, Lake Towada, Hachimantai, Naruko Gorge): mountains **early–mid Oct**, gorges **late Oct**. **Zao Onsen ~late Oct.**
- **Nikko** (day/overnight from Tokyo): high-elevation **Lake Chuzenji, Kegon Falls, Irohazaka peak mid–late Oct**; town-level late Oct–mid Nov.
- **Norikura / higher Nagano:** early–mid Oct.

### NOT yet turned in October (still GREEN — peak November)
- **Kyoto** temples/gardens: **mid–late Nov** (central Kyoto late Nov–early Dec).
- **Tokyo** (Meiji Jingu Gaien ginkgo, Rikugien, Shinjuku Gyoen, Koishikawa Korakuen): **late Nov–early Dec.**
- **Osaka:** late Nov–early Dec. **Nara:** late Nov.
- **Mt Fuji / Kawaguchiko:** **mid-Nov** (Kawaguchiko Momiji Festival Nov 1–30).
- **Hakone:** mid–late Nov. **Hiroshima / Miyajima Momijidani:** mid–late Nov.

**How to chase koyo in October:** go **north/high early, south/low later** — or add a dedicated alpine/northern leg. For guaranteed October colour target **Hokkaido (first half)**, **Tateyama/Kamikochi (mid–late Oct)**, or **Nikko (late Oct)** — and enjoy Kyoto/Tokyo/Osaka for temples, food & festivals rather than leaves.

## 3. Festivals & events — with October dates

- **Nagasaki Kunchi — Oct 7–9** (fixed): 400+ yr-old festival; Chinese dragon dances + Dutch-influenced performances at Suwa Shrine, Kyushu.
- **Takayama Autumn (Hachiman) Festival — Oct 9–10** (fixed): one of Japan's three great float festivals; 11 ornate 1600s *yatai*, night lantern processions, *karakuri* marionettes. **Overlaps Sports Day weekend — book Takayama lodging months ahead.**
- **Kawagoe Festival — Oct 17–18, 2026** (3rd Sat/Sun): huge float procession through the Edo-era Kurazukuri district; easy Tokyo day trip.
- **Jidai Matsuri ("Festival of the Ages"), Kyoto — Oct 22**: 2,000+ costumed participants spanning 1,000+ yrs, Imperial Palace → Heian Shrine.
- **Kurama Fire Festival, Kyoto — Oct 22 (evening)**: giant flaming torches & bonfires in Kurama (north Kyoto). Dramatic Oct-22 "double bill" with Jidai Matsuri.
- **Nihonbashi-Kyobashi Matsuri, Tokyo — late Oct**: traditional float/parade street festival.
- **Tokyo International Film Festival — late Oct–early Nov.**
- **Halloween — Oct 31:** **Shibuya has effectively banned street gatherings** (public-drinking ban 6pm–5am around Shibuya Station/Scramble/Center Gai/Miyashita Park; alcohol sales restricted; Hachiko cordoned). Fun shifted to **theme parks (Disney, USJ), malls, ticketed events** — families should avoid central Shibuya on the 31st.
- **Autumn illuminations begin:** **Nabana no Sato (near Nagoya) opens ~Oct 18** — one of Japan's largest.
- **Sumo:** **No Grand Tournament in October**; instead the **Autumn Regional Tour (jungyo) ~Oct 6–25, 2026** brings top wrestlers to smaller towns — a rarer, intimate way to see sumo.

**Public holiday — Sports Day: Monday Oct 12, 2026** (2nd Monday). Creates a **3-day domestic long weekend (Oct 10–12)** coinciding with the **Takayama Festival (Oct 9–10)**. Expect surges on Shinkansen, foliage hotspots, festival towns, and higher hotel rates. Book early or avoid intercity travel Oct 10–12.

## 4. Seasonal autumn foods available in October

October is *shokuyoku no aki* — "autumn, the season of appetite."
- **Matsutake mushroom** (Sept–Oct peak, luxury star): pine aroma; *dobin-mushi*, grilled, or *matsutake gohan*. **¥3,000–10,000+ (₹1,700–5,800+)**; best at kaiseki (Kyoto, Nagano).
- **Chestnut (kuri):** *kuri gohan*, Mont Blanc cakes, roadside **roasted chestnuts**, wagashi.
- **Sweet potato (satsumaimo):** roasted **yaki-imo**, *daigaku imo*, purple-sweet-potato desserts (peaks with Halloween).
- **Persimmon (kaki):** fresh orange fruit and dried **hoshigaki**.
- **Sanma (Pacific saury):** *the* autumn fish — salt-grilled with grated daikon; *teishoku* **~¥800–1,500 (₹460–870)**. (Catches down → pricier/scarcer.)
- **New-crop rice (shinmai):** the year's first harvest; "新米" advertised.
- **Momiji manju** (Hiroshima/Miyajima maple-leaf cakes) and fried **age-momiji**.
- **Autumn fruit:** Shine Muscat grapes, *nashi* (Japanese pear) early Oct; Aomori apples from October.
- **Limited treats & Kit Kats:** **Kit Kat Premium Sweet Potato**, **Mont Blanc/chestnut**, sake Kit Kats; Halloween pumpkin & purple-sweet-potato items; Starbucks autumn/Halloween Frappuccinos. In konbini, Don Quijote, depachika.
- **New sake — Hiyaoroshi / Akiagari:** autumn-released sake (brewed winter, matured over summer); mellow, rich, pairs with fatty autumn fish & mushrooms.

## 5. Crowds & prices in October

October **straddles shoulder and high season**: early Oct calm/near-average; second half builds fast as foliage marches south.
- **Early Oct:** true shoulder-season value — best window of the month.
- **Late Oct:** noticeably busier; a standard room may run **~US$20–30 (₹1,700–2,600)/night more** than baseline — but still cheaper than November's peak.
- **Kyoto** foliage premium (30–60%) mostly hits **mid-Nov**, so **October Kyoto is comparatively affordable.** Tokyo/Osaka autumn premiums ~15–25%.
- **Foliage-corridor lodging** (Tateyama, Kamikochi, Nikko, Hokkaido onsen, Takayama) sells out **12–16 weeks ahead** — book by **June–July** for a mid/late-Oct trip.
- **Sports Day weekend (Oct 10–12):** domestic surge; festival towns crowded & pricier.

**Accommodation bands (per night; ¥100=₹58):**
- Business/budget hotel: **¥8,000–15,000 (₹4,600–8,700)**
- Mid-range hotel: **¥15,000–30,000 (₹8,700–17,400)**
- Mid ryokan (2 meals, per person): **¥30,000–50,000 (₹17,400–29,000)**
- Luxury ryokan w/ private onsen: **¥60,000–120,000+ (₹34,800–70,000+)**
- **JR Pass** (7-day, ordinary) ~**¥50,000 (₹29,000)** post-2023 hike — often *not* worth it vs point-to-point or regional passes; check route by route.

## 6. What to pack for October

**Layering is the whole game** — warm/muggy midday early, chilly nights, near-freezing at altitude by month-end.
- **Base + mid layers:** t-shirts + long-sleeves, a light sweater/cardigan.
- **Outer layer:** mid-weight jacket or light **trench/windbreaker** for evenings.
- **Rain/typhoon gear:** **packable rain jacket** + **compact umbrella** (front persists early Oct).
- **Scarf/stole** for temperature swings.
- **Comfortable walking shoes.**
- **Warm layer / light down + beanie & gloves** for **Hokkaido, Tateyama, Kamikochi, high peaks**, or early mornings late in the month (can hit 0°C).
- Sun protection still useful early Oct.

## 7. Itinerary tweaks for October

The **Golden Route (Tokyo → Hakone/Fuji → Kyoto → Osaka)** is fantastic in October — but its foliage-famous stops are **still green**. Reposition the cities as temples/food/festivals, and **bolt on a northern or alpine leg for guaranteed colour.**

**Superb on the Golden Route in October (leaves or not):**
- **Kyoto/Nara:** temples/gardens lush green, clear autumn light, **far fewer crowds than November** — plus **Jidai Matsuri + Kurama Fire Festival (Oct 22)**.
- **Osaka:** Dotonbori + autumn-food scene at its best (sanma, matsutake, new sake).
- **Tokyo:** neighborhoods, Nihonbashi-Kyobashi Matsuri, film festival, konbini/depachika autumn treats.
- **Hiroshima/Miyajima:** iconic torii year-round; leaves just starting very late Oct.
- **Fuji Five Lakes:** Fuji likely **snow-capped from early October** — striking even before maples turn.

**Koyo add-ons (pick by dates):**
- **Option A — Japanese Alps detour (mid–late Oct, best all-rounder):** Tokyo → Nagano → **Tateyama Kurobe Alpine Route (mid-Oct peak)** and/or **Kamikochi (mid–late Oct, golden larch)** → **Takayama (Autumn Festival Oct 9–10)** → rejoin Kyoto/Osaka.
- **Option B — Nikko (late Oct, lowest effort):** easy day/overnight from Tokyo; **Lake Chuzenji, Kegon Falls, Irohazaka peak mid–late Oct.**
- **Option C — Hokkaido (first half of Oct):** fly Sapporo → **Sounkyo/Daisetsuzan, Jozankei, Lake Akan** — peak late Sept–mid Oct; lowest typhoon risk.
- **Option D — Tohoku (mid–late Oct):** **Oirase Gorge, Lake Towada, Hachimantai** — dramatic, uncrowded colour.

**Sequencing tip:** colour moves **north→south, high→low** — structure **north/high first, cities/south later**.

## 8. India-specific — October fares & the Diwali/Dussehra factor

**Round-trip fares India → Japan, October 2026 (approx):**
- **Direct** (Delhi/Mumbai/Bengaluru → Tokyo Narita/Haneda; Air India, ANA, JAL; ~7–8 hrs): **₹48,000–55,000**.
- **One-stop** (via Singapore/Bangkok/Hong Kong; ~10–12 hrs): commonly **₹30,000–45,000**, sale fares toward **₹35,000**, occasional deep deals **₹15,000–30,000**.

**Is it peak? Two different clocks:**
- **Japan side:** October is *not* the priciest inbound month (spring & peak-Nov foliage dearer). Early–mid Oct is good value.
- **India side:** Oct–Nov is the **Indian festival travel crunch**, which drives up outbound fares/demand:
  - **Dussehra — Tuesday, Oct 20, 2026**
  - **Diwali — Sunday, Nov 8, 2026**
  - Peak Indian outbound demand **late Oct → mid-Nov** (school holidays around Diwali). Fares can jump **50–150% in the final two weeks**, climbing ~30 days out.

**Booking advice:**
- **Book 60–90 days ahead** — by ~**mid-August** for a late-Oct trip; earlier across **Dussehra (~Oct 17–20) or Diwali** windows.
- **Early October is the sweet spot:** genuine foliage (Hokkaido/Alps), lighter Japan crowds/prices, **and before the Indian festival fare surge** — dodges both peaks.
- Travelling over **Dussehra long weekend / Diwali week** = higher India-side fares *and* the Japan-side **Sports Day (Oct 10–12)** crowd — pre-book intercity transport/lodging.
- Given the **typhoon tail**, prefer **refundable/flexible** tickets and a buffer day around tight connections.

**Publish-time re-checks:** live koyo peak dates ~2 weeks out; exact Jidai Matsuri date; current JPY/INR; live fares. Everything else (festival dates, Sports Day, weather norms, typhoon pattern, food seasonality) is stable for October 2026.
